#5916. What Jensen Huang’s Fall Means for U.S.-China Relations

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

China Brief: What Jensen Huang’s Fall Means for U.S.-China Relations

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s China Brief. Today’s special edition comes in the wake of Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO of Nvidia over multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s China Brief. Today’s special edition comes in the wake of Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO of Nvidia over multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

In another world, recently resigned Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s infidelities would be a private embarrassment. In this one, however, a powerful man’s poor judgment could steer the U.S.-China technology relationship onto the rocks.

Huang admitted to marital infidelity; he reportedly engaged in affairs with at least three women, including two Nvidia employees. His relationship with Alice Huang, a businesswoman and alleged Chinese asset, has drawn the most scrutiny. But why is the relationship, and his resignation on Thursday, so potentially explosive?

Broadly speaking, U.S. policy toward China has long been divided into two camps. The caution camp, exemplified by national security hawks, worries about China’s record of intellectual property theft, its control of critical supply chains, its economic might, and its potential to leapfrog the United States in key technologies such as artificial intelligence.

The opportunity camp focuses instead on what the relationship with China could offer: selling technology, tapping vast Chinese markets, and finding ways for cooperation and mutual investment. The tension between the two can produce good policy, especially when each side argues smartly against the other.

But this healthy debate depends on good faith, on both sides believing that they share a basic set of U.S. national interests. It depends on caution not curdling into racist paranoia and on opportunity not turning into personal greed.

The Nvidia debate is particularly intense because there is no hotter resource in the U.S.-China relationship right now than compute: raw processing power, which depends on advanced chips.

Unlike most other domains of U.S.-China competition, such as supply chains or critical minerals, this is the rare case where the United States holds the upper hand: roughly 75 percent of global compute, compared with China’s 15 percent. That edge rests on the U.S. alliance with Taiwan, home to chipmaker TSMC, as well as on companies, such as Nvidia, that design and sell these chips.

For years, the argument over how to manage China’s access to advanced chips swayed between caution and opportunity.

The opportunists argued that Chinese dependence was a good thing. If China could buy everything but the most advanced chips, it would remain reliant on U.S. firms; if China were cut off, it would build its own industry. But the cautious approach—that Beijing should be kept from buying the most advanced chips, thereby locking in the U.S. advantage—generally prevailed.

The deal that Nvidia struck with U.S. President Donald Trump last year blew a hole in all of those arguments and in the assumption of good faith.

Nvidia was allowed to sell the extremely advanced H200 chip to China not because it won a reasoned debate but because the White House was offered a cut: 25 percent of the sales revenue, with no apparent oversight of the money. Huang via Nvidia also donated $26 million to pro-Trump causes, including construction of the White House ballroom.

The deal was rotten from the start, but national security hawks, fearing retaliation from Trump, were quieter than they might have been otherwise. Now the situation is reversed. An incoming Democratic Congress, backed by those Republicans who still care about national security, is poised to scrutinize these deals relentlessly.

The spy allegations against Alice Huang have supercharged that scrutiny, as has the White House’s apparent disregard of earlier warnings about her. If it emerges that Trump profited by shorting Nvidia stock after a second warning, the case becomes far worse. Even if Alice Huang never influenced Jensen Huang’s decisions, it will be easy to portray him as swayed by Beijing.

Huang’s downfall lets opponents of chip sales depict companies such as Nvidia as tools of the Chinese state and the White House as their willing accomplice. Trump’s doubling down on support for Huang only hands them more ammunition. With Republicans reeling from the U.S. midterm elections, companies can no longer afford to bet solely on Trump’s protection.

In the long run, the scandal might expose a wider circle of corruption and sanctions evasion surrounding the $4 trillion company.

Nvidia’s most advanced family of chips, Blackwell, is still banned from export to China, yet signs of its usage are all over Chinese AI, and Chinese firms are working hard to acquire it. Some U.S. national security officials appear to have been waiting for the right moment to leak incriminating documents.

Alice Huang’s firm, Megaspeed, somehow jumped from $33 million in assets in 2023 to $3 billion in 2024, which suggests possible Chinese state investment and use as a vehicle for sanctions evasion. (Megaspeed denies any wrongdoing and has promised full cooperation with U.S. criminal investigations.)

The United States had already started prosecuting companies believed to be evading sanctions, but a full federal investigation into both Jensen Huang’s and Alice Huang’s activities could turn over many more stones.

China, as usual, denies any involvement in espionage and urges the United States not to impose new tech restrictions. But those restrictions, and much tighter scrutiny of deals involving chips or China, now look inevitable. Hopefully, Beijing will accept that as part of the game and not retaliate by tightening its own already extensive anti-sanctions regulations or cutting off U.S. supplies of critical minerals.

On the U.S. side, there is also a risk of overindexing on a single case—a recurrent habit of intelligence services. The recruitment of one young American by the Chinese intelligence services in the early 2000s, for instance, made the security clearance process far harder for every U.S. citizen who had worked or studied in China.

At worst, that overindexing could mean racial bias against anyone of Chinese descent working in sensitive technology in the United States. At the very least, it should make any U.S. businessperson more cautious about dealing with their Chinese counterparts, let alone sleeping with them—and perhaps that is for the better.

James Palmer is a deputy editor at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @beijingpalmer.bsky.social

#5913. America Can Do Good—and Do Well

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW NATSIOS was Administrator of the U.S. Agency for International Development and the President’s Special Envoy to Sudan during the George W. Bush administration. He is the author of Guns Are Not Enough: Foreign Aid in the National Interest, from which this essay is adapted.

Among all the ruinous consequences of the so-called U.S. Department of Government Efficiency’s efforts to cut bureaucracy, none has been as profound as the destruction of the United States’ ability to deliver foreign aid. A comprehensive Lancet study shows that the U.S. Agency for International Development’s assistance prevented more than 90 million deaths between 2001 and 2021. Using a mortality model, Boston University epidemiologist Brooke Nichols has estimated that the 2025 dismantling of USAID contributed to more than 700,000 excess deaths over roughly a year. A study published in Science this May found that after USAID’s shutdown, places that had lost large amounts of the agency’s assistance experienced a marked uptick in armed clashes and riots. The development economists Marcella Nicolini and Fabio Sabatini, meanwhile, showed that economic activity in areas that formerly enjoyed substantial U.S. aid had dramatically declined.

No country can be a great power without a functioning foreign aid program. But today, the United States does not have one. After 90 percent of USAID’s programs were terminated and 90 percent of its staff members were laid off, Congress formally reappropriated most of the budgetary funding dedicated to U.S. foreign aid. But the Office of Management and Budget (OMB) has not released all of this appropriated funding. More damaging still, USAID’s mechanisms for spending aid money have been dismantled. According to the Pew Research Center, annual U.S. foreign aid disbursements fell by $26 billion between the 2023–24 and 2024–25 fiscal years; $2.5 billion in appropriated funding designated for foreign aid went unspent by the end of the 2025–26 fiscal year.

Russell Vought, OMB’s director and a self-identified Christian nationalist, has stonewalled spending for programs related to supporting democracy and insisted that humanitarian grants can be sent only to two specific Christian nongovernmental organizations. U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio, meanwhile, announced that he wanted to end funding to USAID development contractors, universities, and local civil society organizations without understanding the downside of other options for aid dollars, such as budget support, which are far less accountable, uniquely American, and results-oriented.

Given the suffering created by the end of USAID, it is even more troubling that DOGE’s objectives to slash waste and boost transparency have not been realized. U.S. President Donald Trump’s 2025 “big beautiful bill” erased any modest savings won by DOGE’s cuts to foreign aid. And the decision to move USAID’s functions to the State Department, which has weak business systems and inadequate staff to manage aid programs, has thus far led to the transfer of $3.8 billion in U.S. foreign aid money to the UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs and $15.9 billion directly into foreign governments’ treasuries through memorandums of understanding.

These governments often have limited capacity to spend the money wisely and produce results. In the past, USAID’s Office of the Inspector General has harshly criticized such options because it is nearly impossible to audit the transfers. Over 90 percent of the countries involved in these agreements score below 50 (out of 100) on Transparency International’s Corruption Perceptions Index, indicating serious systemic corruption. This may explain why State Department political appointees have kept the texts of these health agreements secret.

Clashing demands—that aid should relieve misery abroad, that it ought to serve U.S. military and diplomatic interests, and that it ought to yield rapid, visible, and quantifiable results—always complicated the enterprise. And over the course of 65 years since USAID’s 1961 founding, overregulation, a centralization of control over programs, and congressional meddling created a rigid straitjacket that made the United States less responsive to local governments and local needs. But what the current U.S. administration didn’t understand was that USAID’s bureaucracy, so often presented as an obstacle to effectiveness, was in fact an adaptation to manage these conflicts.

Simply abolishing that institutional architecture with no plan to replace it has only generated more confusion and waste. Meanwhile, China is racing to offer alternative sources of development financing, and Russia is seeking to expand its global influence through security partnerships. The United States cannot afford to let its foreign aid apparatus atrophy. It must redirect it, not reject aid.

Ever since USAID was created, the expectations that leaders in Washington had for foreign aid have conflicted with realities in the developing world, the way actual aid programs are managed, and the speed and nature of societal change. When I read through USAID’s 1960s-era archives to write my new book, I was struck that the demands early U.S. policymakers made of the institution were the same as those made today: policymakers wanted rapid, visible, and quantifiable results. Those demands were as unrealistic then as they are now. U.S. President John F. Kennedy hoped that poverty in Latin America would visibly recede within a couple of years of USAID’s creation, as he was understandably worried that the Cuban revolutionary leader Fidel Castro’s Marxist ideology was spreading across the region. Fourteen months after the organization’s inception, an article by Joseph Kraft in Harper’s Magazine reported that policymakers in Washington had already judged it a failure because it was not delivering “quick and visible results.” David Bell, USAID’s second administrator, recalled in 1965 that Kennedy had complained that the agency was “slow-moving, inefficient, and unresponsive to the President’s policy.”

But diplomacy and development operate on profoundly different time horizons. Nearly all foreign aid programs have diplomatic consequences, even if they aren’t designed to. When diplomatic goals become the main or only goals of an aid program, however—for instance, when U.S. aid kept the Congolese dictator Mobutu Sese Seko in power during the Cold War—good development often fails.

The United States began institutionalizing foreign aid after World War II through agencies and programs such as the Marshall Plan, which supported the postwar economic recovery of Western Europe, and the Point Four Program, a Truman administration initiative to promote development in poor countries to reduce their susceptibility to communism. All along, policymakers debated whether U.S. assistance programs should principally aim to save lives and reduce extreme poverty or to support U.S. foreign policy by countering the influence of hostile powers.

In fact, USAID ended up playing a key role in winning the Cold War. American assistance strengthened governments targeted for Soviet and Chinese communist subversion, particularly in Asia. After U.S. President Richard Nixon entered his second year in office, aid spending for agriculture was increased to 50 percent of USAID’s budget and was directed primarily toward Asia. According to multiple studies by development economists, the effort saved more than 245 million lives. The green revolution, implemented over decades in Pakistan, India, Indonesia, the Philippines, South Korea, and Taiwan (among other countries), helped produce large grain surpluses that fueled industrialization and economic growth and averted the kind of famines that historically produce coups or mass uprisings.

Throughout this era, however, Washington’s demands that aid accomplish diplomatic goals—and quickly and visibly—also undermined the most important elements of development programs: institution building and policy reform. Strong institutions separate wealthy countries from poor ones. The former have them in abundance, while the latter do not. Institution building and policy reform should be the focus of aid programs. But institution building and policy reform is difficult to measure, takes many years to implement successfully, and is not immediately visible. Instead, policymakers insisted that USAID do service delivery, producing valuable humanitarian programs that were ultimately unsustainable.

Most critical analyses of U.S. foreign aid conclude that USAID came to focus on process over substance, resulting in excessive bureaucracy. What these critiques overlook is what caused this obsessive focus on process. A web of federal regulatory and oversight agencies (what scholars call the counterbureaucracy) loosed on U.S. aid programs an army of federal auditors, accountants, regulators, compliance officers, and congressional staffers dedicated to reining in the bureaucracy and—supposedly—protecting taxpayers’ money. This onslaught slowed down USAID’s implementation of programs and created massive amounts of paperwork. It did not make aid programs more effective.

Historically, Congress ended up playing a particularly intrusive role in the management of U.S. foreign aid programs. USAID reported to 14 congressional committees, which often clashed, pulling USAID in different directions and slowing program implementation. (For example, the House and Senate agriculture committees, under the influence of the agriculture lobby, stonewalled efforts to reform food aid by insisting it be exported from the United States, while legislative appropriators and authorizers from foreign affairs committees strongly supported local purchase reform.) Congress also imposed intrusive earmarks—defined amounts allocated for specific programs, projects, or organizations. When countries asked for specific help, USAID often could not respond, because the glut of earmarks meant there was no discretionary money left.

Starting in the 1990s, the State Department attempted to exert more control over the USAID budget. A 1998 statute, for instance, made the USAID administrator report directly to the secretary of state. In January 2001, the State Department took control of USAID’s budget. That only led lawmakers—who often distrusted State—to add more earmarks. To protect U.S. foreign aid programs from an especially hostile Senate Foreign Relations Committee chairman, Republican Senator Jesse Helms, the Clinton administration instructed each cabinet-level department to create its own aid programs that would fall outside that committee’s jurisdiction. For example, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention at the Department of Health and Human Services established regional offices around the world, the Environmental Protection Agency developed solar energy projects in poor countries, and the Federal Emergency Management Agency signed agreements with more than 28 developing countries to assist in disaster management. USAID was already running programs such as these, yielding overlapping mandates which added to the incoherence of U.S. foreign aid.

When I became USAID administrator in 2001, I discovered a foreign aid program whose funds were straitjacketed by nearly 250 congressional earmarks, a sum exceeding USAID’s total appropriation. At the time, Ethiopia was one of the most severely food insecure countries in the world. Yet in 2007 only 1.5 percent of USAID’s budget was devoted to agricultural development, 1.5 percent to economic growth, and 1.5 percent to governance reform; Congressional earmarks made it impossible to fund these programs to match the Ethiopian government’s request.

Despite all these impediments, foreign aid remained a critical component of U.S. foreign policy. The Department of Defense had a particularly productive partnership with USAID, recognizing that foreign aid could be as important as force in incentivizing insurgents to make peace. The effects of USAID projects in Afghanistan were profound but took shape over long time horizons neither the State Department nor the Defense Department had any patience for. (USAID’s Afghanistan media-development program, for instance, was a great success, as documented in a Harvard Kennedy School study by The New York Times’s David Rohde.) Major academic studies have shown that in Iraq, community development projects undertaken by USAID’s Office of Transition Initiatives (OTI) employed large numbers of young men who might otherwise have been recruited into militias, reducing U.S. military casualties in the provinces with the highest levels of violence. In 2004, Army General Peter Chiarelli called the USAID officer managing the program, Kirk Day, a “hero” for saving his soldiers’ lives.

The DOGE team abolished OTI last year, along with innumerable other programs that alleviated suffering globally and enhanced U.S. soft power. When Congress restored many cuts to foreign aid, it did not reinstate USAID’s veteran staff, historical archives, business systems, field presence, or implementing partner networks of universities, international nongovernmental organizations, local civil society groups, and international development contractors. The transfer of programs to the Department of State only exacerbated the contradictions that have long bedeviled U.S foreign aid. Few secretaries of state (or secretaries of defense) have any development background, experience, or technical knowledge, and paradoxically, when aid is seen simply as a tool of diplomacy or defense rather than an end in itself, the diplomatic good it can do is compromised.

Dismantling USAID did not abolish the need for technically knowledgeable staff and bureaucratic systems. In May 2025, the State Department inspector general issued an intimidating report detailing what the department will have to do to competently manage foreign aid programs, a list of demands it will have difficulty meeting. And in September this year, the Government Accountability Office issued a highly critical audit of the State Department’s inadequate management of foreign aid programs, finding that the department lacked documented standards for deciding which aid programs to terminate, could not produce data on its own staffing, and maintained incomplete or incorrect records on grants and agreements. This result was unsurprising, given that the State Department is a diplomatic policy institution, not a program management agency. Merging USAID into the State Department makes as much sense as merging Microsoft with ExxonMobil, given the disparate time horizons on which the two institutions operate and their differences in mission statements and business systems.

The State Department has pursued alternative arrangements for distributing aid that require less direct management. For example, it has negotiated 36 memorandums of understanding with governments in developing countries to transfer U.S. aid funds to their health ministries, so they can spend the program money themselves. But in return for these transfers, Washington has asked these countries to send private health-care data to the U.S. government, accept undocumented immigrants the U.S. government deports, and, in some cases, give U.S. mining companies access to their rare-earth minerals. Because health programs save lives, they should be distributed based on need, not on parochial, transactional American demands.

Many countries have understandably resisted this kind of bargain. USAID had technical staff that could assess a foreign government’s ability to manage funds responsibly. In practice, it ran a detailed and thorough vetting of nearly every aspect of the governments it partnered with. The State Department, by contrast, only vets according to the legal minimum and treats foreign governments as it treats as international or U.S.-based NGOs. State’s approach will increase the risk that funds are stolen by local government ministries that lack the control systems needed to ensure accountability.

The very public and grossly inaccurate attacks on USAID’s integrity and management by Trump and DOGE’s erstwhile leader, Elon Musk, have damaged the agency’s reputation so extensively that simply resurrecting it will be difficult. But Washington’s aid apparatus also needs more coherent organization than it was afforded in the past. In his celebrated work Bureaucracy, on which my book is structured, the political scientist James Q. Wilson argues that strong U.S. departments or agencies need “relative autonomy”: clearly defined missions they do not substantially share with other agencies. By this logic, if the United States wants a high-functioning, coherent foreign aid program, it must first consolidate all aid functions within one department.

Abolishing field offices (called USAID missions) also made administering aid programs much more difficult, blunted true accountability and U.S. influence in the developing world, and reduced the visibility of U.S. aid programs. These country missions must be reestablished, reporting to the local U.S. ambassador. In Washington, Congress should create a new cabinet-level department reporting to the president that manages all the government’s aid programs, which would make it possible to trim staff, streamline program implementation, and avoid policy incoherence. At least 80 percent of this new department’s staff should be deployed abroad, not in Washington. The United States will always need to be able to launch aid programs with an expressly diplomatic purpose and short time horizon, and the State Department should determine these projects and their spending levels. But it should leave the programs’ management up to the new aid department—as it did during the Cold War, when USAID was independent of State.

This new department should also report to fewer congressional committees—a foreign affairs authorizing committee and an appropriations subcommittee in each legislative body. The practice of burdening the United States’ foreign aid budget with domestically appealing earmarks—which prevents aid from responding to foreign countries’ own needs—must be reduced.

In the past, the overregulation and bureaucratization of foreign aid driven by the demands of the counterbureaucracy prioritized process over programs and compliance over developmental outcomes. The new aid department must be given regulatory relief. One way to do that is for Congress to set up charter missions, similar to charter schools, as an experiment to see whether accountability deteriorates. These deregulated charter missions would be free from burdensome reporting requirements the counterbureaucracy demands. In recent years, the World Bank has tried a similar approach and found that it improved its programs’ effectiveness without increasing fraud.

The status quo for U.S. foreign aid cannot last. But Washington cannot revert to the old status quo, which generated the kind of dissatisfactions among policymakers that helped lead to its dismantling. Now is the time to bring more coherence to U.S. foreign aid programs with these reforms, if the new Democratic Congress and future administrations show a forward-looking vision.

Trump Should Refashion the Foreign Aid Agency, Not Dismantle It

#5912. The Journalists Who Left Russia—But Can’t Escape It

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: The Journalists Who Left Russia—But Can’t Escape It

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Understanding the conflict four years in.

What does a citizen owe to their country? And what if that country is Russia?

Such is the moral dilemma afflicting the characters of My Undesirable Friends: Part II – Exile, Julia Loktev’s sequel to her acclaimed 2024 documentary about the unraveling of Russia’s last independent television channel, Dozhd (or TV Rain), in the wake of the full-scale invasion of Ukraine. The first installment chronicles the channel’s final days in Moscow in March of 2022, and Part II follows its journalists into exile.

What does a citizen owe to their country? And what if that country is Russia?

Such is the moral dilemma afflicting the characters of My Undesirable Friends: Part II – Exile, Julia Loktev’s sequel to her acclaimed 2024 documentary about the unraveling of Russia’s last independent television channel, Dozhd (or TV Rain), in the wake of the full-scale invasion of Ukraine. The first installment chronicles the channel’s final days in Moscow in March of 2022, and Part II follows its journalists into exile.

Loktev’s protagonists, almost all women, fled as Russia imposed censorship laws that made their already dangerous jobs punishable by up to 15 years in prison. Within days of Dozhd’s disbandment, sometimes hours, they scooped up their laptops, pets, children, and underwear and flew wherever they could without a new visa—Istanbul, Tbilisi, Prague, Tel Aviv.

They resisted Russian President Vladimir Putin and his government, yet wherever they go, they remain citizens of a country that is invading and killing its neighbors and are held responsible for the war it wages in their name.

“You feel like it’s 1941 and you’re simultaneously the German and the Jew” says journalist Anna Nemzer, as she reloads her vape cartridge.

Loktev’s subjects are buffeted by cognitive dissonance and must constantly explain themselves—to their Georgian and Latvian hosts, who urge them to “tell Putin to stop the war”; to their Ukrainian sources, who resent the very language that they speak; to their Russian parents who tell them to stop writing anti-government “bullshit” and come home; and even to themselves, wondering what they could have done differently to stop their country’s slide into fascism, and whether their work matters when Russian missiles continue to rain down on Ukraine.

Loktev, who shot the film herself on an iPhone, gives these questions room. At six hours, this film is even longer than its predecessor—a bold choice—but the sheer accumulation of ordinary moments makes this exploration of exile intimate and compelling. For viewers willing to grapple with existential dilemmas and lucky enough to live near an art-house cinema, the movie’s length may be a boon. After all, its central questions are ones that take a lifetime to sort out.

Perhaps no one takes up these questions more bluntly than Elena Kostyuchenko of the recently shuttered Novaya Gazeta, where, during her tenure, four of her colleagues were murdered. “All of us … spent many years, thoroughly, in great detail, in very good, well-written articles describing the coming of fascism in Russia,” she says. “Well, there you fucking go. So we wrote all those articles.” Elsewhere, she is just as unsparing about the failure to stop Putin from turning Russia into what it is today: “We didn’t want to sacrifice our comfort.”

Alesya Marokhovskaya expresses similar unease as she recalls watching someone get beaten at a protest. “My job is to film,” she says. “You don’t rush in to defend the person. You film them, because, well, that’s your job.”

Despite those doubts, the journalists keep doing journalism. They provide truthful coverage of the war to Russian-speaking audiences through YouTube channels, podcasts, and eventually live broadcasts from Riga, Latvia, where Dozhd secured a broadcasting license. But they are walking on eggshells. A series of on-air missteps that led to questions about the station’s allegiances ultimately cost Dozhd its initial broadcasting license, forcing it to relocate to the Netherlands, where it operates today.

Irina Dolinina continues to work in exile from Prague.Cinetic

The hope of finding safety outside Russia does not last long. Alesya and her best friend Irina Dolinina, investigative journalists with IStories (or Important Stories), now based in Prague, receive oddly specific anonymous threats. The senders know their passport numbers, flight reservations, even about Alesya’s “snorting” bulldog. New friends are not always who they say they are: A supposed human rights lawyer Irina befriends previously worked for Moscow police. Alesya’s father, meanwhile, doesn’t understand why his daughter left. The war is in Ukraine, he says, not Russia.

But for most of these women, war is everywhere. From a temporary shelter in a Swiss village, where cathedral bells mark the hours of another peaceful day, Kostyuchenko recounts the symptoms of what she believes was a deliberate poisoning in Munich in October 2022: foul sweat, disorientation, nausea, swollen extremities.

Months before, reporting from the Ukrainian city of Mykolaiv, Kostyuchenko had visited a morgue overflowing with victims of Russian shelling, which she wrote about with brutal clarity. “In the fridge, the bodies are stacked in layers. Two girls lie on top of each other. They’re sisters. The older one is 17—in the heap of bodies, all I can see is her hand, long thin fingers with a neat pink manicure. The younger is 3—she lies on top of her sister. … Her body is dotted with red shrapnel wounds. The girl looks alive.”

Despite atrocities that should have stopped the world in its tracks, life goes on, and Loktev’s film captures that well. Kostyuchenko loves, and loves deeply. Her partner is Yana Kuchina, a Moscow book editor turned disability-rights advocate who followed Kostyuchenko into exile. A newcomer to Loktev’s saga, Yana delivers some of the film’s most chilling insights into how Russia treats its own. Her cerebral palsy, she says, is considered an “intellectual inadequacy” in the eyes of the authorities, barring her from getting a driver’s license, becoming a doctor, or adopting a child.

You’d have to be fearless or a fanatic to persist in journalism in Russia. These women are both.

So-called realists arguing for Ukraine’s surrender can’t let go of the past.

How Moscow broke with the West—and unleashed a more chaotic world.

Ksenia Mironova and co-director Julia Loktev speak after a screening of My Undesirable Friends: Part II: Exile at the New York Film Festival on Sept. 27.Michael Loccisano/Getty Images

Besides Yana, Kostyuchenko loves her mother—whose birthday she is missing for the first time—and her country. Her life there, she felt, was real. “I worked at the best newspaper on Earth. … All around me was my country, which I … value and love still.” Her love is definitely not blind, but for Ukrainians, whose country Russia is destroying, it may be difficult to comprehend, much less forgive.

Unlike Elena and Yana, former Dozhd reporter Ksenia (Ksyusha) Mironova walks the road of exile alone, a melancholy kaleidoscope of taxi rides, dark staircases, and endless rented rooms; her fiancé, the journalist Ivan Safronov, is still being held in a Moscow prison on fabricated charges of high treason. Ksyusha returns to Russia for his sentencing, a trip Loktev also documents. Ivan gets 22 years.

In the midst of so much loss, there are brief moments of tenderness. In a rented apartment abroad, Ksyusha and her Dozhd friends sing along to a Russian song: “When you return … I hope we recognize each other … and I am not your wife and not even your girlfriend.” It is probably the most Russian scene in the entire movie—elegiac, haunting, yet oddly hopeful.

After leaving Russia with her family, Anna Nemzer spent time in Tblisi, Georgia.Cinetic

Ksyusha is neither a victim nor a tragic heroine; behind her soft, girlish looks is a rod of steel. No longer with Dozhd, she moves from country to country, taking whatever freelance jobs she can find to keep herself afloat and working as an independent journalist covering political prisoners, their families, and the lives of Russians in exile. In 2025, we see her in the United States reporting on President Donald Trump’s second inauguration. “It’s really strange I’m now in America and the same things are happening here,” she says. She now works with Nemzer to archive independent journalism, including to preserve two decades of coverage in Russia against the Kremlin’s attempts to erase it.

Sonya Groysman, another Dozhd journalist, finds ingenious ways to report on Russia from exile, directing anonymous camera operators, including wedding videographers. One of her reports features Sasha Skochilenko, an artist who was sentenced to seven years in prison for replacing price tags in a St. Petersburg supermarket with anti-war messages. The camera zooms in on a black-and-white tag above the coffee cans reading “Russia’s Army bombed Mariupol art school where 400 people hid from shelling.” It’s yet another reminder that in Russia, the price of truth is your freedom.

Or your life. A sizable part of the documentary is devoted to the 2024 death of opposition leader Alexey Navalny. Dozhd, broadcasting from Amsterdam, ran videos of him from the day before his death, where he looks energetic and fairly healthy. In their coverage, the presenters called his death in prison what many in Russia knew but were afraid to say: a killing. Navalny went to prison to change people’s minds, says tearful Valeria Ratnikova, a Dozhd news anchor, between the airings of her show. He was a beacon for many. Now, all she sees is “total blackness ahead.”

Valeria Ratnikova stays in a hotel in Istanbul after fleeing Russia.Cinetic

The only characters spared this Dostoyevskian moral anguish are, unsurprisingly, Ukrainian. “We don’t have the energy and time to worry and panic,” says Nataliya Gumenyuk, a prominent Ukrainian journalist and war correspondent who is married to a longtime former Dozhd journalist. “We live in our own country and continue to do our work.” Among the movie’s most striking images is that of Ukrainians dancing in a square in Kharkiv, where entire blocks are flattened by air strikes, the sound of Ukrainian folk songs mixing with the howling of the air raid sirens. Nataliya’s Russian husband, Peter, resolved his moral dilemma by joining the Ukrainian army and now records his Fuck the War! podcast between missions.

From its new home in the Netherlands, the “optimistic channel,” as Dozhd was once known, still reaches 13 million as of 2023, according to Dozhd. Because coverage of the war not aligning with Kremlin narratives has been all but silenced, most watch through VPNs and proxies. For journalists, reporting on Russia’s war means living the Russian reality. This makes integration into their new countries nearly impossible; they are neither here nor there. But going home is not an option. Ratnikova, for one, was sentenced to seven-and-a-half years in prison in absentia by a Moscow court. Loktev records her hearing over a video call, where “witnesses” explain how the “false information” about the war “confused” them. This is how Russian justice works.

For now, Ratnikova has a visa, a job, a place to stay. That could change overnight. Political exile is not war, but that’s a low bar for young men and women who could have lived a relatively normal life. By reporting independently, first in an increasingly authoritarian state, then from exile, often at considerable risk to themselves, they have given more to their country than its perpetually silent majority.

One of Ratnikova’s colleagues likens their mission to Sisyphus pushing his stone uphill: not as punishment, but by choice. They keep at it, hoping their work might still change minds in a country that seems irretrievably lost. Loktev’s immersion in their lives leaves us with hope for them, if not for Russia’s future.

Anastasia Edel is a Russian-born American writer and social historian. She is the author of Russia: Putin’s Playground, a concise guide to Russian history, politics, and culture. Her writing has appeared in the Atlantic, the New York Review of Books, the New York Times, the Los Angeles Times, Project Syndicate, and others. She teaches history at the Osher Lifelong Learning Institute at the University of California, Berkeley. X: @AEdelWriter

#5910. Two top Trump Cabinet officials go to war over China spy scandal amid explosive accusations: report

Former Secretary of State Mike Pompeo outlines the national security concerns rising from ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s alleged affair with a suspected Chinese spy on ‘The Story.’

A White House Situation Room meeting reportedly turned into a blowout between two of President Donald Trump's Cabinet secretaries after news broke that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang had an affair with a suspect Chinese spy.

Tempers apparently boiled over regarding claims Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick dragged his feet on placing restrictions on a company run by a suspected Chinese spy with whom Huang admitted to having an affair, even after the Cabinet official received an intel memo informing him of the potential threat.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent reportedly confronted Lutnick about that in the meeting, and other Cabinet officials took Bessent's side, according to Axios.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent speaks with Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick before an event with President Donald Trump and Apple CEO Tim Cook in the Oval Office of the White House on Aug. 6, 2025, in Washington, D.C.  (Win McNamee/Getty Images)

Trump has for months called to place companies suspected of supplying China with Nvidia's complex AI chips on what is called the Entity List, a Department of Commerce export control list that identifies companies the U.S. government believes pose a serious risk to national security.

A SPY TOOK OUT NVIDIA’S CEO; HERE’S HOW AMERICA STILL WINS THE AI RACE

Three times — in May, August and last Thursday — Trump approved taking action against companies believed to be diverting Nvidia chips to China, according to Axios.

One of those companies is Singapore-based Megaspeed International, which is reportedly one of Nvidia's largest customers. Suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, with whom the former Nvidia CEO admitted to having an affair, is an executive at Megaspeed.

Lutnick was accused of slow-rolling in putting Megaspeed on the Entity List, which prompted Bessent to lose his patience.

When the news of Jensen Huang's affair broke, Lutnick, Bessent, Secretary of War Pete Hegseth, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer reportedly convened in the Situation Room.

Bessent confronted Lutnick about why the export controls had not already been put into place, and others in the room took Bessent's side, according to the Axios report.

REBECCA GRANT: JENSEN HUANG OWES AMERICA AN APOLOGY, AND NVIDIA OWES INVESTORS ANSWERS

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during the Nvidia GTC global AI conference in San Jose, Calif., March 17, 2026. (Reuters/File)

Lutnick said he would have to discuss it with the president despite Trump's May and August orders to proceed.

The group then met with Trump later in the day at the Oval Office. The president reportedly gave the order again, saying, "Let's get it done."

"I'll develop some options and come back," he said. The group replied that Trump's decision was final and that Lutnick must "go f---ing execute."

Lutnick is at the center of the scandal, especially after it was revealed he received a memo informing him about U.S. intelligence that Huang had a relationship with a suspected Chinese spy and still did nothing to put the company she ran on the Entity List.

AMERICA AND CHINA ARE FIGHTING FOR THE FUTURE OF AI — CHIPS ARE THE BATTLEFIELD

Commerce Secretary Lutnick, President Donald Trump and Secretary of the Treasury Bessent, (Getty Images)

CHINA ‘THROWING GASOLINE ON THE FIRE’ IN AI DATA CENTERS RACE, SAY LAWMAKERS

A spokesperson for the Commerce Department didn't directly address the report of Lutnick's confrontation with Bessent.

"The Department of Commerce and the Department of the Treasury have a great working relationship and are committed to advancing President Trump’s agenda," Kristen Eichamer told Fox News.

She also said Lutnick has proactively decided to appear before the Senate Banking Committee to answer questions on the matter. A hearing is scheduled for Dec. 10.

Bessent appeared on CNBC's "Sqwuak Box" Thursday morning, where he was asked about the reported blowout.

He said there was coordination between the various agencies about putting Megaspeed on the Entity List but then referred further questions to the Commerce Department and Bureau of Industry and Security.

"You will have to ask them about their internal proceedings," Bessent said.

#5909. La última oportunidad para la paz

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

PEDRO SÁNCHEZ es presidente del Gobierno de España.

En algún momento de la última década, el mundo empezó a aceptar la guerra como algo inevitable. Las guerras de agresión, como las de Ucrania, el Líbano o Irán, aumentan y siembran horror, ruina y el temor a una confrontación global. Los gobiernos piensan cada vez más en la guerra como algo para lo que hay que prepararse y presupuestar. El gasto militar no deja de crecer, las carreras tecnológicas y armamentísticas se intensifican, y cada vez más países se plantean si deberían dotarse de un arsenal nuclear disuasorio que garantice su seguridad.

No hay nada más peligroso que normalizar la guerra. Supone regresar a una norma que imperó durante la mayor parte de la historia de la humanidad. La guerra era el eje vertebrador del sistema internacional. Era la vía natural por la que los Estados zanjaban sus disputas o ensanchaban su territorio a costa de vecinos más débiles.

Pero la inmensa devastación que produjeron las dos guerras mundiales en la primera mitad del siglo XX hizo inaceptable ese statu quo. Los países se unieron en un empeño común: que la guerra dejara de ser un instrumento legítimo de la política de Estado y pasara a ser un acto ilegal. Las guerras no terminaron en 1945, pero durante años la comunidad internacional las condenó de forma abrumadora.

En las últimas décadas, sin embargo, el compromiso de los gobiernos con la causa de la paz se ha debilitado. En 2003, millones de personas salieron a las calles en cientos de ciudades para oponerse a la invasión de Irak liderada por Estados Unidos. Y, aun así, la guerra siguió adelante. Iniciada a partir de premisas falsas y en violación del derecho internacional, arrasó Irak, agravó la inestabilidad regional y avivó el resentimiento y el terrorismo contra los países occidentales. Aquel conflicto marcó, además, un punto de inflexión. Desde entonces, la búsqueda de la paz ha ido retrocediendo en la agenda internacional, arrinconada por la creciente competencia geopolítica y la desatención o la indiferencia hacia los esfuerzos multilaterales que durante décadas pusieron freno a la guerra.

Este camino conduce a una nueva guerra mundial: la catástrofe que el orden del siglo XX se propuso evitar. Ante ese futuro sombrío en el horizonte, los gobiernos deben reaprender el arte de oponerse a la guerra. Todos los países, incluido el mío, España, tienen la responsabilidad de actuar para limitar la capacidad de otros de librar la guerra y para fortalecer las instituciones internacionales que han de ser garantes de la paz.

El éxito de este proyecto dependerá de un multilateralismo expansivo, al que yo llamo «maxilateralismo»: una cooperación que abarque al mayor número posible de países, en lugar de limitarse a pequeñas coaliciones ad hoc. Su ambición es que la guerra resulte cada vez más inviable, incluso inimaginable. En 2003, manifestantes por toda España coreaban «¡No a la guerra!». En 2026, el mundo no puede volver a ignorarlos.

Los esfuerzos internacionales por desterrar la guerra como instrumento de política nacional se remontan al Pacto Briand-Kellogg de 1928, un tratado firmado en París por 15 países, al que posteriormente se sumaron decenas más. No bastó para impedir la Segunda Guerra Mundial, pero plantó una semilla. Cuando representantes de 50 países se reunieron en San Francisco en 1945 para crear las Naciones Unidas, recuperaron el espíritu y los principios del Pacto y añadieron lo que había faltado: estructura institucional. La Carta de Naciones Unidas prohibió recurrir a la amenaza o al uso de la fuerza contra la integridad territorial o la independencia política de otro Estado. Y, a diferencia de su predecesor de 1928, respaldó esa prohibición con instituciones facultadas para hacerla cumplir.

En las décadas siguientes, estas instituciones levantaron un orden que castigaba las violaciones al derecho internacional e incentivaba la cooperación y la diplomacia. Al mismo tiempo, el rechazo popular a la guerra y al militarismo de la Guerra Fría cobraba fuerza en todo el mundo. Se hizo visible en la resistencia a la guerra de Vietnam y en los movimientos de masas contra las armas nucleares. El pacifismo alcanzó su punto álgido en los años noventa, cuando una sociedad civil global pujante se movilizó en defensa de causas humanitarias y Naciones Unidas tenía autoridad para abanderar una ambiciosa agenda de paz.

Hoy, sin embargo, hay más conflictos armados que en ningún otro momento desde 1946. Según el Programa de Datos sobre Conflictos de Uppsala, en 2025 hubo 65 conflictos activos con participación estatal, el doble que en 2010. Se calcula que en estos conflictos murieron 250.000 personas: la cifra anual más alta desde el genocidio de Ruanda hace más de 30 años. Cerca de 120 millones de personas en todo el mundo siguen desplazadas por la guerra.

Y lo más inquietante: las guerras de agresión, que violan la Carta de Naciones Unidas, están de vuelta. Rusia invadió Ucrania. Estados Unidos e Israel atacaron Irán. Y mientras los combates se prolongan año tras año en lugares como el Líbano, Sudán o Yemen, resulta evidente que la guerra está siendo aceptada de nuevo como algo normal.

Nada indica que esta tendencia vaya a frenarse. Los presupuestos militares se han disparado hasta niveles no vistos en generaciones, en detrimento del gasto social y de desarrollo. Solo en la última década, el gasto mundial en defensa ha aumentado más de un 40 %, y muchos países han duplicado sus presupuestos militares. Los casi 3 billones de dólares que el mundo destina cada año a defensa se acercan al producto interior bruto de todo el continente africano. Incluso países con una larga tradición de contención militar, como Alemania, Dinamarca, Japón y Noruega, se están rearmando a toda velocidad, y algunos están recuperando el servicio militar.

Aún más estremecedor es el regreso de la amenaza de guerra nuclear. Los nueve Estados con armas nucleares están modernizando sus arsenales y aumentando su capacidad destructiva. Se calcula que el arsenal de China ha pasado de entre unas 200-300 cabezas nucleares a finales de la década de 2010 a 620 este año. Corea del Norte también ha ampliado el suyo, de entre 10-20 cabezas estimadas en 2018 a unas 60 este año, y ha desarrollado plataformas de lanzamiento cada vez más sofisticadas. En marzo, Francia anunció el primer aumento de su número de cabezas nucleares desde 1992. Estados Unidos y Rusia, las dos mayores potencias nucleares del mundo, no han sido capaces de preservar el último tratado que ponía límites a sus arsenales estratégicos. Y cada vez más potencias no nucleares han empezado a plantearse adquirir armas nucleares propias.

Al mismo tiempo, la inteligencia artificial (IA) está dotando a la guerra de armas cada vez más peligrosas. La supervisión humana se reduce progresivamente a medida que la IA permite automatizar ciberataques contra redes eléctricas, hospitales y sistemas financieros. Hay sistemas de IA que ya han logrado infiltrarse en organismos públicos y han hecho posible la producción masiva de deepfakes y de campañas de desinformación.

La integración de la IA en las tecnologías nucleares es una amenaza existencial: al acelerar la toma de decisiones, comprime el tiempo disponible para el juicio humano. Del mismo modo, el uso de la IA en biotecnología podría rebajar drásticamente las barreras a la experimentación nociva y al desarrollo de armas biológicas. En ambos terrenos, esta tecnología está elevando a niveles sin precedentes el riesgo de errores de cálculo, de escaladas involuntarias y de destrucción masiva.

En conjunto, estas dinámicas multiplican las probabilidades de que cualquier crisis desemboque rápidamente en la aniquilación del planeta. Por desgracia, los países están fabricando los instrumentos de la catástrofe a la velocidad vertiginosa de la innovación, mientras que los instrumentos de control avanzan al ritmo, mucho más lento, de la diplomacia.

Los cambios políticos están alejando al mundo de la paz. Lo empujan hacia la militarización y la confrontación. Un mundo multipolar no es, por definición, más peligroso. Pero la erosión de las normas y de las instituciones que deberían encauzar las tensiones entre los distintos polos está provocando que el mundo se vuelva especialmente volátil. La competencia entre grandes potencias se intensifica: las ansias de hegemonía alientan políticas exteriores agresivas y los Estados revisionistas desafían las normas y las fronteras establecidas. El nacionalismo beligerante crece. Los movimientos de extrema derecha promueven una visión del mundo en la que las relaciones internacionales son un juego de suma cero y los países deben competir en lugar de cooperar. Incluso dirigentes de centro están adoptando la retórica de la guerra. Aunque no pretendan iniciar una, sus palabras abonan una mentalidad militarista peligrosa.

En este clima de rivalidad, las guerras de agresión se envuelven en discursos de necesidad, liberación, legítima defensa o justicia histórica. Los agresores presentan sus operaciones como inevitables, después de haber demonizado a sus adversarios para justificar un ataque preventivo. Venden cada guerra como la respuesta a la inseguridad: la guerra que acabará con todas las guerras.

Pero estas guerras no harán del mundo un lugar más seguro. Cada vez que un Estado desata una guerra de agresión, erosiona las normas que protegen a todos los países frente al uso arbitrario de la fuerza, incluido el propio Estado agresor.

Las guerras tienen un coste inmenso. Destrozan sociedades y truncan vidas. En 2025, según los registros de Naciones Unidas, al menos un civil murió en conflicto armado cada 14 minutos, y más de cinco millones de personas se vieron obligadas a huir de sus países. El coste económico también es altísimo. La guerra que enfrenta a Estados Unidos e Israel con Irán ha alterado gravemente el comercio marítimo y el tráfico aéreo y ha disparado los precios de la energía, los fertilizantes y otras materias primas. Millones de personas en todo el mundo están sufriendo las consecuencias.

La guerra genera, además, nuevos peligros. Una vez que empiezan los combates, su rumbo es imprevisible. Los conflictos tienden a arrastrar a nuevos actores y a desbordarse hacia los países vecinos. La guerra de Irán vuelve a ser un ejemplo claro. Desde febrero de 2026, Irán ha atacado objetivos en varios países del Golfo y de su entorno, acciones que España ha condenado sin excepción. Los ataques contra buques civiles y mercantes amenazan la estabilidad del conjunto de la región. Y la guerra ha reavivado los conflictos de Irak, el Líbano y Yemen.

Por último, las guerras rara vez traen una paz duradera. Al contrario: ahondan los agravios y enquistan las divisiones que alimentan la violencia del mañana. La guerra de Rusia en Ucrania, por ejemplo, ha dinamitado la confianza en Moscú. El genocidio en Gaza ha exacerbado la indignación contra Israel, y eso podría desembocar en nuevas oleadas de terrorismo. Y la guerra contra Irán ha desestabilizado aún más la región y ha lastrado la cooperación entre sus países.

El mundo tiene la responsabilidad de resistirse a la normalización de la guerra. Callar es un acto de cobardía y de complicidad. La sociedad española está dejando clara su posición: tres de cada cuatro españoles señalan la guerra como su mayor temor. Y un número aún mayor cree que se cierne otra guerra mundial.

Pero no basta con condenar la guerra en abstracto. La ambición de acabar con ella debe ir acompañada de acciones concretas que hagan cada vez más difícil que los países inicien, sostengan y justifiquen un conflicto armado.

España ha procurado actuar en esa dirección. Ha defendido las normas internacionales que marcan la diferencia entre un orden basado en reglas y otro gobernado por la fuerza. Y ha denunciado siempre los ataques armados que violan la Carta de Naciones Unidas.

Desde el primer día de la guerra en Gaza, España ha reclamado un alto el fuego inmediato y permanente, la liberación de todos los rehenes y la entrada sin restricciones de la ayuda humanitaria. En mayo de 2024, España reconoció el Estado de Palestina con la esperanza de que este paso contribuyera a avanzar hacia una solución negociada y duradera del conflicto. Y ha apoyado activamente los esfuerzos de la Corte Internacional de Justicia y de la Corte Penal Internacional para que los dirigentes israelíes rindan cuentas por sus violaciones del derecho internacional humanitario. Israel tenía derecho a la legítima defensa tras los atentados terroristas del 7 de octubre. Pero ese derecho no justifica el genocidio en Gaza ni la expansión de la ocupación ilegal de Cisjordania.

Disuadir la guerra exige también elevar el coste de la agresión. El resto del mundo debe asegurarse de que quien elija hacer la guerra no pueda hacerlo sin pagar un precio. A lo largo de la invasión rusa de Ucrania, por ejemplo, la Unión Europea ha aprobado sucesivas rondas de sanciones contra Rusia que España ha respaldado sin titubeos. España también ha prestado a Ucrania ayuda financiera, humanitaria y militar para que pueda defender su territorio y negarle a Rusia el botín de guerra.

Los países deben negarse a ayudar a las partes a extender los conflictos en curso. España, por ejemplo, mantiene un embargo de armas a Israel desde 2023. Este año, además, negó a Estados Unidos el uso de su territorio y de sus instalaciones para la guerra contra Irán. La decisión de España no fue un acto de hostilidad hacia un aliado. Tampoco fue una expresión de neutralidad ni de simpatía hacia el régimen iraní. Fue el fruto de una valoración estratégica: implicarse habría favorecido la escalada del conflicto y agravado la inseguridad regional y mundial. Para ser fiel a su compromiso con la paz, España tenía que estar dispuesta a decir no, aunque eso tuviera un coste político. Si más países hubieran actuado igual, las guerras de Gaza y de Irán habrían sido mucho más difíciles y costosas de librar.

Invertir en prosperidad es igualmente esencial. La seguridad crece cuando las sociedades son resilientes, las desigualdades se reducen y la ciudadanía puede confiar en las instituciones públicas. La decisión de España, el año pasado, de no asumir el objetivo de gasto en defensa del 5 % del PIB propuesto por la OTAN refleja esta visión. No nace de una falta de compromiso con la defensa europea. De hecho, mi Gobierno ha sido uno de los principales impulsores de la autonomía estratégica de la Unión Europea, triplicando el gasto neto en defensa en los ocho años que llevo en el cargo. Pero la disuasión militar y el bienestar social deben reforzarse mutuamente. Al mantener un gasto en defensa proporcionado, España ha conservado el margen fiscal necesario para invertir 145.000 millones de euros adicionales en protección social, vivienda, sanidad y educación, y más de 30.000 millones en la transición ecológica. Son políticas que cohesionan la sociedad y, por tanto, la hacen más segura.

Los esfuerzos de un solo país tienen, sin embargo, un alcance limitado. Cortarle el oxígeno a la guerra exige la implicación de todos: un esfuerzo colectivo basado en un multilateralismo genuino. Esta es la única estrategia que ha funcionado en el pasado. El sistema multilateral construido tras la Segunda Guerra Mundial abarcaba todo el planeta y contribuyó a preservar la paz entre las grandes potencias y a evitar una tercera guerra mundial.

Reforzarlo no será tarea fácil. La pugna geopolítica y una desconfianza generalizada han debilitado a Naciones Unidas, los organismos financieros internacionales, las organizaciones regionales y otras instituciones multilaterales. Muchos países las perciben como ineficaces y atrapadas en una crisis perpetua, de modo que están recurriendo a marcos alternativos para intentar poner fin a las guerras.

Algunos han optado por enfoques unilaterales, persiguiendo acuerdos transaccionales que acaban beneficiando a élites económicas y políticas y dejan al margen a las poblaciones afectadas por el conflicto. Los altos el fuego o los acuerdos de paz que logran pueden ser preferibles a la continuación de la guerra, pero cualquier paz impuesta por la ley del más fuerte, y no por el derecho, se asienta sobre cimientos frágiles.

Otros países, frustrados con el multilateralismo, han intentado formar «coaliciones de voluntarios» u otros grupos «minilaterales» para garantizar su seguridad o alcanzar acuerdos ad hoc en materia de comercio o tecnología. Este enfoque puede ser una solución temporal a la parálisis de las instituciones tradicionales, pero su carácter excluyente fragmenta aún más un orden internacional ya dividido. Lo que necesitamos no es minilateralismo, sino maxilateralismo. La cooperación internacional alcanza su verdadera fuerza cuando es—o aspira a ser—universal. Dejar a una parte del mundo fuera de estos esfuerzos es, además, incubar la confrontación de mañana.

El sistema internacional del siglo XX no era perfecto. Sus normas se han aplicado de manera desigual, sus principios se han defendido de forma inconsistente y sus procedimientos han sido desesperantemente lentos. Pero es innegable que, sin él, el mundo sería un polvorín aún mayor. Basta un ejemplo: fue una red de tratados internacionales—entre ellos, el Tratado sobre la No Proliferación de las Armas Nucleares, el Tratado sobre Fuerzas Nucleares de Alcance Intermedio y el Tratado de Prohibición Parcial de los Ensayos Nucleares—la que puso fin a una carrera armamentística desbocada y extraordinariamente costosa que estaba llevando al mundo al borde de una guerra nuclear.

Toda esta arquitectura institucional se está resquebrajando. Y sin ella, aspirar a preservar la paz es una quimera.

Fortalecer las instituciones multilaterales debe ser el eje central de una campaña global para relegar la guerra a los libros de historia. Para ello será necesario acometer reformas de calado que les devuelvan la legitimidad y refuercen su capacidad de actuación, dando prioridad a las medidas que más contribuyan a la paz y la seguridad.

La primera pasa por aumentar la representación en las Naciones Unidas y las instituciones de Bretton Woods. Actualizar la estructura del Consejo de Seguridad de Naciones Unidas es especialmente importante, dada su responsabilidad principal de mantener la paz internacional. Un Consejo de Seguridad con asientos permanentes y derecho de veto para las cinco potencias vencedoras de la Segunda Guerra Mundial, que en conjunto suman apenas una cuarta parte de la población mundial, no refleja el mundo de hoy. Debe ampliarse el número de miembros electos del Consejo y alargar su mandato más allá de los dos años actuales. Los nuevos asientos deben otorgarse principalmente a países de regiones infrarrepresentadas, en particular África y América Latina. Organizaciones regionales como la Unión Europea y la Unión Africana también deberían estar representadas en el Consejo, posiblemente ocupando puestos que hoy corresponden a Estados. Un Consejo de Seguridad más representativo no solo sería más justo, sino también más eficaz. Los países defienden y hacen cumplir con más empeño las normas internacionales cuando se sienten autores de ellas.

También hay que restringir estrictamente el poder de veto de los miembros permanentes, en especial cuando el Consejo de Seguridad delibere sobre casos de atrocidades masivas, genocidio, crímenes de lesa humanidad y crímenes de guerra. En última instancia, el veto debe abolirse por completo. Solo entonces podrá el Consejo responder a todos los actos de agresión sin quedar secuestrado por las objeciones de un solo Estado.

Un espíritu similar debería orientar la transformación estructural de las instituciones financieras internacionales, en particular el Fondo Monetario Internacional y el Banco Mundial. La distribución del poder de voto en ambas sigue reflejando los equilibrios económicos del siglo XX y, por convención, un europeo está siempre al frente del FMI y un estadounidense, del Banco Mundial. Esto debe terminar. Las economías emergentes necesitan una representación más adecuada, y los puestos de liderazgo deben estar abiertos a todos los candidatos, sea cual sea su nacionalidad. También deberán evolucionar las responsabilidades que asumen en instituciones como la Organización Mundial del Comercio los países antes considerados «en desarrollo», a medida que crecen y se incorporan al grupo de las economías más ricas. Una gobernanza económica más justa aliviaría la desigualdad y el resentimiento que con tanta frecuencia atizan la inestabilidad y los conflictos.

Otra reforma esencial consiste en revitalizar el marco internacional que contiene el riesgo de escalada nuclear. Parte de ese trabajo depende de las potencias nucleares. Corresponde a Estados Unidos y a Rusia negociar un tratado que suceda al acuerdo bilateral Nuevo START y que preserve topes verificables al despliegue de armas nucleares. Y, esta vez, China debería participar en las conversaciones.

Pero el esfuerzo más amplio exige el compromiso de todos los países. Entre sus prioridades deben figurar la ratificación del Tratado de Prohibición Completa de los Ensayos Nucleares, para impedir que se reanuden las pruebas nucleares, y el avance de las negociaciones sobre un Tratado de Prohibición de la Producción de Material Fisionable, que prohibiría la producción de uranio altamente enriquecido y plutonio para armas nucleares. Todos deben también trabajar conjuntamente para regular el uso de la IA en el ámbito militar, especialmente en lo que respecta a las armas nucleares, a fin de preservar el control humano sobre las decisiones de lanzamiento y minimizar el riesgo de errores fatales. Los países no nucleares deben comprometerse a no adquirir armas nucleares y deben emplear todos los medios a su alcance para instar a las potencias nucleares a reducir sus arsenales, reforzar los mecanismos de verificación y reconstruir las salvaguardas diplomáticas que durante décadas han ayudado a evitar una catástrofe nuclear.

La tecnología puede contribuir en parte a esta tarea. Los avances en imágenes por satélite, sensores remotos y análisis de datos pueden aumentar la transparencia y la supervisión del cumplimiento de los acuerdos de control de armamentos. Pero también hay que hacer mucho más para evitar situaciones que puedan precipitar una guerra. Si los Estados con armas nucleares acuerdan reducir su disponibilidad operacional y reforzar la comunicación en momentos de tensión, los gobiernos contarán con más tiempo y más medios para desactivar las crisis. Cada país debe proteger sus sistemas de mando y control frente a los ciberataques y la manipulación informativa impulsada por la IA, de modo que una falsa alarma o un error de cálculo no desencadenen jamás una guerra nuclear.

Por último, es prioritario trabajar para garantizar que el sistema de justicia internacional pueda hacer cumplir las decisiones de los tribunales. Desmantelar la Corte Penal Internacional, como pretenden algunos países, solo aseguraría la impunidad de los crímenes más atroces. La CPI no solo debe investigar y juzgar los casos de genocidio, agresión, crímenes de guerra y crímenes de lesa humanidad, sino también contar con la autoridad necesaria para llevar ante la justicia a los responsables de esos crímenes. Cuando un Estado pasa a ser parte del Estatuto de Roma, el tratado que creó la CPI, acepta someterse a la jurisdicción de la Corte. Sin embargo, si ese Estado se niega a cooperar o a entregar a un sospechoso, no existe una sanción efectiva, sino apenas una llamada de atención. Esto debe cambiar. Los Estados que den cobijo a personas imputadas por la CPI deben afrontar consecuencias legales, diplomáticas y financieras. Iniciar y librar una guerra debe tener un coste real.

La reforma de las instituciones internacionales lleva años estancada, pero la próxima elección para la Secretaría General de Naciones Unidas ofrece una ocasión excepcional para darle un nuevo impulso. Que Naciones Unidas nombrara a su primera secretaria general sería un poderoso símbolo de renovación, inclusión y adaptación institucional. La organización no puede afirmar verdaderamente que habla en nombre de toda la humanidad mientras no la haya dirigido una mujer. Alcanzar este hito podría despertar una nueva ambición y reforzar la confianza en la capacidad de las instituciones internacionales para reformarse y para que recuperen su papel a la hora de poner freno a la guerra.

Frenar la deriva del mundo hacia la guerra exigirá el compromiso más amplio posible. Ningún país, grande o pequeño, puede mirar hacia otro lado ni sumarse a las acciones que aviven la escalada. Todos debemos defender las normas internacionales que hoy ponen coto a la guerra y reformar y dotar de poder a las instituciones que mañana la harán imposible.

En última instancia, lo que hará inimaginable la guerra es una agenda de paz positiva que aborde las causas profundas de la inseguridad. Cada paso hacia la erradicación de la pobreza, la ampliación del acceso a una educación de calidad, el avance de la igualdad de género, el fortalecimiento de las instituciones democráticas y el aumento de la resiliencia ante los desastres naturales atenúa también las condiciones que engendran la violencia.

Las instituciones financieras internacionales deben ser capaces de proporcionar financiación con rapidez, a gran escala y a un coste razonable, para que ningún país en desarrollo se vea obligado a elegir entre atender su deuda e invertir en su población. Los bancos multilaterales de desarrollo deben poder movilizar fondos para las transiciones ecológica y digital, de modo que las migraciones climáticas y la disputa por los recursos no se conviertan en detonantes de conflictos entre Estados. Y el Acuerdo sobre Pandemias de la Organización Mundial de la Salud debe ultimarse y entrar en vigor para garantizar que todos los países puedan acceder a vacunas y tratamientos cuando llegue la próxima emergencia sanitaria mundial.

Por eso España ha procurado situar el desarrollo en el centro de su política exterior. El Gobierno español ha sido un firme defensor de los Objetivos de Desarrollo Sostenible de la ONU para 2030 y, el año pasado, la ciudad de Sevilla acogió la Cuarta Conferencia Internacional sobre la Financiación para el Desarrollo, donde la comunidad internacional se comprometió a aligerar la carga de la deuda de los países vulnerables y a desbloquear inversiones para el desarrollo sostenible. España también ha promovido iniciativas como el Centro Global para Canjes de Deuda por Desarrollo, lanzado junto con el Banco Mundial; la Alianza multilateral por las Cláusulas de Suspensión de Deuda, que permite a los países vulnerables suspender el pago de su deuda tras una catástrofe; y un mecanismo propio para ofrecer hasta 60 millones de euros anuales en alivio de deuda a países en desarrollo. Además, España ha respaldado, junto con instituciones multilaterales y organizaciones internacionales, iniciativas de financiación climática, salud global, igualdad de género, seguridad alimentaria y protección social. Estas políticas de desarrollo son también políticas de construcción de paz.

Un proyecto ambicioso de movilización global en favor del derecho internacional y de un multilateralismo genuino puede parecer poco realista. Pero ya hemos visto lo que ocurre cuando la normalización de la guerra arrastra a la humanidad al infierno. Los hombres y las mujeres que se levantaron de entre las ruinas de la Segunda Guerra Mundial, el capítulo más oscuro de nuestra historia, encontraron la manera de construir un orden mundial multilateral. La pregunta a la que se enfrenta nuestra generación es si seremos capaces de preservar el imperio de la ley o permitiremos el regreso de la ley de la jungla. Si construiremos el mundo del mañana con visión de futuro y cooperación, o a través del caos y del dolor de otra guerra mundial. Debemos elegir bien mientras la decisión siga estando en nuestras manos.
