#5898. The Case for a Trump-Xi-Putin Summit

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

CHARLES KUPCHAN is Professor of International Affairs at Georgetown University and a Senior Fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations. He is the author of Bringing Order to Anarchy: Governing the World to Come.

In just a few days’ time, U.S. President Donald Trump, Chinese leader Xi Jinping, and Russian President Vladimir Putin could be holding a three-way summit on the sidelines of next week’s Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation meeting in Shenzhen, China. According to The Wall Street Journal, Putin floated the idea of a trialogue last month as an opportunity to discuss ending the war in Ukraine. No firm plan has yet been announced, but even the prospect of this meeting has provoked widespread consternation. Polish Foreign Minister Radoslaw Sikorski warned against a repeat of the Yalta Conference of 1945, when U.S. President Franklin Roosevelt and British Prime Minister Winston Churchill capitulated to Soviet leader Joseph Stalin’s push for the Soviet Union to dominate Eastern Europe. U.S. Senator Chris Coons posted on X that “there can be no talks about Ukraine’s future without Ukraine at the table, and there can be no real negotiations as long as Trump signals weakness and servitude to Xi and Putin at every opportunity.”

These worries are entirely justified; Trump has a track record of cozying up to autocrats and turning his back on democratic allies. Should he sit down with Xi and Putin, he must avoid selling out European and Asian partners who will not be at the table. And given the three leaders’ headstrong personalities and competing national interests that pit the United States against what Xi calls a “no limits” partnership between China and Russia, the meeting might ultimately yield little progress on major issues.

Nevertheless, Trump would be right to join a summit with Xi and Putin. No U.S. president has ever held a trilateral meeting with the leaders of China and Russia, and the proposed talks would break the ice and begin to normalize much-needed dialogue. Authoritarianism is not going away, so autocracies and democracies must learn not only to live comfortably alongside one another but also to work together to tame geopolitical rivalry and tackle global challenges in an interdependent world. Americans will not find it easy to get along with the likes of Russia and China, whose political systems threaten the U.S. commitment to spreading democracy. But Trump is taking a bold step in the right direction by engaging with the heads of the world’s leading autocracies.

If he meets with Xi and Putin, Trump should aim low and focus on making meaningful, if limited, progress on a small number of core issues that could benefit from a three-way conversation: the flash points of the Korean Peninsula, Ukraine, and Iran, as well as arms control and strategic stability. But even if the summit yields no concrete results, it will nonetheless have been worthwhile. Trump, Xi, and Putin are paving the way for their successors to advance great-power cooperation across ideological lines and stabilize a fragmented world.

I have argued previously, including in a 2021 Foreign Affairs essay with Richard Haass, that a global concert—an informal steering group of great powers modeled on the nineteenth-century Concert of Europe—offers the best hope of curbing geopolitical competition and enabling collective action in a multipolar world. In addition to the United States, China, and Russia, a new concert would include India, Japan, the European Union, and representatives from Africa, Latin America, the Middle East, and Southeast Asia. Concerts sacrifice representation for efficacy and give only the geopolitically influential states that must be there a seat at the table. They set aside ideological differences and bring democracies and autocracies together to focus on international cooperation. Because concerts eschew binding, enforceable procedures in favor of informal consensus building, they can succeed when formal institutions such as the UN Security Council are paralyzed and when the United States wavers in its commitment to rules-based multilateralism.

But the time has not come for a global concert. Concerts function effectively only when their members are committed to the territorial status quo, are willing to exercise strategic restraint and pursue mutual benefits, and are ready to settle disputes diplomatically. Putin’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine and Xi’s decision to back Russia have, for now, scuttled hopes that the great powers will normalize restraint and the peaceful settlement of disputes. Meanwhile, Trump’s “America first” statecraft shuns joint action and mutual gain in favor of unilateralism.

Yet even though Trump, Xi, and Putin are the wrong leaders at the wrong time to establish a great-power steering group that other major players would eventually join, Trump’s iconoclastic diplomacy sets a useful precedent. His Alaska summit with Putin last year and two summits with Xi this year were admittedly long on pageantry and short on substance. But by taking the controversial step of pursuing joint talks with the two leaders, Trump is widening the diplomatic Overton window and expanding what is politically possible for future U.S. presidents. Avoiding great-power conflict and taming a world in increasing disarray requires the United States to build constructive relations with both China and Russia. Trump deserves credit for starting a trialogue that his successors can carry forward.

If they meet in Shenzhen, Trump, Xi, and Putin should focus on easing tensions at key geopolitical flash points. The Korean Peninsula presents the best opportunity for the three leaders to collaborate. At the UN General Assembly in September, South Korean President Lee Jae-myung laid out an ambitious plan for normalizing relations with Pyongyang, including a proposal to replace the peninsula’s 1953 armistice with a “peace regime” that promotes “respect for each other’s systems and acknowledgement of our differences.” As part of that plan, Lee has advocated a phased approach to North Korean denuclearization, with an initial freeze on weapons development followed by gradual reductions in Pyongyang’s arsenal of nuclear weapons and ballistic missiles until the peninsula is denuclearized.

Lee’s bold proposal could bring U.S., Chinese, and Russian interests into alignment. Trump has made it clear that he wants to de-escalate tensions on the Korean Peninsula and is already seeking a meeting with North Korean leader Kim Jong Un. Beijing and Moscow are competing for influence over Kim, but both want a stable relationship between North and South Korea, so they might be willing to cooperate on proposals to halt nuclear weapons production and encourage talks between Pyongyang and Seoul. Trump, Xi, and Putin should aim to emerge from their meeting with a plan for advancing Lee’s peace agenda; jointly supporting a freeze on North Korean nuclear weapons development is a good place to start.

The prospects for progress toward ending the war in Ukraine are much dimmer but not nonexistent. Trump should be striving to weaken Russia’s ability to sustain the war, but he has long resisted tightening sanctions on Moscow. He talks up potential business ventures with Russia and, in recent weeks, even struck a deal with Putin to get more Russian diesel into global markets and waived a new round of congressional sanctions against Moscow. Trump evidently cares more about trying to bring down fuel prices than about curbing Russia’s ability to fund its war against Ukraine.

A summit offers Trump the opportunity to change course and try to convince Xi to restrain Putin. Trump has vowed to bring the war in Ukraine to an end, and the Chinese leader can help him achieve that goal. Xi has enormous leverage over Putin: China backs the Kremlin diplomatically, is the largest buyer of Russian oil and gas, and is the main supplier of dual-use items such as semiconductors and machine tools that are critical to Russian weapons manufacturing.

Xi has played along with Putin’s war against Ukraine so far, presumably because the conflict diverts U.S. attention and resources away from Asia, divides NATO, and allows Xi and Putin to cast themselves as leading a global resistance to Western hegemony. But when Xi and Trump met this May, the leaders agreed to pursue “a constructive relationship of strategic stability” between China and the United States, which Xi could advance by pressing Putin to de-escalate. As more Russians feel the effects of the war, the Kremlin may be newly receptive to such pressure. Ukrainian drone strikes are taking a significant toll on Russia’s oil sector and logistics networks, which means Russian citizens face mounting gas and commodities shortages. Meanwhile, some 35,000 Russian soldiers per month are losing their lives or sustaining severe injuries in eastern Ukraine, forcing Putin to consider the unpopular move of ramping up military mobilization. With no battlefield victory in sight, Putin has reasons to entertain a diplomatic off-ramp.

Trump and Xi could press Putin to accept a set of limited but consequential steps to de-escalate the conflict. These could include an agreement between Russia and Ukraine—brokered and monitored by Washington and Beijing—to end attacks on energy installations and infrastructure, civilian targets, and commercial shipping. Trump and Xi could also jointly press Putin to refrain from actions that could broaden the war, such as penetrating NATO airspace with Russian drones, sabotaging European infrastructure, and threatening to use nuclear weapons. This pressure, combined with the allure of improving diplomatic and commercial ties to the United States, might convince Putin to relent on at least some of these issues.

Trump could also use a trilateral summit to seek help extricating the United States from its war in Iran—a quagmire that has produced regional mayhem and soaring oil prices. As Tehran continues to issue maximalist demands and the war disrupts shipping through the straits of Hormuz and Bab el Mandeb, Trump needs an out.

Neither Xi nor Putin has yet been inclined to lend a hand. China is still buying Iranian oil and providing Tehran with military intelligence and dual-use items that end up in Iranian drones and missiles, and Russia is providing satellite targeting data, munitions, advanced drone technology, and logistical support. Both Beijing and Moscow are happy to see the United States mired in a conflict that is exacerbating divisions within the American electorate and fracturing NATO.

As a major energy exporter, Russia benefits from the spike in oil prices that the conflict has triggered, so pressing Putin to cease supporting Iran is likely a lost cause. But China is the world’s largest oil importer, and Beijing has a vested interest in lowering energy prices by stabilizing exports from the Middle East. Although China’s large oil reserves have buffered its economy from rising oil prices so far, those reserves have been significantly depleted. China’s crude oil imports have begun to climb again, leaving its economy exposed to high energy prices if the conflict drags on. And as the world’s largest exporter of goods, China suffers from the dampening effect high energy prices have on global growth.

Xi might therefore be amenable to helping Trump end the war. Trump should ask Xi to stop or at least slow the flow of Chinese dual-use items to Iran. Washington’s naval blockade and financial and commercial sanctions on Tehran have already taken a toll on Iran’s economy and military supply chains. By cutting off Chinese technology shipments to Iran, Beijing could deepen this economic isolation and help convince Tehran to negotiate an end to the conflict.

Xi could also press Iran to drop its unrealistic demands for ending the war, including that the United States withdraw its forces from the Gulf, release frozen Iranian assets, and remove economic sanctions. Washington might well be willing to offer economic relief, but only as part of postwar negotiations toward a deal that reins in Iran’s nuclear ambitions. Xi could help clear the way for those talks by urging Iran to accept credible terms for ending the immediate conflict.

Beyond working to resolve current conflicts, Trump should promote future strategic stability by pursuing arms control negotiations with Xi and Putin. Every arms control agreement negotiated between Washington and Moscow has expired; their final nuclear pact, New START, ended in February. There are currently no caps on the American and Russian strategic arsenals and no regular inspections, data exchanges, or notifications of nuclear exercises. This nuclear free-for-all is particularly dangerous given Russia’s nuclear saber rattling amid the war in Ukraine and the Kremlin’s fondness for provocations such as sending explosive-laden drones into NATO airspace.

In October, Trump appropriately proposed that a trilateral summit include a discussion of nuclear arms control. China has long resisted engaging in arms control talks, arguing that it should not have to limit its nuclear program when its arsenal is much smaller than that of the United States and Russia. But Beijing is rapidly expanding and modernizing its nuclear arsenal. The Pentagon has projected that China will possess more than 1,000 operational warheads by 2030, putting it in nearly the same league as the United States and Russia, which have stockpiles of roughly 1,800 operational warheads each. With the uncertain trajectory of U.S.-Chinese relations, instability on the Korean Peninsula, and China’s tense relationship with Taiwan heightening the risk of conflict in East Asia, Beijing must be part of high-level nuclear arms control discussions.

Realistically, Trump can do little more than start this conversation and invite Xi and Putin to continue it in future trilateral negotiations led by teams of diplomats and arms control experts. In future talks, the three countries should discuss not only caps on nuclear weapons stockpiles but also military-to-military contacts, crisis management protocols, notifications of military exercises, counterproliferation, and other measures that can contribute to strategic stability.

Trump could add a host of other topics to the summit’s agenda, including trade, energy security, and AI regulation, but he should avoid a wide-ranging conversation that goes nowhere. He might, however, notch an easy win by proposing a three-way initiative in an area in which cooperation is relatively straightforward—for example, a joint medical mission to the Democratic Republic of the Congo to help fight Ebola or a joint aid operation to deliver humanitarian relief to Gaza or Yemen. A concrete, public effort along these lines would show that trilateral dialogue can yield viable cooperation.

Yet Trump should guard against being so eager for a win that he lets Xi and Putin take advantage of him. The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit takes place only two weeks after American voters dealt Trump and his party a historic rebuke in the U.S. midterm elections. The notoriously thin-skinned president could be tempted to make concessions to Beijing and Moscow to score a foreign policy breakthrough. He must avoid making a behind-closed-doors compromise on Ukraine that he then tries to force on Kyiv and its European supporters. Similarly, he must hold the line on Taiwan even as he seeks Xi’s help on North Korea, Ukraine, and Iran. Otherwise, Trump will leave the trilateral meeting confirming the worst fears of those who foresaw a new Yalta in the making. Capitulating to Xi and Putin would alienate Washington’s allies, project American weakness, and erode global deterrence.

Trump must also carefully manage the optics of a trilateral summit. Putin is an indicted war criminal who continues to prosecute a ruthless war of aggression against Ukraine. It is far too soon to let him out of the penalty box. And although Washington is right to seek a good working relationship with Beijing, it is time for Trump’s “truly great friendship” with Xi to bear fruit. Trump must approach talks with sobriety and pragmatism, seeking to demonstrate that Washington, Beijing, and Moscow can work together to help promote global peace and stability.

A summit next week will not usher in a new era of great-power collaboration anytime soon. It might not even yield concrete deliverables. Still, the meeting would set a valuable precedent. Simply by sitting down with Xi and Putin, Trump would begin a much-needed trilateral conversation.

#5889. These two voter turnout trends explain what happened across the South in the election

Sen.-elect Scott Colom, D-Miss., speaks during a victory rally on Nov. 11 in Tougaloo, Mississippi. Brandon Bell/Getty Images hide caption

Democratic victories in the South this year were driven just as much by who showed up to vote as who stayed home, according to an NPR analysis of turnout data.

Midterm elections normally have lower voter participation than presidential contests. But from Louisiana to the Carolinas, counties that voted for former Vice President Kamala Harris in 2024 came closer to matching — and in some cases exceeding — presidential turnout. At the same time, counties won by President Trump saw a much sharper decline in turnout and fewer Republican votes.

In six southern states — Louisiana, Mississippi, Alabama, Georgia, South Carolina and North Carolina — turnout dropped by 19% in Harris-won counties compared to 26% in Trump-won counties. (In Louisiana, counties are called parishes.)

Across the South, the six states analyzed swung an average of 22 points towards Democrats, larger than the national swing of about 14.5 points.

This differential turnout was a key factor in Democrats flipping Senate seats in North Carolina, South Carolina and Mississippi, and contributed to the closer-than-expected margin in Louisiana's Senate race.

In Mississippi, Democrat Scott Colom outperformed Harris, earning roughly 8,100 more votes than she did in 2024. Colom's margin over incumbent Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith is just over 5,600 votes as final ballots are being tallied.

Democratic Senate nominees in those six states earned more raw votes than Harris in 170 counties. On the flip side, the Republican nominees garnered hundreds of thousands fewer votes than Trump.

In special elections and primaries leading up to the midterms, voting data and polls showed a surge in Black voter participation, spurred in part by a Supreme Court decision that gutted part of the Voting Rights Act.

The Louisiana v. Callais decision led to changed congressional maps across the South, including Tennessee, Alabama, Louisiana and North Carolina. After redrawing boundary lines in a way that aimed to elect an 11-3 Republican majority delegation, North Carolina Democrats are now on track to hold an 11-3 majority.

Majority-Black counties overall had a higher voter turnout with about 85% compared to 76% for white, non-Hispanic majority counties. Sixteen majority-Black counties had more Democratic votes than during the 2024 election, with 12 of them in Mississippi. Clay County, Miss., had the largest voter turnout increase — by 17 points from 2024 to 2026.

Beyond majority-Black turnout, Democratic candidates in these southern states benefitted from a smaller drop in turnout from the 2024 election.

In Georgia, Democratic Sen. Jon Ossoff finished with roughly 79,000 fewer votes than former Vice President Harris won in the state two years prior, despite there being more than a million fewer total votes overall.

All 159 counties in Georgia shifted toward Democrats. Ossoff flipped a dozen of them, including suburban Fayette County, on the way to a 14-point victory.

South Carolina had some of the largest Democratic voter turnout, with Greenville, Spartanburg and Lexington counties having the largest increases in raw Democratic votes from the 2024 election. Greenville, which backed Trump by 20 points two years ago, flipped to support Sen.-elect Annie Andrews by about 2 points.

Nowhere was the Republican dropoff more stark than in North Carolina, where former Gov. Roy Cooper flipped the Senate race by a 20-point margin. Former Republican National Committee Chair Michael Whatley earned 1.25 million fewer votes than Trump did when he carried the state in 2024, a roughly 43% drop in GOP support.

No county or parish across these southern states came close to matching Trump 2024 vote totals. The lowest dropoff came in Greene County, Ga., with 83%, the only one of 518 counties where Republicans earned more than 80% of Trump's 2024 raw votes.

The largest dropoff came in North Carolina, which saw only two-thirds of the Republican votes they did in 2024. Wake and Mecklenburg counties, home to the two largest cities — Raleigh and Charlotte — saw the biggest drops, with about 100,000 and 90,000 fewer GOP voters respectively.

Atlanta and its suburbs also saw a notable decline in support for Republicans, with Fulton, Gwinnett, Cobb, DeKalb and Cherokee counties losing between 35,000 and 77,000 GOP votes each. Democrats saw smaller losses in those areas, with slight gains in votes for Gwinnett and Cherokee counties.

#5886. These swing voters have low expectations for divided government

People vote at a Charlotte polling location on Election Day. A.M. Stewart/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

Key voters in North Carolina are pessimistic the blue wave flipping control of Congress next year will solve their economic woes.

NPR observed two online focus groups on Tuesday night with 14 voters in North Carolina who voted for Joe Biden in 2020 and then President Trump in 2024. Twelve said they have pretty low expectations for what Democrats can achieve with Trump still in the White House.

"I think realistically, it just means nothing's going to happen for the next two years," said 32-year-old self-described independent voter Steven, who, like all participants, took part under the condition his last name would not be used.

"President Trump would veto anything that comes to his desk, and the House and Senate majorities are not veto proof either, so literally nothing will happen for the next two years," he added.

In these groups — part of the Swing Voter Project, held by messaging firm Engagious and market research recruiting firm Nelson Recruiting — 10 people identified as independents, three as Republicans and one as a Democrat.

Almost all these voters said they disapprove overall of the job Trump is doing. Many said they felt Democrats' big wins across the country were a sign that voters "want change."

Yet the participants were largely pessimistic that Congress would be able to effect change, once Democrats have control of both the House and Senate come January.

Rich Thau, president of Engagious, moderated these groups. He said these voters chiefly expect that Trump will be a major obstacle and won't give Democrats the ability to get anything done.

"And the Democrats themselves," Thau added to NPR, "[the voters] think that they're going to be sort of all over the place, that they're going to maybe focus too much on investigations. … But in terms of actually helping them do the one thing that they desperately want, which is to bring their prices down and make their lives more affordable, they are definitely dubious."

Focus groups are not scientifically significant like polls. Instead, they provide a snapshot of what small groups of voters are thinking.

In this year's midterms, the Voter Poll, conducted by SSRS, found that more than half of voters said government corruption was "crucial" to their vote for Congress. That was a bit higher than the share of voters who said cost of living was "crucial."

But this small group of key voters — swing voters in a battleground state — doesn't want to see accountability for the Trump administration eclipse the economy. They want to see Democrats get rid of tariffs, lower the cost of everyday goods, and stop the war in Iran — which they blame for rising gas prices.

"I'm really concerned they're going to pursue those investigations and such," said Rick, a 63-year-old Republican. "And whether they're true or not, it doesn't really matter because there are people in the middle that are being forgotten and can't afford gas and healthcare."

Deborah, a 56-year-old independent voter, said she watched Democrats try to hold Trump accountable for years, and it hasn't yielded anything yet.

"They haven't found anything on Trump yet, so just let it go and move on," she said. "They haven't been able to prove anything, they just keep trying over and over again, and he's still in office."

"I just think it's wasting taxpayer money," added Kristen, a 38-year-old independent voter.

Steve, a 39-year-old independent, echoed a common sentiment among these voters, which is that investigations largely amount to political brinksmanship. He sees it as the parties "butting heads with each other" each cycle.

"They're going to just keep going back and forth and they're just looking for anything to mess with Trump for," he said.

These voters, however, are no fans of Trump, despite voting for him in the 2024 election. Most said they think Trump will block Democrats' every effort in Congress.

"Trump's not going to want to compromise," said Coronda, a 45-year-old Democrat. "Trump is very set in his ways. He's very stubborn, he's going to yell 'his way or the highway.'"

Steven said he expects Democrats will introduce a lot of bills "mainly for show" once they get into office. But he doesn't expect anything to actually get signed into law.

Trump "doesn't want to be embarrassed and he won't let anything pass even if it's a good thing because he doesn't want to give credit to the Democrats," he said.

Thau said this is why most of these voters are setting their expectations low ahead of what could be some serious gridlock in Washington.

"If you have low expectations, you're not setting yourself up for disappointment," he said. "And I think these voters are not setting themselves up for disappointment. They're going in clear-eyed, expecting President Trump and the Democrats to not get along."

#5883. Hegseth awards medal to soldier who chastised him

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, right, and his wife Jennifer Rauchet attend a ceremony at the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier on National Veterans Day Observance at Arlington National Cemetery in Arlington, Va., Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. Mark Schiefelbein/AP hide caption

Coming out of the bruising the Republicans suffered in the midterms, this week was bearing up to be a bad one for Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth. Many thought he'd be the first in the administration to be fired for his support of the Iran war, which proved unpopular with voters.

Then a member of his own party called for a vote to impeach him and, in an embarrassing incident that went viral, he also got chastised for poor decorum when visiting the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier.

Still, he managed to survive the former – except for Thomas Massie, who sponsored the resolution to impeach him, all other Republicans in the House voted against it – and sought to turn the latter into a PR opportunity.

The day after the impeachment effort, an incident occurred at the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier, one of America's most sacred military sites. It happened when Hegseth, his wife, and staffers went to lay a wreath at the memorial just outside Washington D.C., to mark Veteran's Day on Wednesday.

Someone in the defense secretary's group laughed loudly, prompting a sentinel on duty at Arlington National Cemetery to shout: "All visitors will maintain an atmosphere of silence and respect at all times!"

As the video spread on social media, there was a flurry of condemnation online, with some dubbing Hegseth "the secretary of offense."

Political opponents, including Rep. Gwen Moore (D-Wis.), as well as former GOP congressman Adam Kinzinger, criticized Hegseth and his group.

"Pete Hegseth continues to demonstrate his utter lack of fitness for his office," said Moore on X. "One day after narrowly avoiding impeachment, he shows his contempt for our men and women in uniform by disrespecting the sanctity of the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier."

On Thursday, the embattled Defense secretary tried to regain control of the narrative. He invited the soldier who bellowed at him, Spc. Thomas Iglesias, to the Pentagon, where he honored him with the "Joint Service Commendation Medal for the execution of his duties at the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier on November 11, 2026."

"We love soldiers. We love standards. Well done, Specialist," Hegseth wrote. "Carry on Patriots."

#5869. The Trump administration's voter roll crusade could continue

Election workers process vote-by-mail ballots at the Los Angeles County Ballot Processing Center on Nov. 5. Mario Tama/Getty Images hide caption

In the weeks before Election Day, the Trump administration escalated its hunt for voter fraud and its pressure on election officials — especially in California — to crack down on illegal voting by non-U.S. citizens.

Now, with Election Day come and gone, election officials and civil rights attorneys are preparing for the administration to continue its search for noncitizens on state voter rolls, as well as what they see as intimidation tactics toward election officials.

"It's clear that there is this drive to try to conjure up facts that just don't exist. I don't think that's going to change," James Williams, county executive for California's Santa Clara County, told NPR. "So we fully are prepared to continue to deal with this."

In recent weeks, federal prosecutors announced criminal charges against dozens of people who are accused of voting or registering to vote without being a citizen. Yet compared to overall numbers of voters, noncitizen voting is exceptionally rare. And since summer, the administration has touted what turned out to be grossly inflated numbers of noncitizens on state voter rolls.

In mid-October, federal officials alleged they had found 20,000 "likely" noncitizens on California's voter rolls. The Department of Justice sued the state to try to force election officials not to count ballots of voters whose citizenship had not been verified. A federal judge and a federal appeals court rejected the DOJ's requests, and the U.S. Supreme Court declined to intervene.

In the days leading up to Election Day, with the lawsuit pending, U.S. attorneys in California sent grand jury subpoenas to several counties, asking for records related to how local officials responded to DOJ's demands.

Though DOJ dropped its lawsuit last week, it has not rescinded these subpoenas.

"We have ongoing cases and investigations in various states surrounding voter process," U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche said at a Thursday press conference. "We are actively, and will continue to investigate election integrity cases criminally and civilly, and that wasn't expected to start or stop — no matter what the results of the election were last week."

U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche speaks during a press conference Thursday. Win McNamee/Getty Images hide caption

But recent unfavorable rulings for the Trump administration have created new obstacles for future federal attempts to intervene on state voter list maintenance.

"Do I think they will still keep at this? I do, but there are just a lot of court losses that are going to be really difficult for them to maneuver around," Anna Baldwin, director of voting rights litigation at Campaign Legal Center, told NPR. "They've hit substantial roadblocks because it's a lawless program that Congress has never authorized."

Recent court orders reaffirmed that states have the authority to run their elections and called into question the credibility of the federal government's data on noncitizens.

In an earlier order in October, a different D.C.-based judge vacated the DOJ's project of requesting unredacted voter rolls from states, seeking to compare that voter data to federal databases, and instructing states to remove suspected noncitizens. The ruling found those actions were unlawful and disenfranchised eligible voters. The DOJ has appealed.

The Department of Justice declined to comment on litigation or future plans, but said in a statement, "The Department will continue to enforce federal election laws and will evaluate matters based on the facts and the law."

Federal prosecutors have vowed to continue tracking down noncitizen voting cases, as have some local prosecutors. San Luis Obispo District Attorney Dan Dow is investigating the potential noncitizen voters federal officials flagged in his county. He has so far charged one individual.

"It has always been a crime but I've never had a noncitizen case referred to me. When we got 63 of them, we said, 'We will look at them,'" Dow told NPR. "It is our duty to uphold California law."

Loyola Law School professor Justin Levitt, who was a White House adviser on voting rights in the Biden administration, said he expects "a trickle of actual follow-through on actual cases" of noncitizens voting. "And that is also entirely consistent with everything we knew for decades — which is that this does happen, but it doesn't happen very often."

What remains to be seen is whether the Justice Department will retreat from the criminal threats federal officials have waged against local election officials, or try to escalate those further.

In letters delivered to every California county in October, DOJ officials warned: "If persons knowingly tabulate ballots cast by individuals identified by federal agencies as noncitizens, they risk becoming aiders and abettors to a federal crime."

President Trump added on Truth Social: "They will all go to jail for a long period of time!!!"

In the days before Election Day, officials in Los Angeles County as well as Bay Area counties, including Santa Clara and Sonoma, received subpoenas from their local U.S. attorney's office to turn over records this month.

In Sonoma, the subpoena is for records about an individual the DOJ alleges may have voted in a past election without being a U.S. citizen, as well as records about how county officials responded to DOJ requests to segregate ballots of people on its list of possible noncitizens, according to Sonoma County Counsel Joshua Myers.

The Oct. 21 subpoena sent to Los Angeles County, which was reviewed by NPR, requests records related to how the registrar-recorder's office screens for unlawful voting by noncitizens, as well as records responding to federal correspondence, including the demand to segregate ballots.

The DOJ said it does not comment on alleged subpoenas. But the substance of the subpoenas has raised questions about whether there may be a criminal probe targeted at counties.

Williams of Santa Clara County declined to speak about the subpoenas but said criminal threats against government employees who are trying to lawfully administer elections "strikes at the heart of what it means to live in a free society."

Looking forward, Williams said he is hoping for a way to productively collaborate with the federal government again.

"We should be aligned — federal, state and local government — in facilitating every citizen exercising their right to vote. And of course, we want voter rolls that don't include noncitizens," he said. "There's no disagreement about that."

Another hurdle for the federal government's efforts to publicize purported noncitizens on voter rolls is the inaccuracies in its tallies so far.

Trump claimed in July there were 250,000 noncitizens registered in four states, including in California. But state officials pushed back on the data, and federal officials admitted the earlier numbers were a "ceiling" of potential noncitizens. In mid-October, federal officials had sent California its list of 20,000 "likely" noncitizens registered to vote in the state.

The federal government has never maintained a complete list of U.S. citizens. Trump administration efforts to cobble together citizenship data from various sources have led to naturalized citizens being mistakenly flagged as noncitizens, and data experts have warned the federal government's methodology in California was likely to also lead to false matches.

The weekend before Election Day, a federal judge allowed data experts from University of California Los Angeles' Voting Rights Project to analyze the DOJ's list of 20,000 names. The analysis found 42% did not appear on California's current voter roll and that naturalized citizens were on the list.

Separately, several California counties have noted the lists from DOJ were riddled with duplicates and obvious inaccuracies.

Santa Cruz County's top election official, Tricia Webber, wrote in an email that of the 81 people DOJ had listed as suspected noncitizens registered to vote in her county, "more than half are not registered in Santa Cruz County; some names do not match any person currently registered or previously registered," and some dates of birth "were wildly inaccurate with some dates appearing well into the future like 2044."

Even DOJ attorneys acknowledged in court hearings under questioning from judges that there have been flaws with federal lists of likely noncitizens.

U.S. District Judge David Carter, a Clinton nominee, who was presiding over the federal government's lawsuit against California, had planned to hold proceedings that would assess the accuracy of DOJ's lists. That will no longer happen since the DOJ dropped its lawsuit.

And with Democrats winning control of Congress, lawmakers could hold hearings that probe deeper into how federal officials came up with their list, said David Becker, the executive director for the Center for Election Innovation and Research.

"I think we're going to have some very serious scrutiny of the Department of Homeland Security and their methodology as they assisted the administration in its failed effort to try to demonstrate there was widespread noncitizen voting in this country," Becker said.

#5853. GOP state officials push to revive Trump's mail voting restrictions for 2028 election

An election worker sorts mail-in ballots at the Miami-Dade County Supervisor of Elections Office in Doral for Florida's primary election in August. Lynne Sladky/AP hide caption

Voting for this year's midterm elections just concluded, but Republican officials in a number of states are already looking to shape how votes are cast in 2028.

Twelve GOP state attorneys general are pushing to revive the president's bid for mail-in voting restrictions in time for the 2028 election.

On Thursday, they started the process of appealing a September order by U.S. District Judge Carl Nichols, one of the two judges who blocked the U.S. Postal Service from carrying out a plan released in response to an executive order by President Trump.

The Republican state attorneys general represent Alabama, Florida, Indiana, Kansas, Louisiana, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, Oklahoma, South Carolina, South Dakota and Texas. It's not clear, however, how long some of these states will continue this case, given that Democrats won up and down the ballot in the midterms. In Texas and Kansas, Democratic candidates are set to take control of their states' attorney general's offices in January.

In September, the Supreme Court refused to pause a similar ruling by U.S. District Judge Indira Talwani, who is based in Boston. That order stopped USPS from working on its revised rule for federal ballot mail, but it only applied to this year's midterm elections.

Nichols' preliminary injunction blocks USPS from implementing the plan for all federal elections, including the 2028 race.

In a court filing, Justice Department attorneys confirmed the Trump administration is not appealing Nichols' ruling. The appeal deadline was Thursday.

The blocked rule — which the Postal Service released in late August — would require states to turn over lists of voters' names and addresses in order to have them delivered by USPS.

Nichols, a Washington, D.C.-based judge who was nominated by Trump, found that the mailing agency likely has no power to impose that requirement on states.

"As the Government concedes, the Postal Service could issue (and enforce) the Rule only if Congress gave it the authority to do so, but here no statute grants the Postal Service the power to issue key parts of the Rule," Nichols wrote.

The administration's earlier push to put these new restrictions in place for the midterms alarmed many election officials, who were concerned about last-minute changes causing major disruptions to the tens of millions who regularly vote by mail. Some voters with disabilities and those in places without polling sites, they feared, could have lost the ability to exercise their right to cast a ballot.

A whistleblower report claimed the online system officials would need to use to follow USPS' revised ballot requirements had not been properly tested and could have resulted in many eligible voters not getting their mail-in ballots in time or at all.

While the Supreme Court ultimately stopped implementation of the USPS rule for the midterms, Justices Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas dissented from that ruling.

Fellow conservative Justice Brett Kavanaugh said in a concurring opinion that he believed state and local election officials did not have enough time to "reasonably implement the rule" this time. But Kavanaugh noted that "there is at least a fair prospect that the final rule falls within the Postal Service's statutory authority."

A previous version of this story incorrectly said the deadline for the Justice Department to appeal a court order was Nov. 13. The deadline was Nov. 12.

#5847. Democrat Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins wins race for Alaska governor, replaces a Republican

Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins, a Democrat, has won the race for governor of Alaska and will replace the outgoing Republican. Here he's seen at a polling station at the University of Alaska Anchorage on Election Day. Eric Stone/Alaska Public Media hide caption

Democrat Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins, a former state representative from the small island community of Sitka, won the race for Alaska governor, according to a race call by the Associated Press. At 37, he'll be the youngest governor in state history.

Kreiss-Tomkins will replace outgoing Gov. Mike Dunleavy, a term-limited Republican who has been governor since 2018. The state's last Democratic governor was Tony Knowles, who left office in 2002.

Kreiss-Tomkins, known by his initials JKT, made his "high-energy, bipartisan, problem-solving approach" a centerpiece of his campaign.

He's said he plans to build an administration of Democrats, Republicans and independents and described himself at a debate as a "man of the middle." He was endorsed by three former governors — a Democrat, a Republican and an independent — along with Republican Sen. Lisa Murkowski.

Alaskans have had enough of zero-sum partisan politics, he said in an interview just before voting at a precinct in Anchorage on Election Day.

"I strongly believe people just want things to work, and want people to work together," Kreiss-Tomkins said.

The AP race call came after over a week of counting votes, including those coming in by mail and from remote parts of the state. In the ranked-choice system the state uses, he faced three Republican candidates, including one backed by President Trump. Winning requires getting a majority of the votes.

Voters were also facing a question on the ballot about whether to scrap that unique, ranked-choice system. That proposal failed, according to another AP race call, keeping the system in place.

With Kreiss-Tomkins' win and others in the midterms, Democrats will hold 30 governors' offices across the country, up from 24 now.

Kreiss-Tomkins will take office in a state where voters have loudly complained that things aren't working as they should.

Rising costs and relatively flat state funding have forced schools to close and class sizes to rise. Already-high costs for food, fuel and housing have risen sharply, and a looming shortage of easily accessible natural gas near the state's population centers is threatening to push prices higher. And Alaskans have been migrating out of the state for more than a decade.

The Republicans in the race were Trump-backed business owner Bernadette Wilson, former Anchorage Mayor Dave Bronson and former Alaska Attorney General Treg Taylor.

Kreiss-Tomkins is set to take office at noon Dec. 7.

Eric Stone covers state government for Alaska Public Media.

#5839. Solar and storage companies voice support for federal permitting reform

Hundreds of solar and storage companies have co-signed a letter addressed to Congress in support of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA) (S. 5653).

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), in a press release.

BAAJA was introduced in the Senate on Sept. 30 by Sen. Shelly Moore Capito (R-WV), and co-sponsored by Sen. Mike Lee (R-UT), Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-RI) and Sen. Martin Heinrich (D-NM). The legislation is focused on quickening permitting for a range of projects with federal oversight, many of them related to energy production. BAAJA specifically calls out solar photovoltaic systems, energy storage and on- and offshore wind, but also includes language for fossil fuel sources, such as natural gas, oil and coal.

If passed, the Senate bill would amend sections of the National Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 with the goal of shortening project permitting windows. According to the text, it would change and reduce which projects would need to be reviewed under those acts, such as water quality certifications; shorten the length of review periods for projects; and would not require permits for oil and gas drilling on land not owned by the federal government.

BAAJA would also grant the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission the ability to issue permits for some electricity transmission projects.

According to a SEIA analysis, if BAAJA was passed, solar energy output could increase by 167 GW between 2028 and 2035. The companies that signed this letter to Congress believe that federal permitting practices are outdated, and permitting reform is a solution to lowering energy costs.

“The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern,” the group stated in a press release. “Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.”

BAAJA is currently under review by the Committee on Energy and Natural Resources.

#5830. Australian Plugin Vehicle Market Steady in October — Update

… with a spotlight on the Great Wall Motor ORA 5 rising star.

CleanTechnica readers were probably hoping for the surge to keep surging Down Under. I was. However, it appears to have remained steady. Not the flash in the pan petrolheads were hoping for at least. In September 2026, 108,778 passenger vehicles were sold, including all drivetrains. See the charts here.

Battery electric vehicles (BEVs) achieved a market share of 24.2% (26,287 units), outselling petrol-only and diesel-only vehicles. Add in 10,072 plugin hybrid (PHEV) sales and you get a plugin penetration rate of approximately 34%. PHEV sales are the fastest growing segment in Australia — increasing 122% year on year. For those who care, 17,843 HEVs sold in October (a YoY increase of 20%). I am looking forward to peak HEV, followed by peak PHEV, as we have seen in more mature Scandinavian markets.

Australia’s EV penetration rate (including BEV and PHEV) has hovered in the low to mid 30% for the last few months. Not sure what the future holds, but even just maintaining this rate for a few months will make a difference. Nothing beats lived experience. My neighbours are now asking about how much it costs to fill up. One came for coffee yesterday and stayed to go for a drive in our new Model Y. He was very impressed with the tech, the finish, and above all the running costs. He confided that he had just filled up his wife’s Honda Civic — AU$82 — and his Isuzu D Max — AU$121.

On a recent shopping expedition, we counted 10 electric cars in the car park, out of 50 vehicles. Not just Tesla and BYD, but also Zeekr, Geely, the GWM ORA 5, and a Deepal. Battery electric vehicles are making their presence felt. But not all my friends can see them. They can spot Tesla and BYD vehicles, but they tell me they don’t see any other EVs on the road. Yes, we have tried to educate them, but it is not working just yet. “See if it has an exhaust pipe. Does it have a sealed front?”

One car that I am seeing in ever increasing numbers is the refreshed Great Wall Motors ORA — now in an SUV body and a much cheaper price. When it launched back in 2023, I expected great things (see our review here). Sadly, the Funky Cat (as it is known in China) did not live up to expectations. It was the cheapest EV in Australia at the time (AU$46,000) but couldn’t compete with the BYD Dolphin and the MG 4. Now, relaunched as an SUV and with a more appropriate price (AU$34,000 — scratch that, just got an email that knocks another grand off the price), I am seeing them everywhere — 6 on the highway just yesterday.

So, on to the best-selling BEVs on the Australian market in October 2026:

Pretty much the same as last month. Obviously, BYD was the best-selling brand, closely followed by Tesla. BYD has six cars among the top twenty BEVs. There are no cars from Korean, Japanese, or European carmakers in the top ten BEV numbers. When all powertrains are considered, BYD is the second highest selling brand behind Toyota in the Australian market. There seems to be some talk in the auto news about Tesla and BYD being considered “legacy” brands — who would have thought?

Expect big things from Geely as it aggressively expands throughout Australia. Geely recently announced that it has opened 100 dealerships over the past 18 months. Its EX5 sales figures are putting pressure on the competition in the medium-SUV-segment Mazda CX-5, Hyundai Tucson, Mitsubishi Outlander, and Nissan X-Trail.

Utes still dominate the Australian market. When a compelling BEV offering becomes available, the top ten may change again. I was excited to see the KGM Musso BEV ute, but it has failed to gain traction. Only 269 have sold in the past few months. The Toyota BEV Hilux only has 14 units in the country — likely to be demonstrators. PHEV utes seem to be doing much better, led by the BYD Shark. Over 15,000 have sold so far this year.

Now for some newsy titbits: Dealers for established brands are calling for a temporary ban on new car brands (read Chinese made cars) entering Australia, as they cannot compete. The boss of the Australian Automotive Dealer Association says the industry has reached a crisis point, with the last quarter delivering the first loss since the global financial crisis. Chinese brands now own 36% of new car sales.

“This is a clear warning that the commercial settings underpinning our industry are becoming unsustainable,” Mr Voortman said. He speculates on the possible risks to consumers: “… the influx of new brands also puts consumers at risk, as it can lead to rapid depreciation of vehicles, a lag in parts supply, longer repair times, and orphaned vehicles should those companies choose to leave the country.”

Though, what we are seeing in Australia is that legacy brands, mainly European, are the ones leaving the country.

The FUD continues as misconceptions and outdated information are rolled out as facts. If you want to revisit these arguments, check this out. Trigger warning: some of you may wish to yell at the screen. The author twists himself in knots trying to make the argument that the wrong Australians are buying electric vehicles. I would contend that the right people are buying EVs — those commuting from the outer suburbs of our sprawling cities. Data shows that it is these people who are behind the EV sales boom we are currently experiencing, and these are the ones benefitting most from not paying inflated petrol costs caused by Iran’s unwillingness to admit defeat and grovel at the feet of the orange one. Please excuse my sarcasm. All of this is documented in the 10th annual Electric Vehicle Council Report. We reviewed this report here.

Despite the desperate attempt to promulgate fear of “these here new-fangled doodads” — the creation of doubt and uncertainly — every month shows how intelligent the average consumer is as they seek a better, electrified, and brighter future.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5829. A review of ‘Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV’

By Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza | November 10, 2026

Was today’s modern solar industry inspired by off-grid California-living, marijuana-growing, Grateful Dead-loving hippies? That’s the main question explored in “Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV,” a new documentary that recently premiered in Grass Valley, California.

The entertaining documentary traces the history, colorful characters, off-grid homeowners, solar educators and the entrepreneurs who embraced solar photovoltaic technology for vastly different reasons.

Shot on a shoestring budget with volunteers, “Solar Roots” may not have the polished production of a Hollywood documentary, but it is an untold story about the people and early solar and technology that led to the modern solar industry.

Since 2009, when I first started in the solar industry, I had heard jokes and anecdotes about the marijuana-growing hippies who sparked the residential solar industry. Until “Solar Roots,” no one had ever set up a camera to talk to those early solar pioneers and document why rural America became a hub of off-grid solar innovation.

Enter Jeff Spies, a longtime solar veteran and the documentary’s executive producer. In 2015, Spies organized a weekend party for the off-grid solar pioneers of Northern California, where he started his career in the mid-2000s. After hearing so many colorful stories about why these rural homesteaders bought $14/watt solar panels and started new solar installation and distribution businesses, Spies enlisted another solar industry veteran, Jason Vetterli, to travel to Southern California, Arizona, Oregon, Colorado and Wisconsin to capture these unique and personal stories on video.

This isn’t the first documentary about the solar industry or its challenges, and to be clear, Vetterli and Spies’ narrative is not about creating “the solar biz.” Truly, it’s about a particular tribe of solar early adopters. Through interviews, we learn about the 1970s, ’80s and ’90s counterculture movement and the people who stumbled into solar businesses out of necessity and good timing.

Listening to these stories, you quickly understand that none of these solar pioneers intended to get into the solar business to make millions or solve climate change. Their real motivations were tied to maintaining their independence from cities while having enough electricity to keep the lights on and the music playing while raising a family. Beyond the home applications, solar became a new water-pumping solution that supported the pioneers’ cash-only “agricultural” businesses.

Vetterli sets the film’s tone and theme with the first scene. Instead of fading in to a utility solar farm or suburban residential roofs, the film opens with a young hippie using a wire to connect a single 35-watt ARCO solar panel to a car battery that powers an old cassette boombox. He then inserts a Grateful Dead tape and presses play.

The Grateful Dead-inspired musical score, composed by Midnight North, a band whose members include another solar veteran, TJ Kanczuzewski, appropriately plays under the rest of the film’s narrative and interviews.

After a brief solar technology history lesson, Spies interviews the solar pioneers about the roots of their solar PV fascination, how they came to realize that solar could grow and impact their lives and their off-grid movement.

This realization wouldn’t have happened without the back-to-the-land movement. Buying land was inexpensive but getting a utility to build a power line to DIY houses in rural California cost millions. While living without electricity, we learn about their lives before and after PV.

Before PV, the pioneers had to jerry-rig their pickup trucks’ batteries to listen to music and use dangerous kerosene lanterns for light. Gasoline generators were loud, costly, required maintenance and were contrary to the quiet, remote lifestyle they wanted. After PV (and lead-acid batteries), the solar pioneers could enjoy light, music, small DC refrigerators for cold beer and fresh food, 12-volt TVs and other DC devices that were designed for camping.

After implementing solar PV into their own homesteads, the pioneers wanted to share their find with their neighbors. It was that desire to share the off-grid solar PV love that led to the early solar distributor businesses, publications and training centers — not a desire to make millions.

As businesses grew, cultures clashed. We hear the story of when corporate solar salespeople traveled to Humboldt County in three-piece suits to meet their best customers who are wearing T-shirts and jeans and carry the scent and cash of a “weedpreneur.”

While the majority of the film is about the people who wanted an off-grid lifestyle, Vetterli and Spies also interview the late 1990s PV code and policy pioneers. These solar people also wore suit, but they also saw grid-tied potential to make solar mainstream.

“Solar Roots” does a great job covering this off-grid to grid-tied transition, but the film only covers a fraction of the stories and people who inspired the multi-gigawatt and global solar industry that exists today, and that’s O.K. A single two-hour documentary could never cover all of the world’s solar pioneers, but we’re fortunate to have met these particular solar people. They may not have started the residential solar industry, but they certainly contributed to it.

Spies and Vetterli plan to release the entire documentary online in the coming months and have set up a mailing list at www.solar-roots.com for people to receive updates.

Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza is a former film and TV writer (Stargate SG-1, Dharma & Greg, The Dead Zone) and current chief solar public relations geek at UnThink Solar, a solar PR and communications firm.

Tor, thank you for this review. As you know, I was at the Grass Valley premiere too, and the film is a delight. Its account of how solar cells, battery charging, and inverters developed is the best I have seen: clear, even-handed, and grounded in the people who made it happen, with phenomenal historical footage.

Having spent those years at ARCO Solar, I smiled at the opening scene with the 35-watt ARCO module, the car battery, and the Grateful Dead tape. The homesteaders in this film put those early modules to work, solved practical field problems, and showed what solar could make possible.

The path runs from the 12-volt appliances of those early days to today’s 800-volt DC data centers, and it is far from finished. I look forward to sharing the film with students when it is released online, so the next generation can see whose work they are building on.

#5827. A Tribute to Bunker Roy: The Man Who Turned Village Grandmothers into Solar Engineers

Sanjit “Bunker” Roy, who taught illiterate grandmothers to become solar engineers, passed away peacefully this morning at his home in Tilonia, Rajasthan, aged 81.[1] I first encountered his work not in India, but in the rain forests of Borneo.

It was October 2016, and as night fell, the surrounding Borneo tropical rain forest had come alive with noise, dominated by the trill of frogs and the rhythmic pulse of cicadas. I was about to embark on the Sabah study tour of the Emerging Leaders Dialogue Asia (ELDA). Over the next two weeks, we would visit timber plantations, mountain communities recovering from a deadly earthquake, and marine parks.

But undoubtedly the highlight for me was discovering the inspiring story of Barefoot College, which trains village grandmothers to be solar engineers. On our first evening, while enjoying a mini feast of banana fritters, pomelo, and langsat (a bit like lychees but more delicious), we were told about the college’s remarkable partnership with the Sabah Women Entrepreneurs and Professionals Association (SWEPA) in Borneo.

Barefoot College was the brainchild of Bunker Roy, and he was an unlikely revolutionary. Born into privilege in Bengal in 1945, he was educated at the Doon School and St Stephen’s College, Delhi—two of India’s most elite institutions—and was a national squash champion. A conventional career beckoned. Then, during the Bihar famine of the mid-1960s, he saw rural poverty firsthand, and his life changed course.[2] When he told his mother he wanted to live and work in a village, he later joked, she “went into a coma” and didn’t speak to him for a long time.[3]

In 1972, Roy settled in Tilonia, a dusty village in Rajasthan, and founded the Social Work and Research Centre (SWRC), which later became known as Barefoot College. Its rules turned conventional education on its head. Anyone with a university degree was disqualified from studying there, and no degrees were awarded. In 2011, Roy said that nobody at the college earned more than $100 a month. The campus itself was built in 1986 by a dozen local “barefoot architects” who could not read or write, and it went on to win an Aga Khan Award for Architecture. At the college’s night schools, children aged 6 to 14 elect their own prime minister and cabinet.[4]

Roy’s guiding conviction was simple: “There is no urban solution to a rural problem.”[5] He was awarded the Padma Shri, one of India’s highest civilian honors, in 1986, and named one of Time magazine’s 100 most influential people in 2010.[6] He is survived by his wife, Aruna Roy, the former civil servant who led the grassroots movement behind India’s Right to Information Act.[7]

The positive impact of his work has been immense. By the college’s own count at its 50th anniversary in 2022, it had trained 1,708 illiterate and semi-literate rural women from 96 countries as solar engineers, bringing light to more than a million people in over 75,000 households and saving about 45 million liters of kerosene. Its rainwater harvesting had captured some 70 billion liters and brought safe drinking water to more than 1,650 schools. More than 90,000 working children had studied in its night schools, and its barefoot health workers had treated over 300,000 patients.[8]

During our study tour, SWEPA arranged for us to meet one of Barefoot College’s beneficiaries, a so-called solar mama. She was part of the program that takes illiterate grandmothers from poor rural villages and, within six months, trains them at the college in India for their new job. They return to their villages to install and maintain full solar lighting systems. Roy deliberately chose grandmothers because they are rooted in their communities; men and younger people, he found, were more likely to take their new skills and leave for the city.[9] Because the women shared no common language, much of the training was done through sign language and hands-on practice.[10]

Malaysia’s first solar mama—very likely the woman I met that evening—was Tarihing Masanim, from Kampung Sonsogon Magandai, near Kota Marudu in Sabah. She completed her training in India in 2015 and returned to bring electricity to around 100 homes in her village.[11] Roy loved to tell stories like hers. One of his favorites was about a 55-year-old Afghan grandmother who electrified 200 houses and set an Afghan engineering department head straight on the difference between AC and DC.[12]

The story of the solar mamas captures many elements of what it means to harness the power of responsibility to create a more inclusive society and economy. So many barriers are being broken through, as these amazing women challenge stereotypes about age, gender, and rural capacity. Barefoot College shows that sustainable development—including adopting sustainable technologies—is an option for people living with low incomes in remote villages. The Barefoot model empowers people to be agents of their own destiny.

Bunker Roy stands out as an inspiring example of inclusive leadership. His impact, which is so strongly evidenced by the solar engineers program, is as much about restoring dignity to those who are traditionally excluded as it is about developing or transferring practical skills. Roy, who said he had “a very elitist, snobbish, expensive education in India” that “almost destroyed” him, explained:

“So we started the Barefoot College, and we redefined professionalism. Who is a professional? A professional is someone who has a combination of competence, confidence and belief. A water diviner is a professional. A traditional midwife is a professional. A traditional bone setter is a professional. These are professionals all over the world. You find them in any inaccessible village around the world. And we thought that these people should come into the mainstream and show that the knowledge and skills that they have is universal.”[13]

Roy ended that talk with a line often attributed to Mahatma Gandhi, whose principles guided his life’s work: “First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you, and then you win.”[14] For the solar mamas of Barefoot College, and for the more than a million people whose homes they have lit, Bunker Roy won.

This article draws from content in Wayne Visser’s book Thriving: The Breakthrough Movement to Regenerate Nature, Society, and the Economy (New York: Fast Company Press). For more information, see waynevisser.com.

#5825. Oh, So That’s Who’s Buying All Those New Subaru Trailseeker EVs…

After EV sales in the US crashed into a brick wall last year, the Subaru Trailseeker has been one of the few points of light. Newly introduced this year, Trailseeker has racked up a relatively decent sales track record in the crowded SUV field. As for whether or not Subaru can continue to grow its EV sales in the US, the automaker’s elaborate, multi-faceted “Subaru Loves Pets” campaign has emerged as the secret weapon…

The Subaru Loves Pets campaign first appeared 18 years ago, back in 2008. A lot of demographic and cultural water has passed under the bridge since then. More so than in past generations, pets have taken on the role of family members equal to, if not superior to, anyone else in the household. And if that’s the case, shouldn’t pets have their own seatbelts, too?

Now Subaru’s longtime relationship with the pet-centered population is beginning to muscle into EV territory. One indication of a connection between pet and EV preferences surfaced in October, when Subaru released a new report indicating that pets wield a noticeable influence on purchasing  preferences in the US auto market overall.

Commissioned from the firm Talker Research, the report is based on interviews with 2,000 and pet owners in the US. Of that cohort, a resounding majority of 73% said they are more likely to drive for the holidays, specifically in order to travel with their pet.

“As the holidays approach, more than two-thirds (68%) said their pet will affect their travel plans this year, and nearly half (45%) have turned down travel opportunities that couldn’t accommodate their pet,” Subaru adds.

Households with pets also have distinct priorities for a vehicle. “Plenty of room between pets, passengers, and luggage tops the list of features pet owners say matter when traveling with an animal,” Suburu reports.

“More than one in four (26%) prioritize cargo space for crates, carriers, beds, or gear. Among hybrid and EV drivers, that climbs to 35%,” the automaker further notes.

If you missed the money quote, it’s that thing about 35% of hybrid and EV drivers prioritizing space for pet gear, compared to just 26% of drivers overall. In addition to the traveling-with-pets angle, that generational difference also reflects a sharp generational divergence in attitudes towards EVs.

“Electric vehicles have become a political flashpoint in Washington. But new national polling finds the biggest divide among potential EV buyers isn’t political. It’s generational,” reports the advocacy organization ZETA (the Zero Emission Transportation Association) in September.

“Seventy-five percent of adults ages 18–34 and 72% of adults ages 35–49 say they are likely to purchase or lease a new EV within the next five years,” ZETA elaborates. “Among adults ages 50–64, that number falls to 50%. Among those 65 and older, it drops to just 23%, a striking 52-point gap between the youngest and oldest Americans.”

Now let’s take another look at the Subaru survey. Talker Research found that 75% of Gen Z and 70% of millennials traveled with a pet within the past five years, compared with just 58% of Gen X and only 40% of baby boomers (looking at you, Mitt Romney).

A similar generational difference surfaced in other areas of the survey:

Younger pet owners are also more likely to say they plan travel around their pet, with 42% of Gen Z and millennials doing so compared with 27% of Gen X and boomers.

Among pet owners who haven’t yet road-tripped with their pet, 74% of Gen Z and millennials would consider it, compared with 54% of Gen X and boomers.

Subaru pulled back on its overall electrification plan for the US market after last year’s EV sales crash, but it did follow through on the new Trailseeker, offered for sale on a limited basis in January of this year. Volume deliveries to dealers began in April of this year, and in May the Trailseeker racked up 1,074 in sales. The number is vanishingly small compared to industry leader Tesla but a promising start in the challenging US market. By August, Trailseeker had a total of almost 5,000 in sales for the year to its credit, rising to 6,006 sold by the end of October.

All else being equal, recognition from Wards Auto may help keep the momentum going. On October 13, Subaru announced that the Trailseeker earned a slot in the 2026 Wards 10 Best Engines & Propulsion Systems list.

“For 2026, Wards evaluated 26 powertrains from returning winners as well as all-new or significantly updated cars, trucks, or SUVs. They were evaluated on several criteria, including power, torque, refinement, efficiency, and innovation,” Subaru notes.

“Its 375 horsepower combined with immediate electric powertrain response always satisfies. Its range estimates also hold up well in real-world driving, interstate and all. Yet Subaru keeps the price completely reasonable given such capability,” said Robin Warner, who manages the 10 Best Engines & Propulsion Systems program.

By “completely reasonable,” Warner means the Trailseeker’s starting MSRP of $39,995 and its estimated range of 281 miles. According to Subaru, the Trailseeker can go from 10% to 80% charged in as little as 28 minutes, with up to 150 kW on a DC-fast charger.

“With an onboard battery preconditioning system, enabled either by navigating to a charging destination or by the driver via touchscreen controls, the Trailseeker can charge its battery in cold climates — as low as 14 degrees Fahrenheit — at speeds comparable to those in normal weather conditions,” Subaru also advises.

In addition to the seal of approval from Wards, on October 29 Subaru announced that Trailseeker earned the “Car of the Year” award in the 2027 TopGear.com US Car Awards program

The automaker cites Top Gear US Correspondent Alex Kalogiannis, who was particularly enthused about the vehicle’s off-road performance. ““Off the beaten path, the set-and-forget X-MODE figures out all the tricky terrain stuff as you trundle through the bush, and with about 8.5 inches of ground clearance, it can scale all but the scariest obstacles in your path,” Kalogiannis remarked, somwhat colorfully.

“People still carry a torch for the WRX and its many rally-inspired incarnations, but the heart of Subaru fandom now is its dutiful, rugged, useful wagon – the Trailseeker,” he added.

That remains to be seen. Trailseeker will have to compete for the hearts and minds of Subaru fans against other models in the automaker’s lineup, including gasmobiles. The SUV-styled Outback wagon, for example, clocked 118,470 in sales from January to the end of October this year, and that was not the only Subaru model to hit six figures.

Still, US President Donald Trump has provided Subaru and other automakers with a new opportunity grow their EV sales profile this year. On February 28 the President launched his war against Iran, sending global oil prices on an upwards trajectory and improving the affordability case for EVs regardless of the loss of the $7,500 federal EV tax credit, which Trump and his Republican majority in Congress axed last fall.

Ever since dropping the first bombs on Iran, Trump has repeatedly claimed that the war is over, or almost over, or over any minute. In the latest pronouncement, on  Monday Trump said that multilateral negotiations over a peace settlement in Iran are in a state of “positive momentum toward an end.” And if you believe that…

Photo: Subaru has racked up a fairly respectable EV sales record so far this year for its new Trailseeker SUV (2026 Trailseeker cropped, courtesy of Subaru).

#5824. Senate advances federal energy permitting bill

The Senate is fast tracking legislation that would reduce permitting times for energy projects, but possibly to the detriment of longstanding policies protecting the environment.

The Associated Press reported that the Senate voted 73-25 to advance the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA). The bill was drafted in response to growing electricity costs from sources like grid-connected hyperscale data centers. By reducing permitting times, energy projects, like solar energy or onshore oil drilling, could address rising energy costs.

However, environmental groups consider BAAJA a reckless response to an immediate need for more power. The bill would amend sections of the Natural Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 to shorten review periods for new projects, and reduce which projects actually require reviews under these acts.

“We shouldn’t and don’t need to endanger our wildlife just to save a few dollars on gas. We shouldn’t and don’t need to weaken the rules that protect our water from pollution in order to build more clean energy. We shouldn’t facilitate more global-warming air pollution. And we definitely shouldn’t be taking away the public’s right to weigh in on projects that can ruin their environment, make them sick and lessen their quality of life,” said Lisa Frank, executive director of Environment America.

Despite the bill’s threat to environmental measures, the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA) has voiced its support for BAAJA, as have hundreds of companies in the solar and energy storage industry. More than 300 groups co-signed a letter addressed to Congress yesterday imploring the House pass this bill.

“Americans urgently need lower energy costs, a better electric grid and more nergy made in the U.S.A. This bipartisan legislation delivers all three,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of SEIA.

SEIA conducted an analysis that claims passing BAAJA could spur 167 GW of new solar energy on the grid between 2028 and 2035, while curbing the country’s CO2 emissions by 20%.

“Updating and expediting transmission and clean energy permitting is essential to delivering lower costs, reduced air, water and climate pollution, protecting communities and expanding the grid for the American people,” said Loren Blackford, executive director of Sierra Club, in a press statement on Sunday. “But rushing legislation that erodes Tribal sovereignty and people’s right to have a say in what is built in their communities, abandons clean water safeguards and wildlife protections, while further enabling a rogue president that is waging a war on clean energy and our public lands is not an acceptable outcome.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.
