#5822. Norway’s Electric Snowplow Worked. Sweden Added Hydrogen Anyway.

Norway has already demonstrated battery-electric snowplowing on one of its toughest mountain highways. A Scania 45 R covered roughly 7,000 km on Hardangervidda, plowing, grading and spreading sand through a Norwegian winter. Its performance exceeded expectations. Meanwhile, Sweden has unveiled a hydrogen snowplow that is, in fact, another large battery-electric truck, with a fuel cell and hydrogen tanks added for extra range. The engineering question is why that second energy system is worth having when batteries are already doing the work, and when a conventional generator running on renewable diesel could offer a simpler, potentially cheaper route to occasional extended operation.

The Swedish vehicle has to compete with more than diesel snowplows. My full TFIE Strategy Briefing examines Norway’s measured electric operation, China’s expanding snow-removal equipment market and the additional costs hydrogen must justify.

Sweden’s project brings together Scania, MaserFrakt and Peab. The vehicle carries a 356 kWh rechargeable battery and 56 kg of hydrogen. The fuel cell generates electricity to extend the truck’s operating time between charging sessions. That gives the vehicle two energy-storage systems, plus the equipment to convert hydrogen into electricity. It will still need electric charging, and it will also need hydrogen production, delivery, storage and refueling.

Norway’s experience makes the starting point uncomfortable for hydrogen advocates. The Norwegian Public Roads Administration’s 2025 trial demonstrated that a production-based heavy electric truck could do demanding winter-maintenance work. Its measured consumption averaged approximately 2.02 kWh/km, with around six and a half hours of operating endurance under evaluated mixed-duty conditions. The trial identified shortcomings in charging compatibility and payload, but these were engineering and operating problems to resolve, not evidence that battery-electric snowplows were incapable of doing the job.

China is moving toward broader deployment. Municipalities have used smaller electric snow-removal equipment for years, while manufacturers have developed full-sized specialized electric vehicles. Beijing’s Mentougou district awarded contracts for 24 multifunction snow-removal trucks in September 2026. The final chassis specifications have not all been confirmed as battery-electric, but the procurement demonstrates a market moving beyond isolated demonstrations.

China’s much larger battery-electric truck industry is also providing specialized equipment manufacturers with an expanding supply of powertrains, batteries and heavy-duty chassis. Electric snowplows benefit from components developed and manufactured for freight vehicles at much greater volumes.

The Swedish approach looks increasingly difficult to justify against that background. If six or seven hours of productive operation is adequate, a battery-electric truck can already do the work. If longer shifts are required, larger batteries, better charging arrangements, mobile charging equipment or operational changes deserve assessment first. Hydrogen cannot claim the extreme-duty market merely because charging requires planning.

There is another alternative that the hydrogen comparison tends to omit. A conventional generator running on hydrotreated vegetable oil, or HVO, could supply additional electricity when the battery needs support. Diesel generators, fuel tanks and liquid-fuel distribution systems are mature technologies. Major generator manufacturers already approve suitable equipment for HVO, which can use conventional diesel-type storage and handling systems. Integrating a generator into a particular electric truck still requires engineering, certification and emissions controls, but it avoids constructing a hydrogen supply chain for a small fleet of seasonal vehicles.

For some operators, the even easier solution is to retain existing diesel snow-removal equipment and use certified renewable diesel for the relatively few extreme shifts that electric vehicles cannot cover. Copenhagen Airport has already adopted that approach, progressively electrifying its equipment while using HVO in compatible heavy vehicles, including snow-removal machines.

HVO is not unlimited, and its climate benefits depend on feedstock origin and production. Burning it also produces exhaust emissions. These constraints are important, particularly in populated areas. But a relatively small quantity used for exceptional winter duty is a different proposition from trying to replace a large share of road transportation with biofuels.

The hydrogen proposal must beat all these alternatives. It needs to demonstrate that the additional hours gained from the fuel cell are worth the vehicle cost, maintenance, fuel premium and refueling infrastructure. It also needs to outperform the cost of providing those hours with conventional generating equipment or retaining a small reserve fleet.

Snow-removal authorities have an obligation to maintain roads during severe weather. They should purchase equipment that meets that obligation with the lowest defensible whole-system cost and emissions. Norway has shown that batteries can perform demanding winter work, and China’s industrial scale is making specialized electric vehicles more accessible. Sweden’s hydrogen truck is entering a market in which the simpler electric solution is already working.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing analysis examines the operating results, procurement evidence, charging limitations, airport fuel-cell trials and the practical alternatives that make hydrogen’s business case so difficult.

#5820. Introducing the Waymo Accessibility Network UK: Co-Designing the Future of Autonomous Mobility

Today, we’re launching the Waymo Accessibility Network UK to inform the future of autonomous mobility in the UK and enable a more independent future of transport. Whether it’s travelling to work, visiting friends, attending an appointment or simply enjoying everything a place has to offer, everyone deserves the freedom to travel with confidence and independence. Getting around should be about possibility, not barriers, and fully autonomous driving technology can provide more people with independent means of travel.

“Establishing the Waymo Accessibility Network UK is a vital step in ensuring our rollout of autonomous technology in London is inclusive, equitable, and aligned with local needs,” says Chris Ludwick, Waymo Product Management Director. “Working hand-in-hand with our UK partners allows us to refine, test, and deploy solutions that give passengers the freedom of truly independent travel.”

Waymo’s Accessibility Network provides a forum for leading British disability advocates and charities to engage with our team in a consistent, two-way dialogue, ensuring that diverse voices within the UK’s disability community actively shape how our technology is deployed on UK streets. Waymo’s Accessibility Network UK is an extension of our longstanding commitment to inclusive mobility and how we continuously improve our service based on feedback from the disability community. That feedback lives within our product today, helping inform features like audio cues and vehicle-finding tools.

Robin Spinks, Head of Inclusive Design at the Royal National Institute of Blind People (RNIB) says: “RNIB warmly welcomes the Waymo Accessibility Network, and we look forward to contributing alongside the blind and partially sighted community.”

London is a global leader in transport. But as we have been hearing from UK accessibility groups over many years, getting around can still present daily challenges, in particular for disabled and older passengers. For sight impaired Londoners commuting at peak times, finding the right vehicle on a busy street can be difficult. Guide dog users still face refusals in private hire vehicles. For older passengers and people with different physical or cognitive needs, even seemingly small aspects of a journey can make a significant difference. As we shared in our Vision for London, opening travel to all matters – and autonomous driving technology offers the opportunity to rethink some of these experiences.

The Waymo Accessibility Network will work directly with member organisations to support user research and participate in product testing. The feedback from members helps inform the passenger experience of our fleet and build upon Waymo’s current accessible design features, including:

We are honoured to collaborate with an exceptional group of national and community-based organisations that champion inclusion across physical, sensory, and cognitive disabilities. We welcome our 10 inaugural participants: RNIB, Re-Engage, AgeUK, Transport for All, Thomas Pocklington Trust, Guide Dogs UK, Sense, Motability Foundation, National Centre for Accessible Transport and Scope.

“Autonomous technology offers enormous potential to transform independent travel, but it must be shaped by disabled people from day one. By joining the Waymo Accessibility Network UK, NCAT hopes to ensure that disability-centred design sits right at the heart of the UK’s autonomous vehicle future.” says Clive Gilbert, Consortium Board Member at the National Centre for Accessible Transport and Head of Accessible Transport at Policy Connect.

“Thomas Pocklington Trust is delighted to be joining the Accessibility Network, along with other disability stakeholders. It is important that the voices of blind and partially sighted people are heard and that travelling in a Waymo can become a reality as the autonomous vehicle improves its accessibility, safety and other features. We want to build confidence for blind and partially sighted people, with independent travel on all modes of transport,” says Bhavini Makwana, Policy and Campaigns Lead at the Thomas Pocklington Trust.

Technology alone doesn’t remove every barrier. True accessibility depends on the streets we share, the vehicles we ride in, and the passengers we support. While Waymo can offer a major leap forward in independent mobility, we’ll always have more to learn. Bringing Waymo to the UK means doing so in partnership. As we work towards welcoming our first passengers in London, our goal is that Waymo becomes a trusted and valuable addition to the UK’s existing and iconic transport networks, and provides a new mobility option. Through Waymo’s Accessibility Network UK, we are ensuring that the people who experience barriers to mobility have a meaningful voice in shaping its future. We look forward to welcoming more UK organisations to the network as we work together to build safer, more inclusive streets for everyone.

#5818. FERC’s LaCerte weighs in on midterms, PJM and utility monopolies

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“The only way we’re going to get out of the affordability mess is by building more things,” said David LaCerte, a commissioner at the agency.

Utility Dive met with David LaCerte, a member of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission, on Monday at the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners’ annual meeting in San Juan, Puerto Rico. Never shy about expressing his views, we wanted to hear his perspective on how federal energy regulators are dealing with large load interconnection, the PJM Interconnection and keeping electricity affordable.

LaCerte, a Republican, joined FERC in October 2025. In mid-May, the Senate voted to confirm him for a full term that ends in June 2031. Before joining FERC, he held key positions at the U.S. Office of Personnel Management and the U.S. Chemical Safety and Hazard Investigation Board, and was Secretary of the Louisiana Department of Veterans Affairs.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

UTILITY DIVE: How could the midterm results affect FERC?

DAVID LACERTE: I think [the results are] helpful in many ways. Having Americans' voices be heard about these affordability issues, which have been top of mind for us for a long time, is helpful.

And I wish we'd have more [Congressional] oversight committee hearings. I wish we talked about these issues more because the more we can talk about it, the more we can educate our partners on the Hill as to what the real issues are and how we can actually solve them, not from a political sense, but the real-world economic sense. The more those conversations happen, the better. So I welcome more oversight.

From an outside view, it feels like the five FERC commissioners do a pretty good job of talking to each other and reaching consensus.

We can't let ideology get in the way of a lot of our decisions. Now, I think ideology does get in the way of a lot of [other] decisions, especially at the state level, when state politics get inserted — we just don't want a pipeline, or we just want to exclusively run offshore wind. I don't think those results are good for Americans. And if the market demands natural gas in a region, we need to find a way to get it to them.

Do you think if politics weren't involved, we probably wouldn't have offshore wind?

I think that's a legitimate question. Or would we have natural gas generators in Maryland? Right now, we can't have that conversation because politics are making decisions for us. If I had a magic wand, I would let every area of the country have an energy mix that makes economic sense for them.

I think you see a lot of compromise in this permit reform bill. There is hunger for bipartisanship and consensus for both parties to work together on these issues, and I think that's by and large what's on the table.

So the question becomes: We have Democrats coming in and controlling both the Senate and the House. Are we going to be parties that want to work together to actually build stuff, or do we want to just let the status quo be maintained?

The only way we're going to get out of the affordability mess is by building more things. The only way forward is by building more generation, building more transmission. And to do that, we need to clear the deck on roadblocks, which have stymied us for decades. That's what Americans want. That's what Americans deserve.

Are there things that you feel the power sector could be doing better?

There's some areas of the country that are doing better. You see generators being able to be incentivized more in some regions than others.

I think the [PJM Interconnection capacity] price collar has a lot to do with that as well. I think there's a lot of heartburn on the price collar that was implemented in the spring, and what we do with that price collar in the upcoming auction in May as well, because we have to send the right market signals. We can't expect investment to occur if no one's making money. So the right price signals are paramount. We can't live under this price collar forever. But we also need to protect consumers. We can't let the market run unfettered. Our mandate is to have just and reasonable rates and reliable electricity delivered. So we have to balance all these things. But it's an environment where, if we don't send the price signals, new gen will never come.

There's a whole range of issues PJM is dealing with — interconnection, governance, the backstop auction, the capacity auction — so, how are you feeling about how PJM's been responding?

I think PJM has shown more responsiveness as of late. But we still need faster action, and we have a stakeholder process there that has been proven to be detrimental to improvements in PJM. We have an entrenched incumbency in PJM that has, by and large, been successful in their own operations, but we need new entrants. We need new investment, and we need to focus on how governance impacts all of that. These states have opted into PJM, and a lot of their mandates now go through the region. So I do think it's important that the states have an appropriate-sized role in governance.

On cost allocation, formula rates and other issues, how do you think about transparency in terms of people understanding what's happening, what's in their rates?

Transparency has been a big problem in the entire utility industry, and a lot of that is just a product of how monopolies were created within the [Federal Power Act]. And for a while, we got by because the need to more strictly scrutinize the actual rates wasn't there ... because the cost crunch on the average American wasn't felt.

But now we're at a different period of time, and real Americans are struggling to pay their bills, and … we need to require more transparency in every angle, because I do think that the utilities have been incentivized to use some of their own knowledge of how electricity is distributed to their own advantage.

Do you see anything FERC can do around transparency?

I think you see that in some of our orders. We came out and we first required utilities to consider grid enhancing technologies and advanced transmission in their consideration of a new [transmission] line, and then we took that a step further. We said, not only do you have to consider it, you have to show why you didn't choose one of the other options. We need to let the market decide what is best for utilities and ratepayers, and to do that we need more transparency in the entire process.

We have all these new technologies that come on board, and we also have a heavily entrenched transmission lobby who are incentivized to build more stuff because they get that guaranteed rate of return. But if there is an instance where we can use a new technology that brings a resource online faster and cheaper, we need to show that we're being prudent because this is not our money. This is real Americans' money.

We’re at NARUC, so are there areas where states need to be stepping up?

I think we need to get the states more in line with large load retail tariffs. I want [the states] working in directions which dovetail their state-level retail tariffs on large loads to some of the work that we're doing at the federal level. And I think by pairing those two together, you're going to get an environment that protects consumers more. It provides more market certainty, and it gives all the investors the rules of the road.

I'm sure you're also hearing from states on things FERC should be doing better.

The No. 1 thing we'll hear about is just jurisdictional in nature because there has been an evolution — a necessary evolution — on where the line ends on retail services and where it begins on wholesale or interstate services, because as things are becoming more and more interconnected, that line gets blurred.

And also, over the past several decades, the federal courts have, in my opinion, expanded what FERC’s jurisdiction may be. I come from a state background. I’m a big advocate of states making decisions for their backyards. So it's paramount to me that we don't encroach upon that line unless we absolutely have to.

Do you see issues right now where FERC is dealing with the federal-state divide?

I think you can see where we went with the [Department of Energy’s large-load rulemaking request]. We declined a full rulemaking and instead incorporated a balance with each individual [regional transmission organization] coming back to us to show us that [its] large-load tariffs are not unjust and unreasonable. It also creates a dialog. It allows for comment for all those entities, and a region-specific comment, which I think is of paramount importance.

Otherwise, it’s a generic rulemaking, which in today's day and age, with electricity being so dynamic ... it just breeds staleness within some of your regulations. [In] the rulemaking process, the timeline, the comments, the reply comments, the protests and then the court challenges — because you have all your eggs in one basket with a rule.

The PJM Interconnection’s plan for curtailing large loads that don’t have matching new generation intrudes on state jurisdiction and appears to be discriminatory, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission said.

#5816. Solar & Storage Industry Statement on Bipartisan Permitting Reform Bill Passing First Procedural Vote

WASHINGTON D.C. — Following is a statement from Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA) on the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) clearing its first procedural hurdle in the U.S. Senate:

“Americans urgently need lower energy costs, a better electric grid and more energy made in the U.S.A. This bipartisan legislation delivers all three.”

Today, more than 300 solar and storage companies wrote to Congressional leadership urging swift passage of BAAJA. New SEIA analysis shows that BAAJA could unlock 167 gigawatts of additional solar capacity between 2028 and 2035, bringing solar to over 18% of U.S. electricity generation by 2035. The analysis finds that the bill’s reforms could reduce cumulative power sector CO2 emissions by approximately 20% and avoid $54 billion in public health damages.

About SEIA®: The Solar Energy Industries Association® (SEIA) is leading the transformation to a clean energy economy. SEIA works with its 1,000 member companies and other strategic partners to fight for policies that create jobs in every community and shape fair market rules that promote competition and the growth of reliable, low-cost solar power. Founded in 1974, SEIA is the national trade association for the solar and solar + storage industries, building a comprehensive vision for the Solar+ Decade through research, education and advocacy. Visit SEIA online at www.seia.org and follow @SEIA on Twitter, LinkedIn, and Instagram.

#5814. California orders utilities to develop joint DER orchestration framework

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The October order also directed PG&E, SCE and SDG&E to “more explicitly” consider flexible demand, a move one regulator said could reduce interconnection delays.

The order aims to harness DERs to meet local electricity needs, support grid reliability, reduce or defer infrastructure upgrades, and advance California’s electrification and decarbonization goals, the CPUC said.

“Our decision moves the state toward a common framework for the utilities to manage and forecast DERs on our system, and create visibility for the California Independent System Operator,” CPUC Commissioner Darcie Houck said in a statement.

The order gives Pacific Gas & Electric, Southern California Edison and San Diego Gas & Electric one year to develop a joint DER orchestration proposal that meets dozens of CPUC requirements, including “open and interoperable” communication; data sharing; cybersecurity; ratepayer protections; clear customer participation pathways; recognizing locational and temporal value; and avoiding double compensation for DER owners.

The framework will be developed in a working group process that considers input from stakeholders representing ratepayers, energy service providers, community choice aggregators and others. The process will establish “a common set of rules and policies across California” that the utilities would implement over time based on technological readiness, the CPUC said.

The working group must consider a range of technical, market and customer-facing issues related to DER orchestration, including methodologies for assessing cost-effectiveness and cost-benefit balance; DERs’ visibility to distribution system operators; DER aggregators’ role; technology ownership; flexible dispatch; “fair and non-discriminatory participation” for both utility and non-utility DER providers; and performance measurement, monitoring and accountability, according to the CPUC’s order.

Separately, the CPUC ordered utilities to evaluate how flexible electric loads can mitigate grid constraints and advance electrification as DERs become more common on California’s grid. Like standardized DER orchestration, planning scenarios that contemplate load flexibility may also help reduce burdens on ratepayers, the commission said.

Planning scenarios that “more explicitly” account for flexible demand will complement existing demand planning scenarios and focus on how dynamic changes in electric load — from shifting electric vehicle charging times or charging and discharging stationary batteries — could affect the timing, size and need for grid upgrades, the CPUC said.

In a statement, CPUC Commissioner Christine Harada said the order would help California utilities make “better use of the grid we already have,” reduce interconnection delays and avoid costly system upgrades. It would also make it easier for homes and businesses to electrify, she said.

“Our decision gives families and businesses more options to charge a car, electrify a home, or expand operations while reducing the need for costly upgrades. Those options need to be easy to understand and easy to use, people shouldn’t need an engineering degree to benefit,” Harada said.

#5812. Rubix Battery: Storage Designed for Solar Installers

By James Mast, Managing Partner, Rubix Battery

AI and data center growth, extreme weather, and baseload retirement are straining the US electric grid. For homeowners and businesses, reliable access to electricity is increasingly important. That need is driving solar battery adoption. Solar power with battery storage can help keep systems running when grid power is unavailable and give property owners greater control over energy.

Energy storage is becoming an increasingly important part of residential, commercial, and off-grid power systems. In fact, the off-grid power systems market is expected to increase to USD 7.35 billion by the year 2032, while growing at a Compounded Annual Growth Rate (CAGR) of 15.0%. For solar contractors, that growth brings new opportunities, but they will need battery solutions that accommodate different project requirements without adding needless complexity. As more customers consider storage, solar installers need battery solutions that can be configured for different loads, applications, and long-term goals.

Rubix Battery is a U.S.-based energy storage manufacturer specializing in lithium iron phosphate (LiFePO4) solar batteries. Drawing on personal, hands-on experience with solar and off-grid installations, the company develops battery systems with an emphasis on practical installation, scalable capacity, and long-term performance.

Battery specifications are important, but they’re only one part of what makes a battery good for a project. Installation requirements, wiring access, inverter compatibility, and the ability to expand a system all affect how well a product fits.

Rubix Battery knows what makes installation easier because the batteries were designed by installers with hands-on, real-world experience. They bring that knowledge and experience into product design. Features like enclosed wiring compartments, magnetic covers, modular stacking, and closed-loop communication with compatible inverters help simplify system setup. Integrated safety components and designs that eliminate the need for external racking or exposed cabling further streamline installation.

This installer-focused approach also gives contractors flexibility when planning systems for customers with different energy demands. No matter what type of project, both residential and off-grid, Rubix offers two battery platforms to address a range of storage needs while giving customers reliable energy storage that grows as their needs do.

Battery performance over time depends in part on how effectively individual cells remain balanced. Rubix Battery uses Impactiv Balancing™ technology in every battery to help maintain consistent voltage throughout the battery pack.

Unlike traditional passive balancing, which dissipates excess energy from higher-voltage cells as heat, Impactiv Balancing™ transfers energy from higher-voltage cells to lower-voltage cells. The system also incorporates dissipative balancing when a cell reaches a critically high voltage.

With Impactiv Balancing™, Rubix batteries balance cells at up to 20 times the speed of passive balancing and can add up to 30% more battery life. Effective cell balancing is an important consideration when evaluating battery capacity, performance, and long-term reliability for installers and system owners.

Rubix Battery offers two primary product lines: the modular Stack Series and the high-capacity R-Series. Both use LiFePO4 chemistry and include Impactiv Balancing™ technology, closed-loop communication with compatible inverters, and a 10-year warranty.

Rubix Stack Series solar batteries are modular LiFePO4 energy storage systems designed for residential and light commercial applications. Each Rubix Stack Series battery module delivers 5.12kWh of energy storage, scalable up to 32 batteries in parallel for a maximum system capacity of 163.84kWh.

Rubix R-Series solar batteries provide higher-capacity storage for demanding applications, including off-grid, agricultural, and larger energy systems. Each Rubix R-Series battery module delivers 14.336kWh to 16kWh of energy storage across three configurations (24V/560Ah, 48V/280Ah, and 48V/314Ah) and is scalable up to 63 batteries in parallel for a maximum system capacity of 1,008kWh.

Selecting a battery platform involves balancing capacity, installation requirements, and a customer’s long-term energy needs. Rubix Battery’s Stack Series and R-Series give installers two approaches to system design, from modular residential storage to high-capacity off-grid applications.

With LiFePO4 chemistry, Impactiv Balancing™ technology, and features designed around installation realities, Rubix Battery offers battery options for contractors working on a range of solar projects.

Explore the product specifications and connect with the team at Rubix Battery to learn more about its energy storage solutions.

#5811. Joint Letter on Low-Carbon Steel Credits in the Review of EU CO₂ Emission Standards for Cars

Creating predictable demand for genuinely low-carbon steel is urgent. If properly designed, low-carbon steel credits can create a lead market for green steel and secure a future for a green steel industry in Europe.

Many green steel projects have been announced across Europe in recent years. However, some are facing setbacks due to a lack of offtake for low-carbon steel products. Without strong and timely lead market policies, Europe risks losing industrial capacity, jobs and technological leadership.

Creating predictable demand for genuinely low-carbon steel is therefore urgent. If properly designed and unencumbered by additional flexibility or extensions of scope of the car CO₂ emission standards Regulation, low-carbon steel credits can create a lead market for green steel and secure a future for a green steel industry in Europe.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5808. Trump Administration Continues Systematic Dismantling of Public Lands

Proposed Rule Would Shut the Public Out and Allow the Administration to Make Sweeping Decisions About 250 Million Acres of Western Public Lands Behind Closed Doors

Yesterday, the Administration announced a proposed Bureau of Land Management (BLM) rulemaking that would drastically weaken the public’s involvement in land use planning and gut protections for Areas of Critical Environmental Concern (ACECs), one of BLM’s primary conservation designations, across 245 million acres of Western public lands. The rule proposes to stifle the voices of local communities by significantly shortening public comment periods and cutting out robust environmental analysis. These changes would make it easier for the BLM to fast-track the sale, transfer, or use of public lands for oil and gas drilling, mining, data centers, and other industrial or commercial development, all while shutting out the American people. The announcement kicks off a 60 day comment period (January 9th deadline).

The public lands overseen by the BLM contain crucial wildlife corridors, critical habitat, cultural resources and landscapes, watersheds, historic sites, and access to nature and scenery.  The rule threatens to strip vital safeguards for ACECs and environmental review that protect these values, leaving the West’s most sensitive lands vulnerable to irreparable harm.

This aggressive regulatory rollback is the latest chapter in a coordinated, systematic dismantling of America’s public lands. It directly connects to a broader pattern of hostility toward balanced stewardship, which includes the recent rescission of the landmark Public Lands Rule, stated intention to roll back the Roadless Rule and Wilderness policies, the evisceration of National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) standards, and repeated attempts to give away public lands to corporations and developers. This rulemaking contributes to this administration’s singular focus: fast-tracking industrial exploitation and drilling at the expense of our shared heritage, tribal sovereignty and the outdoor recreation economies that sustain rural Western communities.

The proposal comes as Congress debates permitting legislation that would also weaken public participation in decisions affecting shared lands and give more power to development interests.

Planning serves as the foundational blueprints governing public lands for decades; eroding these processes is a profound threat to the long-term health of the West. Making robust environmental review optional and weakening ACEC standards intentionally ties the agency’s hands, preventing it from effectively responding to the compounding threats of climate change, drought, and habitat fragmentation. This action rejects the unmistakable demand from the American public, including local leaders, Tribal representatives, and local elected officials, who have consistently called for public lands to be managed transparently and protected for future generations.

“The Trump Administration is once again plotting to undermine our world-renowned system of shared lands and the public processes that guide their management,” said Jackie Feinberg, Sierra Club’s National Lands Conservation Manager. “Weakening protections and public participation would fast-track the industrialization and privatization of places essential for wildlife, clean water, cultural and historic preservation, and access to nature. We stand firmly against these attempts to cut out the “public” from public lands for the benefit of corporate polluters and profiteers.”

“Gutting transparency, review, and public engagement requirements within the West’s most ecologically sensitive wildlife corridors, watersheds, and cultural resources are the actions of cowards trying to fast-track industrial development at any and all social costs,” said Kara Matsumoto, senior policy director at the Conservation Lands Foundation. “Resource management plans are the blueprints for keeping public lands and waters healthy, and weakening them will weaken local economies and fragile ecosystems. We’ll continue to fight this corporate greed because the overwhelming majority of Americans want more nature protected, not less.”

“The opportunity to review and comment on land management actions keeps the public in public lands.” said Hilary Eisen, federal policy director at Wild Montana. “Resource management plans provide the long-term framework for how vast swaths of public land across the West are managed and it is essential that these plans be crafted with robust public engagement. This proposed rule sidelines the public, as well as local, state, and tribal governments, in BLM land use planning.”

“Resource management planning is meant to provide the public a real say in how BLM manages our shared public lands, and Areas of Critical Environmental Concern are how the agency turns that input into on-the-ground protections for special places and important wildlife habitat,” said Maddy Munson, senior policy and planning specialist at Defenders of Wildlife. “Cutting back public participation and narrowing the agency’s analysis isn’t streamlining, but rather stacking the deck against the places and species that need protection the most.”

“For Nevadans public land is our way of life. Whether we have jobs dependent on mining, grazing, the outdoor industry or just love taking our families camping, these public lands in Nevada are a huge part of who we are. We want to have an active role in determining how these lands are managed and efforts to take the public out of this planning process and weaken protections are just wrong,” said Shaaron Netherton, Executive Director at Friends of Nevada WIlderness. “We need to stand up and keep the public in our public lands.”

“The Trump administration continues to eliminate tools that the Bureau of Land Management has relied on for decades for conserving wildlife habitat, protecting clean water, and working with local communities. The objective is clear: replace the multiple uses and values of our public lands with the singular use of fossil fuel development. That agenda has no footing in Colorado, where 96% percent of Coloradans support conserving public lands and open space and 81% believe oil and gas development is not an acceptable use of public lands,” said Juli Slivka, Senior Director of Policy and Programs at Wilderness Workshop.

“These are the plans that decide whether a valley gets drilled or a trout stream gets protected, and this proposal is built to make those decisions faster and with fewer people in the room,” said Bobby McEnaney, Senior Lands Analyst at NRDC (Natural Resources Defense Council). “Cutting the public’s time to weigh in by two thirds, scrapping the full environmental review, and letting a political appointee invoke an emergency to skip the rest is not modernization. It is a way to lock in drilling and mining decisions before anyone can object. Governors get 30 days and no appeal, Tribal plans drop out of the review entirely, and the agency gives itself the power to shrink or erase existing protected areas. Interior should withdraw this proposal and keep the public’s seat at the table.”

“These lands are called “public lands” for a reason: They are owned by the people of the United States. The Bureau of Land Management’s job is to manage them in the public’s interest, for the common good. By so severely restricting opportunities for the public—including, importantly, Tribal Nations—to be involved in planning for their management, the BLM is depriving all of us of our rights as the owners,” said Michael Painter, Coordinator, Californians for Western Wilderness.

“The Trump administration continues its relentless agenda of handing over public lands to corporations,” said Thomas Delehanty, senior attorney with Earthjustice’s Rocky Mountain Office. “Cutting the public out of land management benefits no one except the extractive industries hellbent on controlling these shared resources for their own private gain. Anyone who values public lands must be loud and clear: these attacks end now.”

“Public lands belong to all Americans, and decisions about their future should be made through transparent processes informed by science, meaningful public participation and consultation with Tribes and others in the community. This proposed rule would give us less of a say in the management of 245 million acres of our shared lands and weaken areas of critical environmental concern, the BLM’s primary tool for protecting important natural and cultural sites. It is another clear attempt to give drilling, mining and other development interests more influence over our public lands, in the same spirit as rescission of the BLM Public Lands Rule—and only days after voters delivered a stinging rebuke to this administration’s agenda of doing favors for oil and gas companies and other powerful corporations. Our public lands deserve better, and so do future generations that will inherit them.” said Michael Carroll, BLM Campaign Director, The Wilderness Society.

“In New Mexico, the BLM planning process gives communities, Pueblos, Tribes, acequias, recreationalists, conservation organizations, ranchers, hunters and anglers a real say in what happens to the 13.5 million acres of public land that the agency manages here,” said Sally Paez, senior staff attorney for New Mexico Wild. “Places like the Greater Chaco landscape, the Permian Basin’s remaining wildlife habitat, and our desert grasslands have protections today because New Mexicans showed up and spoke out during planning. New Mexicans shouldn’t be shut out of decisions about their own public lands.”

“In Oregon, the BLM manages 2.6 million acres of Oregon and California Lands that are home to imperiled wildlife, endemic botanical resources, wild rivers, and climate change-mitigating ancient forests that provide outstanding recreational opportunities and socioeconomic benefits to Oregonians and all Americans,” said Susan Jane Brown, attorney with Silvix Resources. “The public and other stakeholders must have a central role in the science-informed stewardship of these cherished resources, all of which will be placed at risk of existential loss with BLM’s proposed rulemaking.”

“Oregon’s high desert community has modeled a long, successful history of federal planners, tribal interests, ranchers, hunters and anglers, recreationists, and conservationists working together to craft management strategies that balance land use and promote conservation across 12 million acres of our shared public lands,” said Mark Salvo, Senior Conservation Advisor for Oregon Natural Desert Association. “Now the Trump administration is recklessly dismissing these decades of collaboration and compromise, trampling public processes, and sidelining those who have worked to protect these lands and resources for future generations–all to advance a narrow agenda of exploitation at the expense of the public interest.”

“The public lands are the people’s lands and the American people have spoken loud and clear. The Bureau’s proposal to cut public participation, reduce protections for cultural sites and critically important habitat, and shortcut environmental analysis is just one more indication that the Trump Administration isn’t listening and will do anything to silence the public, Tribes, and scientists and ignore the reality of what’s happening on the ground,” said Josh Osher, public policy director for Western Watersheds Project. “Millions of acres of climate-stressed Bureau-managed lands are already being degraded by extractive industries, and we need planning rules that include thorough and honest science-based assessments and as many voices as possible to help improve ecological conditions and resilience.”

This is just the latest attempt from the Trump Administration to dismantle our public lands piece by piece,” said Sandra Schubert, Executive Director of Tuleyome. “The proposed Planning Rule undermines protections that ensure that our public lands and wild forests remain accessible and are used sustainably. Resource management plans are built on science and centuries of management experience. They give local land managers and communities the ability to maintain healthy ecosystems while supporting the diverse ways communities depend on public lands. Undermining planning undermines our public lands”

“Once again, Doug Burgum is doing everything in his power to cut the public out of public lands,” said Aaron Weiss, executive director of the Center for Western Priorities. “This new rule gives political appointees in DC more power to shrink protected places and ignore local voices. Public lands belong to all of us, and good land use planning means giving everyone the chance to weigh in.”

“America’s national parks are supported by the public lands that surround them, and this proposal is another blatant attempt to unravel decades of conservation progress that the public overwhelmingly supports,” said Beau Kiklis, associate director of energy and landscape conservation at the National Parks Conservation Association. “Roughly 80 national park sites share a border with BLM lands, and Areas of Critical Environmental Concern have helped protect Dinosaur National Monument from impacts of oil and gas development, support Joshua Tree’s wildlife by protecting the neighboring Pinto Mountains, and protect ancestral sites near Hovenweep among countless others. This is another attempt to sell off and sell out our public lands, and the public deserves a real say before more is lost.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5807. Colorado nonprofit launches plug-in solar program ahead of new state law

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 10, 2026

A new Colorado nonprofit is looking to bring distributed solar to households that have traditionally been left out of the residential solar market.

Solar Access Colorado, which bills itself as the state’s first organization dedicated to plug-in and balcony solar systems, has launched with pre-orders for small-scale solar kits ahead of the implementation of Colorado House Bill 26-1007, also known as the Improve Customer Use of Distributed Resources bill. The legislation establishes consumer protections for qualifying portable-scale solar devices, including provisions intended to make adoption easier for renters and homeowners living in communities governed by homeowners associations.

The Denver-based organization is positioning plug-in solar as an alternative for residents who cannot install traditional rooftop systems because they rent, live in multifamily buildings or lack suitable roof space.

“Colorado has taken an important step toward expanding access to distributed solar energy,” said Adam Harris, founder of Solar Access Colorado. “We’re launching Solar Access Colorado to help renters, apartment residents and homeowners participate in the clean energy transition with practical, accessible solar solutions.”

HB26-1007 creates a framework for personal, portable-scale solar generation devices. Beginning Jan. 1, 2027, the law will protect qualifying systems from unreasonable restrictions on their installation, use and operation.

The legislation also addresses HOA restrictions by treating qualifying portable-scale solar devices as energy efficiency measures under Colorado’s common-interest-community laws. This could help expand access to solar for residents who previously faced restrictions on making property modifications.

However, the law does not eliminate all installation requirements. Reasonable safety-related restrictions remain permissible, including provisions addressing secure mounting, fire prevention, electrical overload and potential property damage.

Solar Access Colorado plans to help consumers understand the new regulatory landscape and navigate product requirements and installation considerations as the law takes effect. Its initial product offering is designed for balconies, patios, fences and other outdoor spaces, giving residents additional options for generating electricity without committing to a conventional rooftop installation.

Beyond product sales, Solar Access Colorado plans to work with municipalities, housing organizations, property managers and community groups to broaden access to plug-in solar. The nonprofit also intends to prioritize education and outreach to low-income communities and multifamily households.

As states explore policies supporting smaller, customer-sited generation systems, plug-in solar could become another entry point for residential clean energy adoption. Colorado’s upcoming legislation is providing a framework for that market to develop.

Helpful overview, especially the distinction between expanded access and remaining safety rules. One practical detail for Colorado readers: the enacted HB 26-1007 summary says portable-scale solar becomes an energy-efficiency measure on Jan. 1, 2027, while reasonable property restrictions and applicable fire/building-code requirements still remain. PluginForSolar.com tracks those effective dates and system/compatibility questions for readers comparing plug-in solar options.

#5805. No home left behind: Scaling VPP participation while maintaining customer control

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Utilities that redesign demand management programs around how customers actually behave will be the ones to boost participation and system flexibility, writes Uplight’s Gisela Glandt.

Gisela Glandt is vice president of virtual power plants at Uplight, a provider of utility demand management solutions.

Load growth is back, and it isn't slowing down. Electrification, data centers and extreme weather are pushing utilities to find flexible capacity faster than they can build it. Demand-side programs including demand response, virtual power plants, time-varying rates and energy efficiency are one of the few levers that scale quickly and cost-effectively — yet the industry keeps hitting the same wall.

Roughly 80% of customers say they want help managing their energy, but utilities are still seeing enrollment in demand management programs around 10% — despite larger incentives, more innovative technology, and more targeted marketing. What if the problem isn't the customers and instead is the process of asking them to raise their hand?

Opt-in utility program enrollment puts the burden on the customer: to understand the program, check to see if they are eligible, decide it's worth it, and then take action. The benefits and incentives can show up months down the road, and they may even forget about them. Most people don’t make it past enrollment and it’s not because of lack of interest. It simply involves too many steps and too much brain power.

The industry is shifting from programs customers must join to flexibility that's already embedded as a default or activated at the point of sale of a device, with no enrollment required. Where utilities have made this shift, customer participation climbs from 10% into the 70% to 100% range. One Midwestern utility that restructured a program from opt-in to opt-out scaled capacity four to five times faster — and had 70% to 80% of customers participate.

“Opt-out" can be translated as "no choice,” and that couldn’t be further from the truth. A well-designed built-in flexibility program ensures that the customer stays in control. Instead of asking everyone to opt in to participate, it lets anyone choose not to participate at any time, with control that's visible, immediate, and intuitively easy. However, default programs only work when customers are really in control.

Behavioral demand response programs cast the broadest net with no hardware required, no cost of entry, and are open to nearly every energy customer. That's what makes it the most equitable and accessible path in: it reaches the renters and non-device-owners that automated demand response leaves out.

Device manufacturer-based programs work the other angle, unlocking capacity that's already installed and sitting idle: smart thermostats, connected water heaters, electric vehicles and batteries not yet enrolled in anything. When enrollment happens by default at device setup — through OEM partnerships rather than a separate utility sign-up — that latent capacity becomes dispatchable. The shifts are smaller and steadier than a typical demand response event, but can have even greater impact as participation scales exponentially.

For both types of programs, customers have the option to participate and receive benefits, and there are no consequences if they opt out of a particular event or day.

Participation in demand-management programs is an affordability strategy. Every home added to a utility’s flexible base expands the pool of dispatchable megawatts — and the more flexibility utilities can call on, the more generation they can defer or avoid. Deferred capital is downward pressure on rates. With affordability under scrutiny from regulators and customers alike, participation stops being a program metric and becomes an affordability lever.

A low-friction entry program can be an on-ramp for higher value programs. Customers who join a behavioral or OEM-based demand response program are now more informed and better targets for automated demand response, time-varying rates, managed EV charging, energy efficiency and other programs.

The utilities that redesign enrollment around how customers actually behave, with effortless enrollment and complete customer control, will be the ones that close the participation gap. Reliability and affordability increasingly depend on getting this right, with energy customers seeing the most immediate benefits.

#5802. Constellation pursues hybrid capacity contracts alongside nuclear uprates

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The offering gives Constellation “a new way to pair the existing fleet with incremental capacity” by adding batteries, generation and demand response, CEO Joe Dominguez said.

Constellation Energy is pursuing a new kind of bilateral contract with large customers that combines clean baseload power generation — likely nuclear — with other capacity resources such as battery storage, demand response and new generation, executives said this week.

The company is calling the offering “Peak+” and bills it as a simpler, cheaper and more environmentally friendly alternative to new gas-fired generation for hyperscalers and other large power consumers.

“Peak+ is going to be broadly defined as bringing capacity solutions plus existing clean and reliable generation,” Joe Dominguez, Constellation’s chairman, president and CEO, said on a Monday quarterly earnings call with investors and stock analysts.

Constellation is introducing the hybrid contracts as construction delays, cost overruns, interconnection delays and long-term fuel-price uncertainty present material risks for power plant developers, he said. He also pointed to the “political tilt in this last election,” which he said raises the specter of stronger federal regulation on carbon emissions affecting fossil fuel-powered plants.

“Constellation Peak+ gives us a new way to pair the existing fleet with incremental capacity,” he said. “We could add batteries and generation supply at our plants given the vast amount of land that we own around the plants.”

A slide in Constellation’s third-quarter investor presentation showed Peak+ pricing $20/MWh to $50/MWh higher than the “floor” set by the federal production tax credit for nuclear power but lower than the $110/MWh to $130/MWh levelized cost of power from new-build combined-cycle gas turbines in the PJM Interconnection’s eastern region — where much of Constellation’s nuclear capacity sits — by 2032, assuming a $3,000/kW build cost.

The production tax credit pays nuclear generating units up to $15/MWh when revenues fall between $26/MWh and $44.75/MWh, Constellation says.

The company signed its first Peak+ customer for a 20-year agreement for 270 MW of “unit-contingent energy, capacity and attributes from our existing nuclear fleet,” according to a press release. Dominguez declined to identify the customer.

He said he still expects some customers will pursue new large power plants or off-grid solutions. However, pairing “simpler and less risky capacity solutions with our large volume of clean energy is a compelling solution,” he said.

At about 55 GW, the Baltimore-based independent power producer says it has the largest combined fleet of nuclear, natural gas and geothermal generation in the U.S.

About 22 GW of that is nuclear, much of it in the capacity-constrained PJM Interconnection.

The company has announced more than 1 GW of hyperscaler-backed capacity expansions at existing nuclear power plants in recent weeks, including an 890-MW agreement with Google and a 190-MW deal with Amazon.

“I know that there's been some chatter that once we sold the [nuclear power] uprates, our ability to contract the existing fleet would somehow be exhausted — it is not,” Dominguez said.

In addition to the new Peak+ contracts, the company has “not finished exploring uprates at plants,” he said.

Dominguez also said the company’s planned nuclear restart at the site formerly known as Three Mile Island — now renamed the Crane Clean Energy Center — is on track for the second half of 2027 with the first fuel assemblies and transformers are on site.

Separately, Constellation announced another addition to its non-nuclear generating fleet, which grew dramatically in the past year following its $16.4 billion combination with gas-heavy Calpine.

Constellation said in September that it would pay Shell Energy North America $715 million to buy the Rhode Island State Energy Center, a 609-MW combined-cycle gas-fired power plant in the ISO New England wholesale market. RISEC will run on a merchant basis, Constellation said.

#5800. Data centers could lower your electricity bill. Will utilities let them?

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

When sales increase more than costs, rates should decline. But some utilities are using lower historical sales data with updated cost assumptions to “lock in” higher rates, writes Edward Burgess.

Edward Burgess is a partner at Current Energy Group.

The debate over the impacts of new data centers on customers' electricity bills is only intensifying.

On the one hand, many people blame data center companies for triggering new grid costs that others will pay. On the other, data centers hold great promise to lower customer bills.

For the latter, the theory is that, as high-utilization infrastructure users, data centers can absorb a large share of fixed costs that would otherwise be charged to existing customers.

To their credit, nearly 200 data center companies have signed the Ratepayer Protection Pledge, committing to pay their fair share of costs. However, achieving this commitment depends on cooperative action by local utilities to update their rates accordingly.

This raises an important question: Are investor-owned utilities willing to share the benefits of large-load growth with existing customers? Recent evidence suggests otherwise in some cases.

To fully understand this problem, let’s revisit the fundamentals of electricity ratemaking.

New rates are typically proposed by utilities but ultimately approved by state utility regulators. When utilities propose new rates, the core calculation is based on two key inputs: 1) utility costs in the numerator, and 2) utility sales in the denominator.

If the denominator increases more than the numerator (i.e., sales increase more than costs), then customer rates should decline on average.

But in the era of hyperscale load growth, the specifics of these inputs — how they are defined and when relative changes occur — matter a great deal. In several recent cases, utilities have simply neglected to increase kWh sales from new data center load in their rate proposals, while still including related cost increases.

For example, in North Carolina, Duke Energy recently proposed increasing customer rates by over 15% over the next two years.

However, this increase was based on a rate calculation that kept the denominator essentially frozen at 2024 levels — even though Duke had already contracted to add hundreds of MWs of new data centers in the coming years, and included costs triggered by this in its proposal.

Current Energy Group analyzed Duke’s proposal and presented expert testimony to the North Carolina Utilities Commission on behalf of the state’s Attorney General’s office. We found that 54% of the requested rate increase — approximately $1 billion annually — could be erased if kWh sales in the rate formula were simply corrected to reflect Duke’s own planning assumptions.

In fact, correcting just the kWh sales figure and one other key variable — return on equity — was enough to turn the proposed 15% rate increase into a 4% decrease, even with significant new grid investments.

A similar pattern emerged in Missouri, where Evergy Missouri Metro included costs for new gas generation to serve new data centers but did not account for the increased sales to those data centers in its rate calculation. As data center sales ramp up, the utility would see a windfall over the four years the new rates are in effect.

In Louisiana, increased sales from a new Meta data center could trigger an immediate customer refund over the next three years. Instead, Entergy proposed accounting methods to defer that benefit over a 20-year period.

This “one simple trick” of immediately updating kWh sales in the rate formula could save customers billions in the coming years. So why do utility rate proposals keep failing to account for data center load growth?

Some utility analysts might point to arcane explanations, such as the traditional practice of using a “historic test year.” There may be a grain of truth to that, but it is not a convincing argument given widespread concerns about affordability.

Duke, for instance, had the legal ability to propose a forward-looking Multi-Year Rate Plan but chose not to include any load growth in its rate proposal.

A more cynical view is that utilities are seeking to “lock in” higher rates before new data center load fully materializes and the denominator rises. This creates a major windfall for utilities, at least temporarily, as data center load ramps up before rates can catch up.

Traditionally, utilities have complained about “regulatory lag,” which arises when rates are not increased quickly enough to capture rising costs. Now, in the era of hyperscale load growth, we have the opposite problem — a “reverse regulatory lag.” Instead, rates are not being reduced quickly enough to capture rising revenue from large loads.

This problem is exacerbated by another challenge: As utilities slow-walk updates to the denominator (kWh sales), they speed-run updates to the numerator (costs recovered).

Many utilities, including Duke, use accelerated cost recovery mechanisms, such as Construction Work in Progress, to fund new infrastructure for load growth.

This further strains affordability. Infrastructure costs are front-loaded through near-term rate increases, while any rate relief from load growth won’t materialize for years.

Under Duke’s original proposal, customers would pay for the construction of new gas-fired power plants four years before they start operating, and six years before data center load appears in the denominator.

As affordability concerns persist, what solutions should regulators consider?

Whether hyperscale load growth raises or lowers bills depends on how and when the numerator and denominator are updated. Understanding these mechanics and the options for mitigating them will shape how regulators and stakeholders weigh the bill impacts of large load growth.

#5798. BYD’s BEV Sales Soar While Its PHEV Sales Slide

Looking at changes in BYD’s passenger vehicle sales overall, the company saw solid growth in October. Sales were up 11.8%, going from 436,856 in October 2025 to 483,125 in October 2026. However, that overall growth hides the fact that the company’s BEV (full electric vehicle) sales soared while its PHEV (plugin hybrid electric vehicle) sales actually declined a bit.

BYD’s BEV sales rose 40.6% year over year in October! They went from 222,559 in October 2025 to 305,743 last month. Month over month, they rose 11.9%, up from 273,143 in September.

Meanwhile, PHEV sales were on the other side of the coin. They dropped 19.6% year over year, going from 214,297 in October 2025 to 177,382 in October 2026. Month over month, they dropped 3.4%, down from 183,570.

Looking at overall passenger vehicle sales once more, BYD’s sales were up 5.8% month over month, rising from 456,713 in September 2026 to 483,125 in October 2026. The charts really help to show all of these changes. Below are static versions of the embedded charts above.

#5796. Around 400 onshore wind projects await DOD action, official tells court

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Assistant Secretary of Defense Dale Marks told a federal court that the Pentagon doesn’t have enough staff to review permits.

Since the court stayed the wind review pause, DOD said, the office that issues wind project permits — the Military Aviation and Installation Assurance Siting Clearinghouse — has shifted from a “pause on processing-related activity to active assessment of projects.”

American Clean Power Association Vice President of Policy and Regulatory Affairs Tom Vinson disputed this in an Oct. 20 declaration, based in part on “information that ACP members provided to me.”

“Despite the Court’s clear orders, DOD has not resumed review,” Vinson said. “While it may have reported some administrative activity, none of this activity has resulted in any substantive movement through mitigation review.”

He testified that DOD’s delay has threatened the economics of wind projects awaiting permits as developers continue to incur costs as they wait for DOD to move forward.

Vinson said one project has seen turbine delivery pushed back and noted that “equipment delivered after December 31, 2027, will not qualify for the Advanced Manufacturing Credit, which the developer estimates would otherwise provide a benefit of approximately $200,000 per turbine, or roughly $24 million across the project.”

In the Nov. 3 filing, Marks said none of the teams tasked with reviewing wind energy permits “are sized to manage the dramatic influx of applications received in recent years during a period simultaneously characterized by downward pressures on resourcing (manning, funding, and contractor support).”

Contractors can’t execute inherently governmental functions, Marks wrote, so action on the approximately 400 projects in the queue rests “solely” with eight federal employees across DOD.

“In an average year, the [DOD] would execute 13 mitigation agreements, but more recently, this number has hovered around 30,” Marks said.

Plaintiffs’ reply to DOD’s Nov. 3 filing is due Nov. 17.

#5795. Heliene and Caelux combine PV cells and solar glass for a hybrid module

Heliene, a solar modules manufacturer, and Caelux Corporation, a perovskite solar technology company, are starting commercial production of their Hybrid-Tandem solar module.

This module combines perovskite glass from Caelux and silicon N-type cells from Heliene, placing two energy generating sources in a single solar panel. Heliene is producing the modules in its plant in Mountain Iron, Minnesota, with first deliveries slated for 2027.

“One of the advantages of Caelux’s technology is that we can integrate its Energy Producing Glass into our existing manufacturing lines without having to build an entirely new production setup,” said Martin Pochtaruk, CEO and founder of Heliene. “With both companies manufacturing in the U.S., we’re bringing together two fully domestic supply chains to advance what’s possible with American-made solar, besides the fact that partnering with true friends also makes it fun.”

The companies are reporting a 27% efficiency from the Hybrid-Tandem module. Heliene and Caelux desiged this module to have a higher power density in a smaller project footprint.

“Heliene is a true leader in U.S. manufacturing, and we share a belief that when we lead the market, our customers win. Together, we’re commercializing ultra-high-efficiency solar modules that will set a new bar for American-made solar,” said Caelux CEO Scott Graybeal.

Heliene will showcase the Hybrid-Tandem module at RE+ in Las Vegas from Nov. 17 to 19, at booth #W16546.

#5791. BYD Commercial Vehicle Sales Rise 68% in October!

Following up our usual monthly report on BYD’s passenger vehicle sales, let’s have a look at the companies commercial vehicle sales — all electric, of course.

The company’s bus sales were up 62.1% year over year in October, rising from 367 in October 2025 to 600 in October 2026. Month over month, compared to September 2026, sales were actually down 24.1% (from 791 in September), but sales vary quite a lot from month to month in this segment, and 600 is still one of the company’s best months ever for electric bus sales.

In terms of non-bus commercial vehicle sales, they did even better. They rose 69.1% year over year, from 4,483 in October 2025 to 6,443 in October 2026, and they rose 6.4% month over month, from 6,057 in September 2026.

#5789. ConEd joins utilities seeking to loosen New York restrictions on owning generation

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company says reforms would boost renewables and help it achieve resource adequacy and environmental compliance targets.

Consolidated Edison, Inc. continues to plan for major infrastructure upgrades to meet a broad-based rise in electricity demand in New York City and state clean energy targets, according to the company’s regulatory filings and executives.

State law requires the state’s electric sector to reach 70% renewable energy by 2030 and fully decarbonize by 2040, despite amendments passed earlier this year that watered down some aspects of the law to “reflect the importance of feasibility, cost effectiveness and consumer affordability,” Jen Hensley, ConEd’s senior vice president of corporate affairs, said in an annual presentation for investors in October.

The company released third-quarter earnings on Nov. 5 but, as it has in recent quarters, did not hold a quarterly call to discuss its filing.

To boost system reliability while working towards the state’s clean energy goals, Con Edison has asked regulators to approve a new Reliability Asset Dispatch Rights (RADR) program aimed at distribution-level storage systems and extend a key deadline for adding transmission-connected bulk storage systems.

ConEd and its in-state peer companies have also proposed a framework for New York utilities to collectively develop and own up to 1 GW of land-based renewable energy projects annually, Hensley said.

That would mark a significant shift for New York’s restructured electricity landscape, where electric service companies like ConEd’s utilities generally do not own bulk generation and transmission. But it could help keep New York on track to meet its clean energy goals, Hensley said.

“We believe this would provide a long-term cost-effective strategy for supplying clean energy to customers, and we anticipate a white paper on this topic from the New York State Public Service Commission later this year,” she said.

Calls to ease restrictions on utility ownership of generation have grown in restructured U.S. markets with high power prices and development costs, despite pushback from independent power producers.

Earlier this year, for example, New Hampshire passed a law allowing utilities to own individual gas and nuclear generators under 5 MW capacity. Another New Hampshire bill that would have allowed utilities to own nuclear reactors under 300 MW capacity did not become law. The New York utilities’ proposal is more expansive than either.

With or without clearance for its three electric utilities to own generation, ConEd’s transmission subsidiary plans to make a significant investment in downstate New York’s power supply as a minority partner in the Propel NY Energy project.

The $3.26 billion joint venture with the New York Power Authority and other private developers would add three new substations, an underground transition vault and about 90 miles of underground and submarine 138-kV and 345-kV lines across western Long Island, parts of New York City and suburban Westchester County. The partners aim to finish work by mid-2030, pending state and local approvals.

Still, ConEd executives said more transmission, distribution and energy storage investment will be needed to keep the lights on in the coming years.

Consolidated Edison Company of New York, the ConEd utility serving the five boroughs and Westchester County, projects a capacity shortfall of 125 MW in New York City by 2033, rising to 675 MW by 2036 and 2,275 MW by 2045, Matthew Ketschke, CECONY’s president, told investors.

In July, CECONY filed a reliability contingency plan with state regulators that “includes market-based solutions, including procuring bids for additional transmission connected battery storage systems, creating a new distribution connected battery storage framework and issuing [a request for proposals] for non-storage clean and non-emitting technologies,” Ketschke said.

CECONY is “also planning for, but not seeking authorization for, transmission cooling enhancements that could increase transfer capability of generation from outside New York City by 300 megawatts,” he added.

Unlike other regions where near-term electricity demand growth is driven by large data center and industrial loads, ConEd sees more diffuse building and vehicle electrification driving growth in its territory. For example, new buildings’ electric demand requests are 20% to 25% higher than the historical norm for comparable structures, the company said last week.

ConEd did name a handful of new or expanded large-load customers set to come online soon, however: a new soccer stadium and entertainment complex adding 44 MW in Queens; a “construction and modernization” project adding 93 MW at John F. Kennedy International Airport, also in Queens; and an expanded Port Authority of New York and New Jersey bus terminal adding 42 MW in Manhattan.

As demand grows, ConEd utilities look for ways to increase system capacity without adding new substations, which Ketschke framed as a last resort.

“As the network grows beyond the capacity of the substation serving it, we divide the network in two and build a new substation to serve the new network, but not before we exhaust our toolkit of non-wire solutions, including demand management, battery storage and a host of other measures to avoid costly infrastructure investment,” he said.

But some new substations are inevitable. As a whole, ConEd says it will add 28 new electric substations over the next 10 years to the networks overseen by its electric utility companies, CECONY and Orange & Rockland Utilities.

CECONY, which serves the five boroughs and Westchester County, is in line for nine new substations. O&R, whose two electric utilities’ territories straddle the New York-New Jersey border west of the Hudson River, will get 19, the company said. Both projections are unchanged from the previous quarter.

ConEd’s three electric utilities took in a combined $4.4 billion in electricity revenue in the third quarter, up 8.8% year over year, the company said on Thursday. It’s the second consecutive quarter of strong electricity sales performance for ConEd. The company’s electric utilities grew sales revenue by about 10% year over year in the second quarter of 2026.

Major work by regional planners is focused on flexible access to existing transmission. Some say greater emphasis on advanced transmission technologies can quickly and cost-effectively create room for new large loads.
