#5786. Science For The People, Right On!

Today United for the Future launches at COP31 today with a plan to unite 100 million people in peaceful, non-partisan marches in cities worldwide. Their goal is to assemble the largest coordinated climate demonstration in history in order to put climate action back at the top of the political agenda.

Backers of the initiative include Christiana Figueres, former UN climate chief and a key architect of the Paris Agreement; Bill McKibben, author and co-founder of 350.org; Ram Ramanathan of Scripps Institution of Oceanography, UC San Diego; and Sandrine Dixson-Declève, honorary president of the Club of Rome.

Together, they beckon citizens around the world: “Protect our future. Act on the science.” Their call asks governments to stop weakening climate protections and to deliver action that cuts emissions and protects nature in line with science.

Limiting temperature rise to a 1.5 degrees Celsius, or 2.7 degrees Fahrenheit, above the preindustrial levels of the early 1800s was a goal established during the 2015 Paris Agreement. Since then, it’s been a focus of international climate negotiations, and one that’s often been politically contentious.

“For too many people, climate change is deadly,” UN climate chief Simon Stiell said at COP31. “At this COP, nations face a choice: electric or shock. Rewire the global economy for the electric age. Or accept ongoing fossil fuel cost pain for families, firms, and governments.”

Global carbon emissions from fossil fuels are projected to decrease by 0.4% in 2026, driven by expansion of solar and wind power and the Iran conflict, according to new research by the Global Carbon Project.

Then there’s the recent blue tsunami that took place in the US midterm elections, which served as a condemnation of the Trump administration. Historian and activist Rebecca Solnit exclaims, “It was a dam breaking, the dam that held back the will of the people, the people of the world’s most diverse country, the people who believed in their own rights and equality and in the rights and equality of others.”

Now, through promised agendas to increase affordability for consumers, Democrats have a keen opportunity to foreground science as a way to accomplish goals. Senator Bernie Sanders (I-VT) admonishes the new Democratic majority to deliver on its campaign jargon. “No more empty rhetoric. No more telling us how terrible Trump and the Republicans are. No more excuses,” he says. “We now have the votes to pass legislation that will demonstrably improve the lives of millions of working families. And that’s what we must do.”

Sanders insists that the time is now to “act boldly.” Democrats can create millions of good-paying jobs, he continues, “by moving our energy systems away from fossil fuels to energy efficiency and sustainable energy.”

David Fickling outlines in Bloomberg this week that renewables have already made tremendous headway, to the point that “sometimes, change comes at you so fast that you forget to congratulate yourself on what you’ve achieved.”

In 2025 clean energy sources generated nearly the same amount of electricity as the entire world consumed in 1996. About half of that electricity, Fickling continues, was sourced from wind and solar power.

“Instead of concentrating on squeezing the final tons of carbon from electricity,” he offers, “governments need to make abundant clean power cheap enough to drive fossil fuels out of everything else — in particular, electric cars and trucks, home heating systems, and the sort of low-temperature heat used in food and paper manufacturing.”

In that new cohort of leaders is William Lawrence, co-founder of the climate action movement, Sunrise. The Michigan Democrat flipped a House seat and sees clean energy as a way to fulfill campaign promises.

He describes clean energy tax credits as a good place to start.

“It’s a huge issue not just for the climate, but for our energy security,” he states. Climate is as important to energy security as it’s ever been, he tells Claire Brown of the New York Times.” People are experiencing the rising costs of fuel due to the war and volatility around the world. And we can all see the value of being able to have cheap, clean renewable energy that doesn’t depend on the global commodity markets.”

Several scientists and physicians were elected to the US Congress as part of the reinvigoration of science at the federal level. “The importance of having scientists, engineers, and health professionals in national, state, and local political positions cannot be overstated,” relates Deborah D. Stine, the executive director of the President’s Council of Advisers on Science and Technology during the Obama administration. “If the policy question is outside their own expertise, they know how to call on the best experts.”

The incoming scientists represent “a real repudiation of what has been two terrible years for science and public health,” Shaughnessy Naughton, president of 314 Action, chronicles for Alexander Nazaryan of the New York Times.

Where will the US Congress go from here? Rebecca Solnit muses, “What comes after hope, when hope is realized, when hope is no longer necessary?”

Clean energy expert Steve Hanley has an answer to that question. “The guiding star for all policy decisions going forward should be reducing carbon and methane emissions.”

Did you know that electric motors convert 90% of the energy they consume into motion, while an internal combustion engine converts less than 30%? Not everyone is aware of the efficiency of electrifying everything, though. Some scientists are already talking about the important need to draw in a new generation of clean energy advocates among Republicans in Congress and everyday life.

“In almost all circumstances, electrification is a good thing,” says Chris Bowen, Australia’s minister of climate change and energy and COP31’s president of negotiations.

Now we’ll see if Congress can translate political subterfuge into clean energy reality.

Carolyn Fortuna, PhD, is a longtime journalist and researcher for CleanTechnica who frequently writes about climate science, renewable energy, and environmental communication. Since joining the publication in 2016, Fortuna has authored nearly 1,900 articles focusing on ecojustice, clean technology adoption, and the intersection of science and public policy. #5783. The Netherlands Has €231 Million for Cleaner Inland Ships. It Still Needs Electric Freight Corridors.

The Netherlands is preparing to spend €231.2 million over four years helping inland ships replace fossil-fuel propulsion. The first €36.93 million tender opens on November 17, 2026, and battery-electric vessels are among the eligible investments. This is a serious step toward decarbonizing freight on the waterways that link Dutch ports and Europe's industrial heartland. But the programme's most important scoring calculation rewards emissions avoided per euro of propulsion subsidy while excluding separate subsidies for the shore infrastructure on which the ship may depend. That distinction matters when the commercial product is a complete freight service, not an electric vessel sitting beside a quay.

The new Dutch tender is considerably more sophisticated than a technology demonstration fund, yet it leaves an important accounting boundary. My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment follows that boundary from subsidized vessels to working routes, drawing on my open, 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report.

The RVO programme can support battery-electric systems, some hybrid arrangements and other propulsion technologies, together with eligible charging and refueling facilities. Its published explanation prioritizes electrification where practical. That is sensible: electric inland vessels are already operating in the Netherlands, and a subsidy should encourage the pathway that provides the required service at low emissions and acceptable cost.

The scheme also recognizes that a vessel does not operate in isolation. Applications receive points for commercial cooperation, cargo-owner agreements and the quality of supporting infrastructure arrangements. These are material criteria, not decorative promises. They are a welcome correction to programmes that fund novel equipment and assume the market, terminal and utility will somehow arrange everything else.

Yet most of the available points are tied to projected emissions reductions per euro of propulsion support. That is a consistent measure for comparing applications for individual ships. It is not an accounting of the entire investment required to turn a diesel freight route into an electric one. One barge may need access to battery exchange at both ends of its journey, reserve battery inventory, grid capacity, a crane or handling system and a dependable service agreement. Some of those facilities can serve many vessels, making it hard to allocate their cost and value to any single propulsion application.

Zero Emission Services, or ZES, has already shown what putting the pieces together looks like. Its swappable battery containers allow inland vessels to obtain charged energy without each owner purchasing and maintaining every battery required by the route. At Moerdijk, a terminal arrangement combines battery exchange, handling equipment and access to an existing electricity connection. The ZES network is extending beyond isolated demonstrations, although operating sites, planned stations and funded vessel conversions must not be counted as the same thing.

The difference between a capable ship and reliable freight is familiar to ports. Charged batteries must be available at the right time, terminals must handle exchanges without disrupting cargo work, and the energy-service provider needs enough committed traffic to finance its containers and chargers.

The Netherlands deserves credit for recognizing these dependencies. It need not discard vessel-level grants or wait until every future route is planned before making early awards. But a second programme-level measure is needed alongside emissions per propulsion subsidy: what complete, repeatable electric freight service is each funded project helping create, and which shared assets make that service possible?

This matters all the more because the freight being carried will change. Fossil-linked cargo is likely to decline as coal and petroleum use falls, and ports should not size batteries, terminals and connections around the assumption that today's bulk flows continue indefinitely. A route with strong contracted demand may justify shared charging equipment while a low-utilization route requires a different architecture or timetable. The answer depends on the freight task, not merely on the specifications of an available vessel.

The Dutch programme is a promising way to accelerate the first investments. Its next achievement should be to show that those investments connect: cargo moving on named routes, vessels using dependable shared energy infrastructure, and operating data strong enough to finance the following corridor without perpetually reinventing the subsidy. The ship is the visible part of the transition. The corridor is what makes it commercially useful.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing examines the subsidy rules, the Moerdijk battery-exchange arrangement and the difference between vessel awards and operational corridors. The open, 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report contains the modelled cargo, fleet, battery-pool, terminal and investment evidence behind that analysis.

#5782. Connecticut solar projects directly support Veterans Affairs campus

The Connecticut Department of Veterans Affairs (DVA), Connecticut Green Bank and their partners recently marked the completion of solar installations serving DVA’s main campus and skilled nursing facility in Rocky Hill, Connecticut.

The projects will deliver renewable electricity and reduce energy costs at the campus. The Green Bank developed the projects through its Solar Marketplace Assistance Program Plus (Solar MkAP+). Kearsarge Energy has partnered with Connecticut Green Bank as the long-term owners of these projects as well as nine others and will also oversee operations and maintenance. Greenskies Clean Energy was the engineering, procurement and construction contractor for the two DVA installations.

“To Gov. Lamont and your administration, thank you for having the vision for the GreenerGov CT initiative, providing reliable, environmentally friendly and cost-saving energy for residents and businesses in Connecticut, which is one of your top priorities, along with countless others, enabling Connecticut’s 3.6 million residents to thrive,” said Ron Welch, a retired brigadier general with the U.S. Army, and commissioner of the DVA. “Our team at the Connecticut Department of Veterans Affairs thanks you for your full support of this solar field project and for all you do through your significant partnerships to sustain and improve the environment and the quality of life for Connecticut residents and beyond.”

The two systems have a combined capacity of 1.404 MWDC and are projected to generate approximately 1.84 million kWh of electricity annually. The systems are projected to offset 85% of electricity use for the skilled nursing facility and 35% of the electricity use at the main campus building.

“This project brings together two important commitments: advancing sustainability in state government and supporting the people who have served our nation,” said Michelle Gilman, commissioner of the Department of Administrative Services. “DAS is proud to support this project and the broader Greener Gov CT partnership.”

The two arrays built at the Rocky Hill campus are 604.8 kWDC and 799.2 kWDC.

#5777. As coal burn declines, rising power demand will be primarily met by renewables: EIA

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The electric power sector could install more than 26 GW of battery storage next year, helping support a renewable resource base expected to supply almost a quarter of 2027 consumption, EIA said.

Solar and wind generation are expected to add new capacity of about 72 GW and 20 GW, respectively, this year and next, EIA said.

"Generation from other energy sources is likely to remain relatively flat or decrease,” it added.

While commercial and industrial sector demand will continue to see robust growth next year, that could be “offset somewhat by lower residential electricity demand resulting from forecast milder summer weather next year,” EIA said.

The federal government’s energy data arm is forecasting U.S. gas generation’s share of the electric power generation mix to hold relatively steady at an average of about 40% in 2026 and 2027, and nuclear’s share to average 18%.

Coal’s share of generation will likely decline from 17% in 2025 to 15% by 2027 as 13 GW of capacity is scheduled to retire by the end of next year, EIA said.

“Although the share of generation from those fuels is falling, total generation from those fuels is relatively flat in the forecast, remaining in line with the combined generation from 2025,” according to the short-term outlook.

The electric power sector’s coal consumption has declined, relative to 2025, in eight of 10 months so far this year, EIA noted.

“The decreases have been driven by significant increases in wind and solar capacity as well as a 9% decline in the average natural gas price in the electric power sector during the second and third quarters of this year,” EIA said. “We expect coal consumption to decline an additional 5% in 2027, driven by a 19% decrease in natural gas prices for electric generating plants and further increases in wind and solar capacity.”

EIA’s report also forecasts significant battery storage growth. Capacity will rise from 28.9 GW in the first quarter of 2025 to 66 GW by the end of this year and 91.7 GW at the end of 2027, counting systems with a capacity of 1 MW or greater.

#5774. What’s next for California’s climate laws following an appeals court split decision

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals allowed Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions reporting under SB 253 to move forward while halting the enforcement of scope 3 reporting and SB 261.

The Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals issued a split decision on California’s climate disclosure laws, Senate Bill 253 and Senate Bill 261, on Monday, allowing some emissions reporting requirements to proceed, while blocking others on the basis of them being considered in part or completely unconstitutional under the First Amendment.

In the decision, the federal appellate panel partially upheld and partially overturned a district court's decision from August 2025, which declined to enjoin the laws. The U.S. Chamber of Commerce and other business associations filed the lawsuit, requesting to temporarily block implementation of both climate laws while their constitutionality was under review.

Implementation of SB 261 was already temporarily halted pending review by the Ninth Circuit last November.

SB 253, or the Climate Corporate Data Accountability Act, requires companies with more than $1 billion in revenue to report Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions, with eventual Scope 3 disclosures. SB 261, or the Climate-Related Financial Risk Act, requires companies with more than $500 million in revenue to publicly disclose their climate-related financial risks and countermeasures. Both climate disclosure regulations were signed into law in October 2023 by California Gov. Gavin Newsom and have since faced a spate of legal challenges.

The ruling came a day before the inaugural emissions disclosure deadline for SB 253.

The Nov. 9 decision allowed for the reporting of a company’s direct emissions to proceed under SB 253’s Scope 1 and Scope 2 disclosure requirements, affirming the lower court’s decision. The court ruled such disclosures “regulate commercial speech,” which the appellate panel defined as factual and objective information on a company’s own operations.

However, the appeals court reversed the lower court’s ruling when it came to SB 253’s Scope 3 disclosure requirements, which ask companies to report the indirect emissions generated from their supply chain. The appellate panel said Scope 3 reporting requirements outlined under the bill were considered “likely unconstitutional” as they demanded supply chain data that was “insufficiently tethered” to a company’s own operations and, hence, failed to qualify as commercial speech.

The majority of the appellate panel also concluded that SB 261 “compels noncommercial speech” by requiring covered companies to disclose their financial exposure to climate change risks in biennial reports based on the Task Force on Climate-related Financial Disclosures framework.

U.S. Circuit Judge Jacqueline Nguyen wrote in the Nov. 9 opinion that this requirement asks companies to publish “value-laden judgments on a politically fraught subject” that “go beyond providing economic information tethered to any commercial transaction.”

Nguyen said disclosures demanded under SB 261 ran afoul of First Amendment protections and were likely unconstitutional, ruling the law cannot be enforced. The federal judge added that the state of California could have relied on less restrictive measures to achieve its goals, such as asking for voluntary climate-related financial risk disclosures.

The court’s decision came a day before companies subject to SB 253 were required to submit their first annual Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions for the most recent fiscal year to the California Air Resources Board, the agency tasked with enforcing the state’s climate risk disclosure rules

CARB’s website still states Nov. 10 to be the reporting deadline for covered companies and, since the Ninth Circuit did not pause enforcement of Scope 1 and Scope 2 reporting, the deadline remained legally active. The agency has yet to release an official summary tallying submission counts from this week.

“The principal question facing companies will be whether and when to devote resources to SB 253 Scope 1 and 2 compliance,” Ropes & Gray attorneys Michael Littenberg, Marc Rotter and Peter Witschi wrote in a Tuesday post. “CARB’s various reporting accommodations for this year effectively turned fiscal 2025 compliance into a practice run. Based on CARB’s pre-rulemaking proposals, SB 253 Scope 1 and 2 compliance will ratchet up next year, with more prescriptive disclosure requirements and limited assurance kicking in.”

However, the attorneys noted that the prediction is based on the assumption that Scope 1 and 2 reporting survives going forward and there are “no other intervening developments that delay or modify that portion of SB 253 for next year.”

The Ropes & Gray attorneys said pausing compliance work on indirect emissions, or Scope 3 reporting, seems to be a “low-risk proposition,” given the Ninth Circuit’s decision. The law firm also noted that given CARB’s reporting requirements under SB 253 for 2027 onwards are still in the early drafting stages, companies still lack guidance on what they would need to do to comply in the future.

At this time, the fate of SB 261 seems most uncertain. The Ninth Circuit of Appeals temporarily paused enforcement of the law last November after the Chamber of Commerce and other business groups filed an emergency application with the U.S. Supreme Court to block both climate laws pending the outcome of their appeal filed in the appellate court. Though the group requested that the implementation of both climate disclosure laws be halted, the Ninth Circuit only granted the motion for injunction pending appeal to the enforcement of SB 261.

Given the status of the law, experts from law firm Morrison & Foerster said it is “unlikely that companies will have to comply with SB 261’s reporting requirements in the near future.” However, the firm noted that companies should continue to monitor the trial court proceedings.

Law firm Crowell & Moring echoed this guidance in a Monday post analyzing the Ninth Circuit’s split decision.

“The decision supports preliminary relief for SB 261 and Scope 3 but should not be described as permanently striking down those requirements,” the firm wrote. “Companies should monitor the mandate, district court proceedings and implementing injunction orders, CARB’s response, and any requests for rehearing or further review to evaluate future compliance activities.”

#5772. Renowned Awards for the Newcomer: ID. Polo Wins Golden Steering Wheel, the Autonis Design Award and Is Voted German Car of the Year 2027

The new ID. Polo is already garnering prestigious awards shortly after its market launch. They recognise both the overall package of the new all-electric small car and its design. Another prize was added yesterday evening: the ID. Polo received the Golden Steering Wheel 2026 in the Best car under 25,000 euros category. It had already been chosen as the overall winner of the German Car of the Year 2027 and won the coveted Autonis design award in the small car category.

With the Golden Steering Wheel award, the trade magazine AUTO BILD and the BILD am SONNTAG newspaper recognise outstanding new models every year. This year, the new ID. Polo impressed and received the Best car under 25,000 euros editorial award. In addition to its strong overall package, it was particularly highlighted that, with the ID. Polo, Volkswagen is bringing electric mobility into a price class that is affordable for many car drivers.

The award was presented to Thomas Schäfer, CEO of the Volkswagen brand, Head of the Brand Group Core and member of the Group Board of Management: “Three big awards within a few weeks, and now also the Golden Steering Wheel: the ID. Polo has made a dream start. Above all, however, customers, readers and experts confirm exactly what we wanted to achieve with this car, namely building true Volkswagen cars once more. Affordable, high-quality, suitable for everyday use and easy to operate. In fact: 100-per-cent Volkswagen. The fact that this has been recognised so quickly makes me proud of the entire team.”

Last week, the ID. Polo enjoyed great success at the German Car of the Year Award 2027: first, it won in the Budget car category for vehicles with a starting price of less than 25,000 euros. As a result, the small car qualified for the vote for the overall winner. After two intensive test days, the ID. Polo prevailed against four other strong class winners and won the coveted title of overall winner, making it the German Car of the Year 2027. An independent jury of 47 German and international motoring journalists decided on the award.

The design of the ID. Polo has also been well received: In the Autonis readers’ poll 2026 by auto motor und sport, the ID. Polo achieved first place in the small car category. Volkswagen achieved two category wins in the vote: in addition to the ID. Polo, the new T-Roc also won the Small SUV/Crossover category. The Volkswagen Multivan was a winner in the Vans category. For Volkswagen, the award as overall winner for the ID. Polo is also the first award for the new Pure Positive design language — with a clear-cut shape, a likeable appearance and surprising details.

ID. Polo also in the final of Car of the Year 2027. The new ID. Polo is also among the seven finalists in the renowned European Car of the Year 2027 award. For the first time in the history of the award, the list comprises exclusively of all-electric vehicles. After further test drives and the final evaluation by the international expert jury, the winner will be announced on 8 January 2027 at the Brussels Motor Show.

Electric. Practical. Affordable. The awards underline the positioning of the new ID. Polo as an all-electric compact Volkswagen for a broad audience. With a starting price of 24,995 euros (in Germany), a range of up to 454 kilometres and a luggage capacity of up to 1,240 litres (with the rear seat backrests folded down), it combines electric mobility with high everyday suitability, plenty of space and an attractive price-performance ratio.

#5771. Roll-A-Rack debuting low-profile, manufactured-on-site solar racking

Roll-A-Rack, a developer of patented, on-site-manufactured solar racking systems, will introduce SunChord, its new multi-purpose ground-mounted system, at RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas.

SunChord is a low-profile solar racking designed to use less land than conventional fixed-tilt racking or trackers. It is composed of two components: gutter channels and clamp brackets that attach to solar panels. SunChord assembles using what Roll-A-Rack has dubbed its “snap-slide-secure” system.

“The next generation of solar technology needs to do more than produce clean electricity. It needs to make better use of land, minimize environmental impacts and create value beyond the energy generated,” said Don Scipione, founder of Roll-A-Rack. “SunChord reflects our vision of harmonizing sun and earth, combining solar energy production with rainwater harvesting and soil conservation in a system that can unlock new opportunities for solar development on land where conventional approaches fall short.”

Roll-A-Rack claims an array using SunChord can capture 95% of rain that falls it using its racking channels to redirect the water. The company sees potential for the racking in agrivoltaic applications, sites with land constraints, on brownfields and where ground disturbance should be limited.

SunChord is assembled on-site using a roll-forming device similar to producing roof-edge gutters. SunChord was developed with support from the U.S. Department of Energy’s Integrated Energy Systems Office and the USDA National Institute of Food and Agriculture. The company has two issued U.S. patents, a patent application pending, wind-tunnel ratings and UL 2703 certification.

Roll-A-Rack is planning to supply SunChord for up to five pilot projects. Interested EPCs and developers can contact the company at [email protected].

#5769. Utility, tech officials see opportunities to increase grid utilization

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Specialized AI models and grid-enhancing technologies could help, but the industry already has the basic planning and tools to make the grid more flexible, panelists said at the BNEF Houston Summit.

The panel on grid utilization was held as utilities, hyperscalers and regulators are scrambling to accommodate large-load demand amid backlash from ratepayers concerned about affordability.

Grid Strategies, a consultancy that publishes a closely-watched annual report on U.S. bulk power supply and demand, said late last year that the systemwide load factor is about 60%, heading to 66% by 2030. Grid Strategies defines load factor as the ratio of average energy usage to peak load.

Some utilities run their systems at even lower load factors.

In response to a first-of-its-kind legislative mandate, Dominion Energy’s Virginia subsidiary revealed last month that its distribution grid runs at an average load factor of 41% and peaks just under 80% utilization. Its transmission system’s “median facility utilization” is about 33%, though individual facilities may approach or exceed rated capacity during outages, demand peaks or “significant power transfers,” Dominion said.

Jessica Hogle, NRG’s vice president of federal affairs, said that utilities should think ambitiously about grid flexibility.

“We should just say: ‘This is our goal and we will get there’,” Hogle said.

NRG, a Texas-based independent power producer and retail energy provider, aims to deliver 25 GW of demand response, energy storage and virtual power plant capacity by 2035, she said, adding that NRG believed the goal was ambitious but achievable.

Hogle joined NRG in June. She began her career in the electricity business with Pacific Gas & Electric Corp. as the San Francisco-based investor-owned utility navigated shifting policy winds, she said.

“We would say, there is no way we could do this in terms of integrating these resources, it is too hard and too expensive,” Hogle said, paraphrasing the company’s message to California legislators mulling ever-stricter renewable energy mandates.

In the end, PG&E’s concerns were overblown, she said, adding: “We exceeded the targets.”

California’s power-sector emissions fell significantly as its renewable generation share grew.

Getting more out of the existing grid — finding “a better way to optimize every dollar” — could likewise have broad-based economic and societal benefits, said Arushi Sharma Frank, a Texas-based energy consultant and strategic advisor to the Utilize Coalition.

“The AI narrative is very important, but underneath that, there is [the] onshoring of everything we need,” she said, referring to efforts to site more heavy and high-tech manufacturing in the United States. “We can’t do any of that if we operate in the same mindset we’ve had for years.”

Grid operators already use tools to improve grid flexibility and increase headroom, like the Remedial Action Schemes common in the Western Interconnection, said Dave Berry, CEO of “powered land” developer Cloverleaf Infrastructure.

A Remedial Action Scheme is, in essence, a logic pathway that defines how the grid responds to a disturbance such as a line or generator outage, Berry said. The drawback is that by curtailing generation or load to protect customers in one area, a RAS may harm their counterparts in another.

“The challenge is getting all the stakeholders aligned,” Berry said.

Sharma Frank, an advisor to the data center flexibility startup Emerald AI, predicted more software-based flexibility solutions too.

Inside utilities, artificial intelligence tools can break down silos between grid planners and operators that have both been hamstrung in different ways by constraints on analytical capacity, she said.

“That is the other interesting part about the AI boom: We have compute that we didn’t have before,” Sharma Frank said. “This unlocks the ability to see [grid] capacity that we didn’t see before.”

Voltus CEO Dana Guernsey compared new “bring your own new capacity” contracts to the power purchase agreements that “revolutionized” utility-scale solar and wind development.

#5768. The Perovskite Solar Cell Kraken Has Been Unleashed Upon The US

Perovskite is a relatively inexpensive material that has attracted thousands of researchers since 2009, when a team of scientists in Japan first demonstrated its use in solar cells. They achieved a solar energy conversion efficiency of little more than 3%, but since science loves a challenge, that was just the beginning of the story. In the latest chapter, the US firms Heliene and Caeulux have just introduced a new perovskite-enabled solar module that hits the jackpot at up to 27% efficiency, and it is already in commercial production, no less.

So far in the renewable energy transition, silicon has shouldered much of the heavy lifting due to its excellent solar conversion properties. Still, silicon is relatively expensive material that demands a complex manufacturing process. Perovskite is the reverse. Based on a naturally occurring mineral, synthetic perovskites can be lab-grown and applied to solar cell fabrication in a high volume, high throughput process similar to roll-to-roll manufacturing and other solution-based methods (see lots more perovskite background here).

There being no such thing as a free lunch, “raw” perovskite is challenged by natural conditions, namely, humidity. Tweaking the formula with other, more durable materials can resolve that problem. Researchers have also developed a tandem solution, in which a perovskite layer rests upon a silicon layer, providing an efficiency boost while minimizing the additional cost.

In the US solar market, the sticky wicket is the current state of federal energy, trade, and tax polices. Still, the red-hot demand for solar energy in the US has motivated solar stakeholders to steamroll over those obstacles, attracting overseas investors (here’s one example) as well as new domestic ventures.

On the domestic side, the Corning-backed manufacturer Heliene has been focusing on silicon while the startup Caelux has tackled the perovskite solar cell challenge with a glass substrate that can be layered onto other materials in a solar module. Now the two manufacturers have combined forces, with Caelux’s perovskite-doped glass boosting the conversion efficiency of Heliene’s N-type solar cells by up to 20%.

The Caelux-Heliene mashup comes at a time when Congress has finally put the wheels in motion to streamline the permitting process for energy projects. The two firms already have a running head start. On November 10, the two firms announced that commercial-scale production has already begun on their Hybrid-Tandem™ solar module

“Manufactured by Heliene in Minnesota, the perovskite plus silicon technology combines Caelux’s Energy Producing Glass with Heliene’s established silicon module platform to construct a solar module with two separate power generation layers that deliver higher power density,” Heliene explains.

“One of the advantages of Caelux’s technology is that we can integrate its Energy Producing Glass into our existing manufacturing lines without having to build an entirely new production setup,” added Martin Pochtaruk, Heliene CEO and Founder, in a press statement.

“With both companies manufacturing in the U.S., we’re bringing together two fully domestic supply chains to advance what’s possible with American-made solar,” Pochtaruk emphasized.

Stay tuned for the next development. The new modules are now in production at Heliene’s manufacturing facility Minnesota, with deliveries set for 2027. That’s just for starters. Caelux and Heliene have already targeted 29% efficiency and up for their next-generation modules.

The conversion efficiency improvement is the gift that keeps on giving. In the rooftop solar sector, for example, high-efficiency solar cells enable developers to squeeze more electricity out of a given space, making a rooftop array more economical for smaller properties that are otherwise shut out of the market. The cost of a smaller, more effcient array can also ripple downwards in the form of savings on labor, hardware, and transportation expenses.

For rooftop solar in general, the boost in efficiency provides an opportunity to take full advantage of power sharing programs with local utilities, which enable solar owners to earn revenue and qualify for time-of-use discounts. The benefits can ripple into home energy storage systems and home EV charging as well.

On a larger scale, the space-saving angle can help alleviate land use conflicts that otherwise stymie solar development, particularly in the area of farm-to-solar conversion.

Although real estate development is by far the primary factor driving the loss of farmland in the US, solar opponents (looking at you, Brooke Rollins) have played the farm card to gin up resistance to solar development in rural communities. With efficiency improvements, the economic case improves for solar arrays on marginal lands and smaller parcels. That can be partcularly helpful in the community solar field, where solar arrays are typically smaller.

Additionally, the improved efficiency can help accelerate the uptake of agrivoltaic practices that balance energy-producing solar panels with grazing and croplands. These dual-use arrays can enable farmers to continue working their land while guaranteeing a revenue stream from the solar developer.

Furthermore, high-efficiency solar opens up new opportunities in the field of floating solar, where developers can make use of reservoirs and other human-engineered bodies of water instead of taking up space on land. By taking up less room on water for the same power generation capacity, floating solar can also squeeze into reservoirs where recreation and habitat conservation also compete for space.

Perovskite solar cells represent just one key breakthrough area in the US solar industry. Easing the federal energy permitting bottleneck is another. That can be a two-edged sword, enabling more fossil energy resources to flow through the pipeline. However, permitting reform could also prevent the fossil energy industry from running amok through federal energy policy.

Members of Congress are currently negotiating over the particulars of Senate Bill 5653, aka the “Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act. If enacted as envisioned by the solar industry, the reforms will put a crimp in Trump’s “American Energy Dominance” policy, which has placed new and burdensome restrictions on both wind and solar activity in the US while aggressively supporting fossil fuels and nuclear, alongside other renewables including biomass, geothermal, hydropower. Even energy storage has come in for a share of love from the Trump administration, further underscoring the remarkable exclusion of wind and solar from federal energy policy.

The trade organization Solar Energy Industries Association, for one, is urging members on both sides of the aisle to ensure that permitting reform focuses on fairness, predictability, and certainty. In a letter to the House and Senate, SEIA emphasizes that solar and storage developers “cannot finance multi-year, capital-intensive projects without the predictability and certainty needed to build projects and connect them to the grid.”

“According to researchers at Wood Mackenzie, solar and storage represent 70% of the new capacity added to the grid thus far in 2026. Yet $120 billion in clean energy investment is caught up in current permitting delays, let alone the impact of permitting challenges to future deployment,” SEIA adds.

Of course, even if such a bill passes both Houses of Congress, US President Donald Trump could exercise his veto power, and he has already threatened to make copious use of it during his last two years in office. Nevertheless, US Presidents come and go, and this one is scheduled to go in January of 2029 — peacefully this time, one hopes.

Image: The US perovskite solar cell specialist Caelux is combining with the startup Heliene to manufacture high efficiency perovskite-silicon modules in the US (cropped courtesy of Caelux).

#5765. Doctor Behind Mass Shooting in D.C., Police Say

— Emergency physician accused of shooting multiple people -- one fatally -- before killing himself

Emergency physician Matt Lambert, MD, was named by NBC News as the gunman in Thursday's mass shooting in Washington, D.C., citing law enforcement officials.

According to police, Lambert fatally shot a man at a gym and left another in critical condition before fleeing and shooting others. He then killed himself after barricading himself in an apartment.

Lambert described himself on his website as a clinician and entrepreneur. Lambert holds active medical licenses in West Virginia and Montana, according to DocInfo.org. Neither license lists any disciplinary action.

An X account that appears to be associated with Lambert posted a manifesto on Thursday morning, which included a screenshot of a document with Lambert's name on it.

"My manifesto is an engineering report," the post stated. Police had said earlier in the day their suspect apparently had a dispute with the building's homeowners association.

The X account, OfMiceandMold, hosts dozens of images and complaints about problems with the condo and building. Lambert's name is mentioned several times in documents or screenshots of documents, including a lawsuit in which Lambert is the plaintiff.

The address on Lambert's website is on 15th Street, where the shooting occurred.

The shootings occurred around 8 a.m. in Logan Circle, about a mile north of the White House. Much of the block on 15th Street Northwest was cordoned off as police urged people to avoid the area, which includes a VIDA fitness center, St. Luke's Episcopal Church, residences, and a Whole Foods supermarket. Schools were locked down.

Law enforcement SWAT teams and other officers had their guns drawn on the roof of an apartment building and on the ground below.

Witness James Spellman, who was at the gym, said there were about 50 people there around 8 a.m. when the shooting began.

"Within seconds there was a man who fell to the ground, and a pool of blood came out," he said. "One gym member rushed to help him. He was instantly dead. He had been shot in the neck."

Spellman said he and others dashed out of the gym. He said he heard about two shots fired in the gym and about five or six fired outside.

Officials believe Lambert shot another person in a parking garage elevator, and then barricaded himself on the seventh floor of the building, the official said.

Another witness, Ryan Dubicki, said he was in his nearby apartment around the same time when he "heard about five bangs."

"They were really spread out so I thought it was construction," he said. A short time later, "there were cops everywhere."

The Logan Circle neighborhood has seen violence before. In early August 2025, a onetime member of President Donald Trump's Department of Government Efficiency was assaulted early on a Sunday morning in the neighborhood by a group of teenagers attempting to carjack him and a woman, authorities said.

Days later, Trump fulfilled his threat to bypass local authority and imposed direct federal control over the city, sending in hundreds of National Guard members to reduce crime. That deployment continues today.

The Associated Press contributed to this story.

#5764. 300 Glaciers Have Disappeared from the American West Since the Mid‑20th Century — the Survivors Have a Few Things in Common

From an airplane, most of the glaciers of the Rocky Mountain West look like small patches of white nestled into rugged landscape where the topography folds. The white is the snow cover, and lower down you can see the gray-blue bare ice.

In the middle of the 20th century, over 1,450 glaciers could be found in the contiguous U.S. Together, they covered 217 square miles (562 square kilometers) in six western states. Today, a comprehensive review my colleagues and I just published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences shows their total area has shrunk by 35% and they are still shrinking, perhaps faster.

A little more than 300 glaciers have disappeared, way more than I had anticipated.

Those that survive reflect just how vulnerable mountain glaciers have become as global temperatures rise. Where glaciers once covered large expanses of the western mountains thousands of years ago, most of the remaining glaciers in the Rockies and Sierra Nevada are small and found in topographically protected places.

When people think of glaciers, they often picture huge ice streams with icebergs calving off the front, like you’ll find in Alaska or Greenland. In the Lower 48 states, with the exception of a few large glaciers in Washington, they’re much smaller.

We found that the average glacier area is about one-tenth of a square mile (about a quarter of a square kilometer) in size. The area of the largest glacier, found on Washington’s Mount Rainier, is about 4.3 square miles (11.24 square kilometers).

I was surprised to find that glaciers in the Rocky Mountains – those in Colorado, Montana and Wyoming and in the Sierra Nevada of California – subsist because of their unique position on the landscape, which somewhat insulates them from the otherwise less-favorable regional climate.

The surviving Rocky Mountain glaciers inhabit shaded niches less exposed to direct sunshine and gain additional snow from avalanches. If these glaciers were exposed to more sun and less snow accumulation, they likely wouldn’t exit at all.

We found the fastest-thinning glaciers are in the Beartooth-Absaroka Mountains of the Rockies in southern Montana, which are losing about 1.5 feet (about half a meter) of ice thickness per year.

It’s a different story in Washington, where the glaciers on Mount Rainier and Mount Baker are much larger, have cooler summers and get a lot more snowfall. Although they too are shrinking, they do so at a much slower rate, about half a foot of ice thickness per year. The glacier area on Mount Rainier alone is greater than the total sum in all the other states.

In Washington, generally speaking, the higher you go, the less glacier loss you find. That makes sense; it’s cooler at higher elevations. But in the Rocky Mountain region there is no similar trend, suggesting the survivors are niche glaciers that depend on local topographic benefits.

In a study a few years ago, colleagues and I modeled the glaciers on Washington’s Olympic Peninsula and found most would disappear by 2070 under current climate trends. That indicates that glaciers elsewhere are probably not far behind.

The Olympics are vulnerable because they’re close to the Pacific Ocean at somewhat lower elevation. The other Washington glaciers, particularly on Mount Rainier and Mount Baker, will likely survive past 2100, but how much longer is an open question.

The principal importance of the glaciers is to provide water to high alpine ecosystems. The glaciers partially melt in late summer, providing cool water for cold-adapted fish and other stream life. They provide a water supply that’s otherwise absent in the dry late summer, after the seasonal snow disappears, when the region doesn’t get a lot of rain.

While the glaciers of the Rockies are primarily important for ecosystems at this point, those in Washington also contribute to hydropower production.

An important hazard is also associated with the glaciers on the steep-sided volcanoes of the Cascade Range, which stretches from California to Washington. The moraines formed by these glaciers are large, partly due to the highly erodible volcanic rock beneath the ice. Moving ice deposits eroded rocks, forming aprons at their lower ends. As the glaciers retreat, they leave the accumulated rocks behind as steep-sided moraines that are no longer buttressed by the ice.

High water flows from rain or glacier melt can undercut the moraines, triggering landslides and creating debris flows that go rocketing down the mountains. This happened in 2006 on Mount Hood and Mount Rainier and closed a number of highways. We’ll see that happen more often as the glaciers retreat.

The shrinking and eventual loss of all of these glaciers will affect ecosystems across the West, as well as waterways humans and wildlife rely on.

By Andrew Fountain, Professor of Geography, Portland State University

#5763. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5762. Democrats Are at COP31 to Show That the US Has Not Abandoned the Fight Against Climate Change

The results of the US midterms on November 3 delivered a resounding victory for the Democrats, and a major loss for US president Donald Trump and the Republican party.

Gaining control of the Senate and the House of Representatives has emboldened the Democrats, giving them a renewed sense of political legitimacy and a mandate to pursue their agenda. But will this shift translate into meaningful changes in US climate change policy?

The Democratic sweep will change the political dynamics around US climate policy and how the country cooperates with the international community on this issue. Although there was no official US presence at the 2025 COP conference, held in Brazil, a coalition of 100 local US leaders, including governors, mayors and other top city and state officials, attended as part of the US Climate Alliance. Former US vice-president Al Gore, and former US secretary of state John Kerry are part of an unofficial US group at the 2026 gathering.

Kerry said in a speech there: “Americans are committed to taking back their future, and we will build and rebuild our partnerships as we do that.”

Being on the ground at COP is important. As the US has a decisive role to play in global climate policy, sending a non-official coalition signals that many Americans are still working to combat climate change.

Trump has repeatedly stated that climate change is a hoax and refused to attend the COP31 climate talks being held in Turkey. But 20 Democratic members of Congress will be attending, although they have been denied official state department funding for the trip.

During the first and second Trump administrations, the US has been an outlier among wealthy countries when it comes to fighting climate change. Trump has actively backed the use of fossil fuels, undermining clean energy projects, and rolled back legislation that stipulated emission standards for vehicles.

Trump also weakened greenhouse gas reporting requirements and methane regulation. Then in February 2026, the Trump administration rescinded the Environmental Protection Agency’s 2009 finding that greenhouse gases threaten the health and welfare of the public, which had regulated the emissions from new cars and other vehicles. This had provided the legal foundation for regulation under the Clean Air Act.

On the international front, Trump was equally determined to undermine global efforts to fight climate change. Famously, he withdrew from the Paris agreement, part of a global initiative to address climate change, and refused to attend COP summits. But the Democrats’ seismic midterm win may be a sign of optimism for those fighting to address climate change.

The biggest issues of the midterms were affordability, corruption and the Iran war, but issues related to fighting climate change were also on voters’ list. There is growing concern about data centres. As many as 56% of US voters felt that data centres and AI were either a major or even crucial factor in influencing who they voted for, with only 13% saying it was not a factor at all.

Data centres require millions of gallons of water for cooling systems, and this is expected to rise to 73 billion litres of water a year by 2028. Over half of planned AI facilities in the US will be situated in areas that have significant water shortages or drought conditions.

After the midterms, Zoe Lofgren, a Democrat, is likely to become the chair of the House of Representatives’ committee on science, space and technology once the new session starts in January. She wants to tackle the administration’s “climate denialism”.

Lofgren’s leadership, along with new Democratic members of the committee, may offer it more power to reverse some of the Trump administration’s changes to science policy.

However, the Democrat midterm wins will not mean that the US is automatically back at the negotiating table at COP nor that the US will be able to rejoin the Paris Accords while Trump is president. Trump still has the power to make these decisions and can continue to keep the US out of international climate initiatives, regardless of who controls Congress. The US Constitution says nothing about withdrawing from treaties, which creates uncertainty for what the Senate’s role might be.

The election, however, does signal that there is considerable domestic opposition to his approach. And while Congress has not passed follow-up legislation prohibiting presidential withdrawal from the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), it has continued to fund several UNFCCC initiatives. During the Biden administration, the US significantly increased climate finance from US$1.5 billion (£1.1 billion) a year in 2021 to US$9.5 billion in 2023.

Democrats could also push back on Trump’s current policies, using their power to hold hearings, investigate the administration’s dismantling of climate programmes and challenge cuts to scientific research. The Democrats can also use the power of Congress’s control of spending to protect or restore funding to some of these environmental initiatives.

They could also try to introduce legislation that reinstates some of the clean energy initiatives that had been implemented in past administrations to strengthen emissions standards and support clean energy. The big issue, however, is that Trump has made it clear that he is going to be very active in using his veto power, and without a two-thirds majority in Congress, the Democrats will not be able to override this.

What this all means is that the world cannot expect the US to be a reliable and constructive partner at COP with Trump still in power. Trump will probably continue to forge ahead with expanding fossil fuel production, while weakening international climate cooperation, even if the electorate disapproves of these policies.

Ironically, Trump’s greatest contribution to the fight against climate change was his initiation of the war with Iran, which helped to cut global fossil fuel carbon dioxide emissions by 0.4% due the closure of the Strait of Hormuz. But climate scientists note this is not a long-term solution.

While the Democrats’ electoral victory offers a glimmer of hope for renewed climate action, both domestically and internationally, restoring US climate leadership will take time, especially while Trump remains in power.

By Natasha Lindstaedt, Professor in the Department of Government, University of Essex

#5761. Martin Solar cuts out zip ties with new mounting rail

Martin Solar‘s newly manufactured Dual-Channel Solar Rail (DCSR) was recently installed on a 70-kWDC solar project, marking its first real-world application.

The Dual-Channel Solar Rail from Martin Solar.

“Management of PV wires and AC trunk cables is one of the most important aspects of rooftop PV installation. Anyone who has ever experienced running hundreds, thousands of feet of conductors on a rooftop system knows what a hassle it is having to apply countless zip ties that are not really suitable for long-term wire management,” said Preston Nelson, director of Martin Solar and co-inventor of DCSR. “What’s worse than the initial application of zip ties is having to pull up finished solar panels to apply even more zip ties after an inspector inevitably cites an installation for having wires that droop down to the roof surface.”

DCSR is designed with a wire trough large enough to fit three-phase trunk cables, like those manufactured by Enphase, Hoymiles, and AP Systems. It can also fit up to twenty standard PV cables, without the use of zip ties. Instead of using specialized panel clamps, DCSR’s panel attachment channel is off to the side of the wire trough, where it separately accepts panel hardware.

“The dual-channel design with separately dedicated features allowed us to increase the cross-sectional area of the wire trough to a size never seen before as a rail feature. In the past, we would install a separate wire tray to manage numerous long trunk cable runs, which was even more expensive and time-consuming. Now, this present project installation involves over 600 total feet of trunk, without a single cable tie. The separate wire trough can even allow access to wire runs without lifting panels, especially convenient when a worker simply needs to track a PV string,” Nelson said.

DCSR is the first product accessory to Martin Solar’s Hidden-Fastener Solar Mount, which mounts to the roof surface without putting holes in shingles. While Martin’s proprietary solar rail and mount can be paired, both are compatible with other solar racking and mounts.

Martin Solar will show DCSR and its solar mounting system at RE+ from November 17 to 19 at booth C5071.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5760. Senators Investigate Whether RFK Jr. Lied to Congress About Samoa Trip

— HHS has maintained that the Samoa visit "was unrelated to vaccines"

Two Democratic senators are launching an investigation into whether Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. lied to Congress about his reason for a 2019 trip to Samoa after the Associated Press and the Guardian published documents in August refuting his testimony.

The new inquiry is one of a vast array of investigations planned into the Trump administration after Democrats regained control of the House and Senate from Republicans in the midterm elections.

Sens. Ron Wyden (D-Ore.) and Angela Alsobrooks (D-Md.) said in a letter Wednesday to Children's Health Defense (CHD), a nonprofit group known for anti-vaccine activism that was formerly chaired by Kennedy, that they were starting an investigation.

The lawmakers asked the group for a broad range of documents related to Kennedy's Samoa travel, his 2024 presidential campaign, and his Senate confirmation process. The letter also requested all communications between the group and federal government officials since President Donald Trump's second term began in January 2025.

AP and Guardian reporting in August showed that while Kennedy repeatedly told lawmakers who held the key to his confirmation last year that his visit to Samoa before a deadly measles outbreak had "nothing to do with vaccines," a letter he sent to the Samoan prime minister ahead of the trip on CHD letterhead showed otherwise. In the letter, Kennedy used the words vaccine or vaccination eight times.

"We intend to hold Secretary Kennedy accountable for the grave harm he has caused to vulnerable children," Wyden and Alsobrooks wrote in their letter. "We also intend to hold him accountable for any false statements made to Congress, a crime that undermines the Senate's ability to fulfill its constitutional duties."

HHS has maintained that the Samoa visit "was unrelated to vaccines." In response to an AP inquiry in August, it said "any claim or insinuation based on these letters that Secretary Kennedy lied under oath or to Congress is not only false but outright defamatory."

Kennedy was not under oath during his confirmation hearing. Knowingly giving material false testimony to Congress is a crime that carries a possible prison sentence regardless of whether a person is under oath, though it is rarely prosecuted.

Kennedy's Samoa visit has become one of the most contentious events in his career. At the time, he was leading CHD. Critics said the visit fueled anti-vaccine sentiment in Samoa, which later experienced the measles outbreak that killed 83 people, mostly children under 5.

In the letter reported by the AP and Guardian, dated Jan. 20, 2019, Kennedy addressed then-Prime Minister Tuilaepa Sailele Malielegaoi and described his desire to study Samoa's use of the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine.

Kennedy speculated that the deaths in Samoa may have been caused by issues with different vaccine lots. Writing on CHD letterhead, he said his team wanted to evaluate whether that was the case through a "detailed health informatics assessment."

He expressed his belief that it was difficult and time-consuming in most of the world to investigate "adverse health events associated with vaccines" and said health information systems are rarely designed to quickly study links "between diagnoses and specific vaccination events."

The letter did not specifically discuss anti-vaccine activism.

While in Samoa, Kennedy met with anti-vaccine activists, including one who helped arrange the trip with a U.S. Embassy employee, the AP and the Guardian previously reported.

Months later, with vaccination rates still dangerously low, measles broke out, sickening thousands.

Public health experts have said their efforts to address the outbreak were hampered by anti-vaccine activists, some of whom they claim were emboldened by Kennedy's visit.

Spokespeople for CHD and HHS did not immediately respond to requests for comment Thursday.

Wyden is expected to chair the Senate Finance Committee, which has oversight into Kennedy's health department, when Democrats retake Congress in January. Alsobrooks serves on the Senate Health, Education, Labor, and Pensions Committee, which also oversees the department.

The same senators on Wednesday announced a separate investigation into hundreds of thousands of dollars worth of gifts reportedly given last year to Kennedy by a donor and former employee.

#5759. The Trump Administration’s Pro-Cancer Agenda

Under Trump, Americans could face an extraordinary increase in exposure to cancer-causing toxics and radiation.

Over the nearly two years of President Donald Trump’s second term, his administration has taken more than 500 actions that harm public health or the environment. When faced with this onslaught of harm, it can be difficult to see the strategic agenda at play. However, NRDC’s new analysis reveals that the Trump administration is executing a sweeping and long planned—but quiet—government-wide campaign to dismember major programs that are intended to prevent exposure to cancer-causing toxics and radiation. In sum, this administration has reversed course: America’s government is no longer fighting a “War on Cancer” but fighting a “War to Promote Cancer.”

This is not an accident. Much of this campaign can be found throughout the Heritage Foundation’s Project 2025 blueprint and has long been advocated for by industry. But a healthy life should not be treated as an obstacle to somebody else’s profit margin. The freedom to breathe clean air, drink safe water, eat safe food, work without needless exposure to carcinogens, and raise children without avoidable toxic risks should be among the most basic expectations of life in America. Americans should not have to wonder whether the water coming from their taps contains cancer-causing chemicals, whether the air they breathe carries toxic pollution from a nearby industrial facility, or whether going to work means being unnecessarily exposed to carcinogens.

Instead of ensuring these basic freedoms, the Trump administration is creating a system in which polluters are given greater leeway to expose everyone in the nation to cancer-causing toxics and radiation.

Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5757. To Honor Veterans, How About We Stop The Oil Wars?

Today is Veterans Day in the United States. Naturally, across the country, people have spent at least part of the day thinking about veterans they know as well as veterans they don’t and thanking them for their service. Whether you agree with the various wars the United States has gotten into or not, you have to honor and appreciate the human beings who offered to put their lives on the line in order to support their country (or think that they’re doing so, at least). I know several people who are veterans, and they typically sacrificed a lot in their previous military roles.

However, I do have to come back to the rationale for the wars we as a country get into. I’m 44 years old, and it seems like we’ve been involved in wars related to oil and gas for more than half of my life. It’s just a continual process. We get into a war in the Middle East connected to XYZ, but it’s really about oil. After years of over-involvement, destruction, and death, society realizes it’s been a super counterproductive, idiotic war and we pull out. The, after a few years, we begin again and the story repeats.

Well, how about we just stop getting involved in oil wars? How about we stop depending so much on oil supply from foreign nations and global oil markets? How about we respect our countrymen and countrywomen who sign up to serve by not sending them into pointless forever wars in the Middle East to get traumatized, injured, or killed? That would be truly honoring veterans of the past as well as protecting current and future Americans.
