#5756. Managing Metastases in Advanced Prostate Cancer

— Beyond Diagnosis continues with Christopher Wee on radiation therapy and multidisciplinary care

Developed in partnership with the Our Partner

In "Beyond Diagnosis: Metastatic Castration-Sensitive Prostate Cancer," Cleveland Clinic oncologist Christopher Wee, MD, and host John Mangels discuss how clinicians can guide patients through the growing complexities of treatment for metastatic disease.

Each installment of this series explores key moments in patient care, including building trust, navigating evolving therapies, aligning treatment goals, and supporting shared decision-making.

In this fourth episode, Wee discusses the role of radiation in managing metastatic disease, strategies for treating bone metastases and preventing skeletal complications, and the importance of multidisciplinary care in balancing disease control with quality of life.

The following is a transcript of their remarks:

Mangels: Welcome to "Beyond Diagnosis," where we talk with doctors about treatment decisions and communicating with patients. I'm your host, John Mangels.

Treatment of metastatic castration-sensitive prostate cancer has changed significantly in the last decade. Those changes are creating new opportunities and new complexities for both physicians and patients. How do clinicians choose among expanded treatment options? How do they navigate decisions involving efficacy and risk, quality of life, and patient preferences?

We'll explore those issues and more with our guest, Dr. Christopher Wee. Dr. Wee is a physician in Cleveland Clinic's genitourinary medical oncology program. A large part of his practice involves patients with prostate cancer. Dr. Wee, thanks so much for joining us today.

Mangels: Metastatic disease involves more complexities and more complications potentially than localized prostate cancer. Is your approach to treat metastases individually? Do you try and control them systemically? What's your approach on that?

Wee: Historically, metastatic disease has been a systemic therapy situation, because the spots that you can see on imaging likely underrepresent the true burden of disease. There could be micrometastases elsewhere. And so that's why we don't go and necessarily go after each individual metastasis.

However, there's a caveat, and this is an evolving science. Radiation is becoming an integral part in certain situations. So if someone were to have low-volume de novo metastatic hormone-sensitive prostate cancer, there is a survival benefit per the STAMPEDE trial to do prostate-directed radiotherapy. Sometimes we'll even consider metastasis-directed therapy in those situations. And then if something were to become oligoprogressive to castrate-resistant, so let's say someone has castration-sensitive prostate cancer, metastatic, and the PSA [prostate-specific antigen] starts rising, but there's only one or two spots that are really lighting up on a repeat PET, there are emerging data suggesting that radiation or often SBRT [stereotactic body radiotherapy] can be a benefit to that patient because then if they can get control, they don't have to necessarily switch treatment.

So this is evolving science. I think this is where it's very important to have a multidisciplinary team evaluate these situations, but systemic therapy is still the backbone of treatment of metastatic disease.

Mangels: We've talked a lot already about patient communication. When patients learn that their prostate cancer has spread to their bones or say an organ like the liver, that's bound to be frightening to them. How do you help them understand what those findings mean without completely eradicating hope?

Wee: I think it's frightening because of the uncertainty, and I wish I could sometimes give more certainty to patients like this. Most patients want an honest answer. They don't want sugarcoating. So I'll ask a patient, "Do you mind if I'm upfront with you?" Vast majority of the time they want to know. This is generally considered, in historically speaking, not a curable situation, but some patients can live for years, many years. I don't know at this point. Our goal is to try to give you the best outcome possible, and we do this with these treatments and we go from there.

If patients start asking more specifically about life expectancies, again, at the time of metastatic diagnosis, the median overall survival is years. So it's really hard to predict at that point what this is going to look like.

Mangels: And that's so important to emphasize that incurable doesn't mean untreatable.

Mangels: And then I'm guessing the discussion about patient treatment goals, quality of life, all the things we've talked about before becomes even more important.

So bones are by far the most common site of metastasis in prostate cancer. Beyond controlling the cancer itself, what are your priorities when you're treating a patient with skeletal metastases? Are you looking at managing pain? Are you looking at trying to prevent fractures?

Wee: So in castration-sensitive disease, the primary treatment is actually just using systemic therapy, ADT [androgen deprivation therapy] plus ARPI [androgen receptor pathway inhibitor], plus/minus chemo. The role of a bone-modifying agent such as a bisphosphonate like zoledronic acid [Reclast] or RANKL ligand like denosumab [Prolia] is reserved primarily for patients who have osteoporosis. So a bone scan would be helpful, bone density scan. And if someone has osteoporosis, that should be treated appropriately.

If a patient progresses to castration-resistant disease, there is an indication to give a bone-modifying agent to prevent a skeletal-related metastasis. So that's where the conversation comes into even if a patient doesn't have osteoporosis, if they have osseous metastases, there should be consideration to be giving a bone-modifying agent in castration-resistant disease. If there's a symptomatic spot that's causing a lot of trouble, that's a great time to also consider palliative radiation.

Mangels: Let's talk about radiation. When do you bring in radiation oncology and is the goal of the radiation to treat the tumor or to treat the pain or something else?

Wee: In metastatic prostate cancer, there's generally a few reasons. One, as we discussed previously, in de novo low-volume metastatic disease, primary prostatic radiation should be considered due to an overall survival benefit in STAMPEDE trial, but then also palliative radiation to specific spots if they're painful and metastasis-directed therapy.

So this is where the gray area comes in. So let's say someone has low-volume metastatic disease, just a couple of bone spots per se -- and we're getting this more often now that we have PSMA [prostate-specific membrane antigen] PET -- or a couple lymph nodes only. We had talked about previously how the standard of care metastatic disease is indefinite ADT, but what if you were able to zap all the spots of disease with radiation? Could you give a defined course of treatment to give a treatment-free interval? It's unclear the optimal duration and everything, but it's something we talk about with patients.

And anecdotally, some of these patients can get prolonged disease-free intervals where they don't have to be on treatment necessarily. And so that's something where it's shared decision-making with the patient.

Mangels: Are there particular patterns of metastatic spread or symptomatology or lab results, clinical findings that make you think, "Wow, I really need to think about whether this approach with this patient needs to change or not"?

Wee: I think there are two reasons why we would want to change. If one, the treatment regimen we're not doing is not working, or two, if it is not having manageable toxicity. Those are two reasons to change treatment.

Sometimes, this is not the most common situation, but sometimes despite the PSA being low, a patient could be clinically doing worse. They may be having more fatigue, more weight loss. That should be a red flag. At that time, usually I would re-image with conventional imaging if the PSA is low, because what you worry about in those situations is could this be differentiating into another process such as a neuroendocrine differentiation of prostate cancer or small cell neuroendocrine? And that's a lot more aggressive, at which point it needs to be managed differently with a lot more aggressive therapy sometimes.

Mangels: We talked a couple of seconds ago about bringing radiation oncology in, but that sort of opens the door to the importance of multidisciplinary care in these patients. Let's talk about that. You've got orthopedic oncology, I'm guessing, pain management, palliative care, rehab. Talk about how you coordinate all of those and involve all those and why they're important to be involved.

Wee: Absolutely. So radiation oncology is probably the most common one we work with, obviously. Palliative medicine with the symptom support, especially if there's pain involved because we can really improve patients' quality of life and oftentimes extend survival if we have good palliative care.

We're increasingly using our colleagues in nuclear medicine, both from a diagnostic standpoint with the PSMA PET scans as well as therapeutics with radioligand therapy. So this is one of the exciting parts of being a medical oncologist is the team-sport nature of this and how we can all work together to try to do what's best for the patient.

Mangels: Thanks so much for your insights, Dr. Wee. And thank you for joining us on Beyond Diagnosis, where we explore not just what physicians know, but how to effectively and compassionately share that knowledge with patients. See you next time.

#5755. From Callback to Confidence: How Engineering an ESS for Field Reduces the Costly Callbacks

Installer on-site callbacks are the silent margin killer in residential energy management and storage. They consume labor, fuel, new project scheduling capacity, and homeowner trust in ways that rarely show up in the original bid. Across the industry, callbacks come from three sources that often overlap: system designs that are too complex to cleanly install, hardware failures after the system is in service, and challenges that arise in the field during installations. Most contractors only feel the cumulative cost years after an install have rolled past. Conversely highly reliable system hardware allows a contractor to confidently “right-size” the project price bid, helping to bring in more business in a period of industry transition.

Preventing callbacks is not only the installer’s responsibility. How the system is engineered, built, and supported all impact callbacks. FranklinWH has focused on quality products and the simplest install, with hardware held to an independently verified reliability standard. The company provides support before, during and after the installation. All of this helps to minimize callbacks. Service data shows once system complexity and hardware failure are engineered down, the dominant driver of post-install activity that remains is the installation stage itself, the real-world variability of the field. That, too, is a problem to solve at the source, not one to pass to the installer.

This paper walks through the anatomy of a callback, the hidden cost stack it creates, the actual root causes industry-wide and in our own data, and the engineering, support, and service decisions made to reduce callback risk before the truck rolls, and to absorb the cost when it can’t be avoided.

#5754. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5753. The High-Rise Bottleneck: Why EV Charging in Indian Apartments Is an Engineering Puzzle

Most Western clean-energy reporting takes a very specific picture of home charging for granted. You pull into a private driveway, plug into an attached garage wall-box, and pull cheap power while you sleep.

That reality does not exist for most urban drivers in India.

Data from Kazam and the Alliance for an Energy Efficient Economy (AEEE) shows that roughly 70% to 75% of urban households in major Indian metros live in multi-family apartments and cooperative housing societies. Parking means an open ground level slot or a space two levels down in a concrete basement. When someone here buys an electric car, their biggest hurdle isn’t battery degradation or highway chargers. It’s their own building.

As an electrical engineer in India, I watch this play out every week. Someone buys an EV, assumes they can hire a local wireman to pull power from their flat’s meter to their parking bay, and runs straight into transformer limits, cable physics, and nervous managing committees.

Here is what is actually going on beneath the floorboards.

Indian housing societies buy electricity under a bulk “sanctioned load” from their local utility (DISCOM). The utility sizes the local transformer using an electrical diversity factor the simple assumption that not all 100 flats will run their air conditioners, geysers, and induction stoves at the exact same moment.

A domestic geyser pulls 2 kW for ten minutes and shuts off. A 7.4 kW AC wall-box pulls 32 amps continuously for six to eight hours. When several residents plug in around 8:00 PM, that heavy, unbroken draw lands on the transformer right as domestic air conditioning and kitchen loads hit their evening peak.

Let’s look at the numbers on a 100-flat complex running on a standard 250 kVA transformer:

Here is where the grid cracks: just five or six cars charging at 7.4 kW pull 37 kW to 44 kW. That wipes out the 30 kW buffer completely. If eight cars plug in at once (59.2 kW), the transformer goes straight into thermal overload.

When that happens, the main incomer breaker trips plunging common areas and elevators into darkness or the utility slaps the society with massive Maximum Demand penalties on their next power bill. Getting a utility to upgrade a transformer or approve higher sanctioned load requires significant capital, transformer yard space, and months of red tape.

In a detached house, wiring an EV charger means running 5 or 10 metres of cable from your distribution board to the garage.

In an Indian high-rise, your electricity meter sits in a ground-floor panel room or inside a service shaft on the 14th floor. Your car is parked two floors beneath the earth in Basement 2. Connecting the two is an expensive engineering problem:

Even if an owner is willing to foot the entire wiring bill, they still need a No Objection Certificate (NOC) from their housing society committee or Resident Welfare Association (RWA).

Most RWA committees are volunteer residents without technical backgrounds. When handed a request for high-power continuous charging, their hesitation usually comes down to three worries:

The courts are starting to step in. In Amit Dholakia v. The State of Maharashtra (2025), the Bombay High Court pushed state authorities to issue clear guidelines preventing arbitrary NOC rejections. More recently, in early 2026, the Supreme Court of India issued notice on a Public Interest Litigation challenging arbitrary charger bans across states. While the legal momentum is clearly moving toward protecting a resident’s right to charge, taking a court ruling to a heated Sunday morning society meeting rarely makes for a quick installation.

Treating an electric car like an extra microwave where everyone pulls their own line from their domestic meter does not scale. Societies have to build shared electrical infrastructure.

India’s EV transition is moving fast on two and three wheels. But passenger electric cars won’t reach mass adoption until high-rises solve the overnight charging puzzle.

The vehicles are ready, and buyers want them. But until building codes, dynamic load balancing, and society committees catch up with automotive engineering, the hardest part of driving electric in India will simply be charging at home.

#5750. Outer Spaces takes solar beyond the rooftop with integrated backyard energy system

Residential solar is finding a new place to call home: the backyard.

Outer Spaces, a new company founded by the entrepreneurs behind outdoor living brand Outer, is introducing a solar-powered pavilion that combines photovoltaic generation, battery storage and usable outdoor living space. The company’s first product, OS1, can be designed for multiple functions, including an outdoor kitchen, office, gym or gathering space, while generating electricity and providing backup power for the home.

Outer Spaces is planning to produce outdoor structures that integrate solar PV and energy storage. Credit: Outer Spaces

The California-based company is positioning OS1 as a new category of residential energy infrastructure that goes beyond conventional rooftop arrays and garage-mounted batteries. It builds on the growing interest in solar structures that also serve a secondary purpose.

OS1 combines a solar pavilion with a finished outdoor power deck. Solar panels integrated into the overhead structure generate electricity, while a thin battery storage system is housed beneath the deck. According to Outer Spaces, the combination allows the structure to produce energy throughout the day and provide backup power during outages.

Rather than competing exclusively with traditional rooftop solar, OS1 offers homeowners an additional way to use available property for energy production, particularly when roof aesthetics, space limitations, or homeowner preferences become obstacles to conventional installations.

Outer Spaces describes OS1 as a modular system designed for rapid on-site assembly, with minimal to no excavation required. The company says installation avoids traditional construction steps such as concrete pouring and lumber cutting. Once the structure is assembled, a licensed electrician then connects its energy system to the customer’s home.

The product’s first availability is scheduled for 2027, beginning in Southern California and select additional markets; pricing is expected to be announced in early 2027.

Solar canopies and pergolas have increasingly incorporated features such as EV charging, lighting, fans and weather protection, giving homeowners additional reasons to invest in solar outside the traditional rooftop model.

Outer Spaces represents another potential avenue for expanding residential solar adoption through design-driven products. The opportunity will ultimately depend on pricing, installation economics, permitting requirements and the system’s measurable energy performance.

Outer Spaces plans to showcase OS1 publicly for the first time at RE+ 2026, Nov. 16 to 19, in Las Vegas.

#5749. QuantumScape Has An Osprey Up Its Solid-State EV Battery Sleeve

Federal policy makers orchestrated an EV sales crash in the US last year, only to see electrification stakeholders gradually piece together a comeback focusing on cars that hit the affordability MSRP of $30,000 or less. New, higher-performing solid-state EV batteries are also inching their way towards the market, with the US startup QuantumScape among the innovators contributing some fresh news in recent weeks (for the record, Finland’s Donut Labs is another).

As the name indicates, a solid-state battery replaces the liquid electrolyte of a conventional lithium-ion battery with a solid material. If that sounds like a deep electrochemical challenge, its is. Nevertheless, science loves a challenge, and so do electrification stakeholders who are determined to sharpen the contrast between EVs and gasmobiles. Compared to the current generation of EV batteries, solid-state technology meets a higher bar for energy density and other performance benchmarks, while avoiding the use of volatile chemicals (see more solid-state background here)

High tech ceramics have emerged as one of the options, and that is where QuantumScape has set its sights. The company launched in 2010 and it surfaced on the CleanTechnica radar in 2012, when Volkswagen dropped word of its interest in the solid-state forumula.

QS has been focusing its attention on developing a lithium-metal formula for the anode (the part of a battery that collects a charge), by tweaking the separator, which is the part of a battery that serves as a buffer on the electrolyte.

“A solid-state lithium-metal battery…replaces the polymer separator used in conventional lithium-ion batteries with a solid-state separator. The replacement of the separator enables the carbon or silicon anode used in conventional lithium-ion batteries to be replaced with a lithium-metal anode,” QS explains.

To ice the solid-state cake, the QS anode does not make an appearance during the battery manufacturing process. It self-forms as part of the battery’s first charging cycle.

QS has accumulated a string of investors and affiliations since 2012, capped by a hookup with the legacy firm Corning last year aimed at developing a manufacturing system that accommodates its ceramic separator.

The latest news takes QS farther into all-solid state technology, this time with a focus on the cathode, under the trade name Osprey.

“Osprey pairs the QS proprietary ceramic separator and anode-free lithium-metal design with a solid cathode, creating an all-solid-state cell,” the company explained in a press statement on November 11.

“Osprey builds on the company’s QSE platform, and opens new licensing and co-development options for current QS customers and expanding its offering to developers of sulfide-based all-solid-state batteries,” the company elaborates. The QS blog further explains that Osprey is a “solid-state platform that allows developers of sulfide-based solid-state batteries to incorporate QS technology into their all-solid-state battery (ASSB) development programs.”

Don’t get too excited just yet — the cathode work is still in the early stages of validation — but QS is now confident that its data will stand up under public scrutiny. In addition to safety improvements compared to conventional Li-ion batteries, QS checks a list of additional performance boxes for ASSBs:

“Cycle life: 1,000 full charge cycles with ~80% capacity retention under gold-standard testing conditions

Fast charging: 10% to 80% in five-minutes

Low pressure: Requires only one atmosphere of pressure

Cold-weather performance: Operational to less than -20 °C (-4 °F)

Want more details? Check out the QS blog, where the company takes a deeper dive into the new cathode.

As for why QS is working the sulfide angle, that’s a good question. “Sulfides can react with lithium metal, which shortens battery life, and they typically need to be held under high pressure to operate, which is impractical for vehicles and many other applications,” QS notes. Despite those challenges, QS also notes the sulfide formula continues to attract widespread interest in the solid-state field, due to its high level of conductivity and ease of processing. If all goes according to plan, the Osprey platform will enable solid-state battery developers to continue pursuing sulfide to their hearts’ content.

“The industry has invested heavily in sulfide-based batteries but keeps hitting the same fundamental limits,” explains QS president and CEO Dr. Siva Sivaram, “As the data shows, Osprey can address these limitations.”

As another indication that sulfide stakeholders are not out chasing rainbows, take a look at Oak Ridge National Laboratory, part of a sprawling network of research facilities under the umbrella of the US Department of Energy. ORNL has just published a new study in the journal Energy Materials under the title, “Operando neutron radiography reveals calendar aging-induced lithium redistribution and back-diffusion in thick-cathode all-solid-state batteries.”

As the title indicates, the researchers deployed a neutron radiation tool to probe the inner workings of lithium batteries while they are in operation, enabling them to deliver a precise map of the degradation process that has bedeviled the sulfide field. They also delivered a roadmap for further work:

“Based on these findings from calendar aging, we envision future formation strategies should be redesigned to minimize sustained potential gradients while stabilizing interphases, for example through optimized composite electronic percolation and catholyte or coating materials and validated with time-resolved neutron imaging to establish transport-informed, durability-first conditioning rules that generalize across cathode loadings and operating temperatures.”

Got all that? The real question is why a federal research institution is still delivering advanced analyses leading to the next generation of EV batteries that outperform conventional batteries while improving EV safety and reducing overall costs. After all, US President Donald Trump and the Republican majority in Congress have expended considerable time and effort to stop, if not reverse, EV sales in the US. In addition to prematurely killing off the $7,500 EV tax credit last fall, the President also tried (and ultimately failed) to claw back funds from the federal NEVI public EV charging station program.

Readers, take a guess at the answer in the discussion thread. One possible explanation is the energy storage angle. While the President’s whackadoodle “American Energy Dominance Policy” dials down solar and wind (especially wind), it supports practically every other domestic energy resource including nuclear, biomass, geothermal, and hydropower alongside fossil fuels, with energy storage also making a substantial appearance.

The electronic war of the future is another factor to consider, with crewless vehicles and “anti-idling” kits serving as a sort of gateway drug that demonstrates the logistical and tactical advantages of battery power, silent running, and a lower heat signature compared to diesel fuel.

Photo: The US startup Quantumscape is contributing its new “Osprey” platform to kickstart solid-state EV batteries into the mass market and help accelerate the vehicle electrification movement (screenshot courtesy of QS via YouTube).

#5748. Another Promising Study on Statins in Compensated Cirrhosis

— There are no approved treatments to prevent hepatic decompensation

Statin initiation among adults with compensated cirrhosis was associated with a lower risk of hepatic decompensation and lower overall mortality, according to a large observational study.

Among over 18,000 patients, the cumulative incidence of hepatic decompensation was 18.2% for those who took statins versus 22.7% for those who didn't take statins (adjusted subhazard ratio [aSHR] 0.86, 95% CI 0.78-0.96, P=0.008), reported Monica Tincopa, MD, of the University of California San Diego, at the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases annual meeting in Denver.

The cumulative incidence of overall mortality was 14.6% for statin users compared with 21.2% for non-users (aSHR 0.77, 95% CI 0.68-0.87, P<0.001).

Over 5 years, the number needed to treat to prevent one episode of decompensation was 25, a number that dropped to 21 over 8 years.

"Decompensation really is the turning point in cirrhosis," Tincopa said. Without approved treatments to prevent decompensation, "current options are really limited to treating the underlying causes of liver disease."

Statins offer potential anti-inflammatory and antifibrotic benefits in cirrhosis, as well as reductions in hepatic portal vein pressure. However, research on statins and decompensation prevention has delivered "highly conflicting" results, Tincopa noted.

Other observational studies have suggested that statin use is associated with lower risks of decompensation and death, including research in veterans with hepatitis C-related compensated cirrhosis. But randomized trials have shown unfavorable results. Ongoing studies such as the phase III SACRED trial of simvastatin in patients with high-risk compensated cirrhosis could bring greater clarity.

The current study included adults with cirrhosis and no prior hepatic decompensation, as determined by ICD-10 codes. Patients with liver transplant or HIV were excluded. The investigators analyzed electronic health record data from the University of California Health Data Warehouse on patients seen at affiliated health facilities from 2012 to 2026.

Statin users and non-users had to have no statin in the 90 days after a cirrhosis diagnosis. Statin users received a prescription after that 90-day lead-in period and had a 90-day qualifying window in which they had no decompensations or deaths. Follow-up for both groups began at 180 days after a cirrhosis diagnosis.

Median age at cirrhosis diagnosis was 58 years, 44% of patients were women, and 51% were white. Metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD) was the cause of cirrhosis in 73.7% of statin users and 59% of non-users, and the Model for End-Stage Liver Disease score was at least 10 in 2.9% of statin users and 9.4% of non-users. Coronary artery disease was present in 17.9% of statin patients and 3.5% of those who didn't take statins.

The median duration of statin exposure was about 2.6 years, and 75% of statins taken were lipophilic. Median follow-up time was 7.9 years after cirrhosis diagnosis.

First decompensation events were lower in statin users versus non-users, including ascites (12.7% vs 17.1%, respectively), hepatic encephalopathy (4.8% vs 6.5%), variceal bleeding (1.4% vs 2.1%), hepatorenal syndrome (0.3% vs 0.7%), and spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (0.2% vs 0.6%).

Limitations included potential residual confounding and the use of ICD-10 codes to determine cirrhosis and outcomes. The study also didn't assess statin doses or formulations.

#5747. OEDRO Car Accessories Fix Common Family Road Trip Interior Problems for SUV & Pickup Owners

Planning a family road trip usually starts with excitement. Everyone gets involved in choosing the destination, checking the route, preparing snacks, and packing luggage for the journey. Parents make sure the kids have entertainment, while other family members prepare drinks, travel essentials, and everything needed for a comfortable ride. However, amid all the preparation, some easily overlooked vehicle interior problems can turn into unexpected inconveniences along the way. A little mud brought into the cabin, difficulty climbing into a tall pickup, or small belongings scattered across the seats may not seem like serious issues before departure. Yet after several hours on the road, these minor problems can become frustrating, particularly when traveling with children or older family members. A comfortable road trip is not only about reaching the destination; it is also about making sure everyone can enjoy the journey without constantly dealing with unnecessary mess and discomfort.

Family travel puts a vehicle’s interior to the test. One of the most common challenges is keeping the floor clean. Children may step into the vehicle with muddy shoes after outdoor activities, while food crumbs, spilled drinks, and snack wrappers can quickly make the factory carpet look messy. Cleaning these stains can take time, especially after a long trip. Another issue is getting in and out of taller SUVs and pickup trucks. Although their higher ground clearance is useful for different driving conditions, the height can make entry and exit less convenient for young children and older passengers. Repeatedly climbing into the cabin may require extra effort and assistance. Finally, there is the problem of small items getting everywhere. Toys, charging cables, tissues, water bottles, and travel accessories often end up under seats or scattered around the cabin. When passengers cannot find what they need, the journey can become less organized. These problems may be ordinary, but addressing them before departure can make a noticeable difference to the overall travel experience.

A few practical interior upgrades can help address these common challenges without requiring major vehicle modifications. First, custom-fit TPE floor mats provide an additional layer of protection against dirt, mud, and everyday spills. For example, the OEDRO all-weather TPE floor mats for 2023–2025 Honda CR-V models, including Hybrid are designed for compatible vehicle interiors.

They provide a stepping surface along the side of the truck, giving passengers an additional point of support when getting into the cabin. The OEDRO running boards for 2015–2020 Ford F-150 SuperCrew are one option for owners of compatible models.

Third, under-seat storage boxes help keep smaller travel essentials in one designated place. The OEDRO 2-in-1 storage box for compatible Chevy Silverado and GMC Sierra models offers a practical way to organize items while making better use of available cabin space. Always check the product’s listed vehicle compatibility before ordering.

These accessories do not change a vehicle’s engine performance, horsepower, or driving capability. Their value lies in improving the everyday experience of using the vehicle, particularly when several passengers and plenty of belongings are involved. Floor mats help reduce the amount of dirt and moisture reaching the original carpet, making post-trip cleanup less demanding. Running boards offer a more convenient stepping point for passengers, which can be especially helpful for children and older family members when entering or leaving a taller vehicle. Meanwhile, storage boxes help prevent small belongings from taking over the cabin, allowing passengers to find their essentials more easily. Together, these upgrades support a cleaner, more accessible, and better-organized interior. They can also help drivers spend less time tidying up after every stop and more time enjoying the trip with their families. Of course, passengers should still use appropriate care when stepping onto running boards, and all loose items should be stored securely before driving. A little preparation can go a long way toward making long journeys feel more relaxed.

A successful family road trip involves more than choosing the right destination and packing enough snacks. The condition and organization of your vehicle’s interior also play an important role in keeping everyone comfortable throughout the journey. Whether you want to protect your carpet from muddy shoes, make it easier for passengers to enter a taller pickup, or keep everyday travel essentials from getting lost beneath the seats, choosing accessories that suit your vehicle can help solve these everyday problems. #5746. A BMW i3 Spent Less Than 3 Hours Charging On A 3,658-Mile Road Trip, Is That Fast Enough For You?

Regardless of how you feel about the exterior styling, interior design, infotainment software, or even driving dynamics of BMW's latest Neue Klasse models, there's no denying the new electric cars are extremely impressive when it comes to range, efficiency and charging. The in-demand Neue Klasse cars have consistently outperformed in real-world range tests, and last year BMW drove an iX3 from Debrecen, Hungary, to Munich, Germany, covering 626 miles on a single charge.

To show off just how road-trip-capable its Neue Klasse cars are, BMW took the new i3 on the Paneuropeana, a long-distance EV rally route devised by Rafael de Mestre, who has circumnavigated the globe in electric cars four times. The car covered 5,886.7 kilometers on the journey, or 3,658 miles rounded up — over 1,000 miles more than you'd drive when crossing the United States. It took 3 days, 2 hours and 41 minutes to complete the drive, and only 2 hours and 46 minutes was spent charging. Is that fast enough for you, yet?

The trip started at the North Cape in Norway, the northernmost point of continental Europe that you can get to by car (about 1,300 miles from the North Pole), and after crossing through eight countries ended in Tarifa, the southernmost point of Europe in the Andalusian region of Spain. Along the way they encounted bad weather and rough roads, so it wasn't a walk in the park. The focus of the drive isn't outright speed, so the checkpoints and stages are organized like "regular road users travelling along country roads, motorways and through towns," according to BMW.

Charging stops weren't pre-determined, though, and BMW says that made for a great challenge: "optimising the route between checkpoints specifically to make the best use of the charging infrastructure, whilst also taking into account external factors such as the weather, traffic jams, etc." The charging stations chosen were public ones, adding to the potential issues. In the video covering the trip, at one point the team got the car down to 0% with 0 kilometers of range left, and in another moment they arrive at a high-power charging station to find no chargers working.

Only one engineer was in the i3 at any given time, with the car being followed by an identical i3 and an iX3 for support. The i3 in question was a 50 xDrive model, which has a 108.7-kWh battery pack, a dual-motor powertrain with 463 horsepower, and an EPA-rated range of up to 468 miles in the States (567 miles on the European WLTP cycle).

The point of the Paneuropeana is to see who can spend the least amount of time charging along the route, and BMW broke the record by more than seven hours. The i3 can charge at up to 400 kW, and on the trip BMW saw an average charging power of 275 kW, which ain't bad. BMW says the i3's average speed was just about 50 mph, and its energy consumption was damn impressive: 12.5 kWh per 100 km, which translates to almost 5 miles per kWh in U.S. figures. That's better efficiency than the i3's WLTP rating.

BMW hasn't said how many times the i3 stopped to charge, or how long the average stop was, but does that really matter? A U.S.-spec i3 can gain 180 miles of range in just 10 minutes of charging, and it can go from 10% to 80% in 21 minutes. At steady highway speeds, the i3 should be able to take you almost 400 miles before needing to be juiced up again. That's got to be good enough to assuage any range anxiety you've still got kicking around in your brain.

#5745. I Have A 100 Mile Commute Through Mountain Roads! What Car Should I Buy?

#5744. Advances and Unmet Needs in Pediatric Sickle Cell Disease

— It's better care that has led to better survival, not new treatment options

As the 1970s came to a close, sickle cell disease (SCD) remained largely a pediatric condition because few children born in the U.S. with the condition survived to adulthood. Today, about 95% of children born with SCD in developed nations survive to adulthood. Median life expectancy in the U.S. has reached 60 years, meaning half of all patients live to that age, as compared with less than 40% just 10 years ago.

The dramatic improvement in survival evolved from an amalgamation of factors including universal screening of newborns, prophylactic penicillin to prevent bacterial infections, immunization against Haemophilus influenzae and Streptococcus pneumoniae, and broader and better-informed use of FDA-approved therapies for SCD. A recent multi-article review covered some of the key developments, as well as ongoing challenges and unmet needs.

Ironically, a clinical trial that did not meet its primary endpoint has profoundly influenced management of SCD. The randomized, placebo-controlled BABY HUG trial failed to show that hydroxyurea reduced the risk of kidney and spleen damage by 50% in very young children with SCD. Nonetheless, the trial showed that patients randomized to hydroxyurea had significantly fewer SCD-associated clinical events, including pain, dactylitis, acute chest syndrome, blood transfusion, and hospitalization. Investigators concluded that hydroxyurea "can now be considered for all very young children with sickle cell anemia."

BABY HUG was a landmark clinical trial because of the history of hydroxyurea, which was initially approved only for adults with SCD who met specific high-risk criteria. Published more than a decade ago, the trial established clinical principles that remain highly relevant today for children with SCD.

"[BABY HUG] gave us the evidence that hydroxyurea can be used as prevention, so before kids start having all these complications," Allison King, MD, MPH, PhD, of Washington University in St. Louis, told MedPage Today. "BABY HUG was really helpful because instead of being so reactive, the practice could be very proactive in pediatrics."

A recent analysis of more than 2,000 children with sickle cell anemia treated with hydroxyurea established long-term safety and efficacy. Children followed for as long as 8 years had significantly fewer and briefer hospitalizations, and laboratory data showed sustained improvement in hemoglobin in patients with good treatment adherence.

The Silent Cerebral Infarct Transfusion trial, published more than a decade ago, also had a transformative impact on SCD management. The results, in 200 children with sickle cell anemia, showed that regular blood transfusions significantly reduced the risk of silent cerebral infarction, which can have more subtle neurologic effects as compared with a stroke.

"A lot of our kids, and adults, have cognitive deficits, which are kind of like invisible disabilities because [the patients] can be quite verbal but have significant attention problems, processing speed, executive function that limit their ability to progress in their daily activities," said King. "Also, silent infarcts are a high risk for having overt strokes. Now it's an evidence-based guideline that we should be screening, as well as responding and transfusing, to prevent the progression."

Fast forward a decade and gene therapy became the next transformative clinical development in SCD, offering the potential for cure. Unfortunately, other therapeutic options for SCD have remained limited throughout the decade. One of the approved drugs, voxelotor (Oxbryta), was withdrawn from the market after post-approval data analysis and real-world clinical experience showed the benefits no longer outweighed the risks. Concern has arisen about the future status of another approved agent, crizanlizumab (Adakveo), which lost its conditional European marketing approval. Additionally, the drug failed to outperform placebo for reducing episodes of vaso-occlusive crisis in a recent phase III trial.

Moreover, cost and access remain major obstacles to expansion of gene therapy as a treatment option. In 2025, fewer than 200 patients worldwide received the two gene therapies, which come with a hefty price tag of $2-$3 million. Additionally, the process for screening and preparing patients for gene therapy can be "laborious" for many families, said King.

Despite the advances, more and more effective therapies are an ongoing need for patients with SCD, said Ofelia Alvarez, MD, of the University of Miami Health System.

"There is still a lot to do," she told MedPage Today. "The most widely used medication, hydroxyurea, does not work for all children with sickle cell anemia. Some patients do not respond. They continue to have low hemoglobin despite the treatment. We need new therapies to improve anemia, to decrease complications. They are always in need."

Better transitional care also has contributed to better survival in SCD. However, the quality of care and the ability to provide good transitional care vary greatly. Transition involves much more than handing off a patient from pediatric care to adult care, said Alvarez. Most young patients need to be educated about the disease itself, what medications are available and how they are used, and how to make appointments. Some need to learn about practical issues, such as budgeting, transportation, and learning to live independently.

"Here at the University of Miami, we have a very robust program that is co-created with patients and explained to the parents," said Alvarez. "After the transition, we follow up with the patients to see how they feel about the process and whether they have become integrated into the [adult] practice... . I personally don't have a lot of problems because I work very closely with the adult providers."

In some settings and circumstances, transition into adult care can be complicated and anything but a smooth process. The age at transition varies from practice to practice, but 18 is a common age. King, who specializes in caring for children, has expanded her patient age range up to 26. She described a 23-year-old patient whose circumstances are not rare in the SCD community.

"He has been intermittently homeless for 2 years," said King. "In his words, he 'couch surfs.' He doesn't have a stable place to live. He's not sure where he's getting his food. His meds are always being stolen, so he's frequently in the hospital for pain."

The case also illustrates the problem with using pain as a patient-reported endpoint for judging treatment efficacy, which has been a historical standard for the FDA.

"Pain is so multifactorial," said King. "It could be just sickling, and I'm not trying to minimize that, but it could be very biologically driven, but often, particularly in older teens and adults, psychosocial factors really are big drivers. That makes it difficult to tease out what's really driving a patient's pain."

Finding the origin of a patient's pain has been complicated by the fact that "for the last 20 years, we were sort of crawling our way out of the opioid crisis," said Jeffrey Glassberg, MD, of the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai in New York City.

"When I was in med school, we were taught that there was no danger to opioids," Glassberg told MedPage Today. "If you're treating pain, no harm could come to the patient. Today, I think most people who treat a lot of sickle cell patients would agree: Opioids cause pain. Full stop."

"We have noted that the more opioids that we were giving over the last couple of decades, the more pain our patients would experience, and that pain is non-vaso-occlusive," he continued. "Our latest research is really finding that patients feel that the pain is identical to vaso-occlusive crisis ... The more opioids you give, the more pain that patients will experience that's non-vaso-occlusive, but feels the same."

A number of candidate drugs for SCD have failed to pass muster with the FDA because they did not show a reduction in vaso-occlusive crises. Moving away from that as an endpoint for efficacy and toward a more objective endpoint -- such as organ damage -- might increase the likelihood of more drug approvals and more treatment options for patients, said Glassberg.

#5743. Four of Seven Dutch Green Hydrogen Projects Have Been Cancelled. The Subsidies Keep Coming.

The Netherlands awarded €783.5 million in subsidies to seven green hydrogen projects in 2022. Together they were supposed to deliver 1,150 MW of electrolysis capacity. Four years later, four projects representing 550 MW have been cancelled, and none of the original seven is operating. The November 2 parliamentary answers also confirm that the government cannot say when electrolytic hydrogen will become commercially viable without dedicated subsidies. Another €780 million is reserved for a future subsidy round anyway.

The TFIE Strategy Briefing follows what happened to the Dutch projects, what the government’s own cost and demand revisions imply, and which industrial activities could prosper without the hydrogen market originally forecast.

The €783.5 million was awarded, not spent. Roughly €150 million had been disbursed under the programme when the parliamentary answers were published. That distinction matters, especially when assessing potential losses to taxpayers. It does not make the project record look good. The Dutch state has spent four years supporting a group of plants that has so far delivered no operating capacity, while almost half the planned megawatts have been withdrawn.

The price assumptions were difficult to defend from the start. Officials say the original subsidy decisions reflected expectations that renewable hydrogen would cost around €2/kg by 2030 and become competitive with fossil-derived hydrogen within a decade. Making a kilogram by electrolysis typically requires about 50–55 kWh of electricity. Even at €50/MWh, a very cheap assumption for dependable electricity supplied to a European industrial facility, that power costs €2.50–€2.75 per kilogram before capital recovery, maintenance, water treatment, compression or delivery. Buying electricity only during the cheapest hours lowers the energy bill but leaves the electrolyser idle for longer, increasing the capital cost per kilogram produced. Eurostat’s latest industrial electricity data show how low the €50/MWh assumption is relative to ordinary non-household electricity prices, although large electrolysers would negotiate different contracts.

There are established industrial uses for hydrogen, particularly in chemicals and refining. The Dutch policy went considerably further, assuming domestic electrolysis could grow into a competitive source of energy for processes where direct electricity would often be cheaper. The cancellations have arrived alongside retreat from those anticipated markets, not as an isolated string of engineering setbacks. Lower-than-expected demand and expensive production are undermining the case for both the plants and the infrastructure intended to connect them.

The government’s September energy planning already gives hydrogen much less work in building heat and electricity generation. It also projects substantially less industrial hydrogen demand than earlier plans. Nevertheless, the proposed response includes more production subsidies, obligations for some industrial customers to buy renewable hydrogen, and measures to support a national pipeline network. Those choices keep public policy committed to volumes that commercial buyers have not shown they can afford.

The network has its own deteriorating economics. The Netherlands Court of Audit found that estimated investment had risen from €1.5 billion to €3.8 billion. Gasunie/Hynetwork Services estimated startup losses of up to €2.5 billion against a €750 million grant. These are forecasts, not bills already paid. But a pipeline system designed for a large future hydrogen market becomes harder to finance when that market contracts. Higher tariffs would make hydrogen less attractive to the customers it needs. Additional subsidies shift the burden elsewhere. The Court of Audit laid out the problem in December 2025.

Dutch chemical manufacturing, steel processing and port industries do not need to disappear as hydrogen expectations shrink. Some energy-intensive feedstocks can be produced where clean electricity is cheaper and imported for further manufacturing in the Netherlands. Industrial heat pumps and direct electric processes avoid producing and transporting hydrogen in the first place. Smaller refineries will also need less hydrogen as petroleum demand declines. These changes have different consequences for particular companies and workers, but they offer a more credible starting point for an industrial strategy than a hydrogen-volume target.

The government now faces another subsidy round with evidence it did not have in 2022: cancelled projects, higher costs, much smaller demand expectations and a pipeline investment whose customers are uncertain. It should evaluate the industrial products being made, the cost of the available decarbonization routes and the amount of hydrogen that those routes actually require before funding another large tranche of production capacity. Otherwise the next awards risk preserving yesterday’s forecasts at tomorrow’s expense.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing analysis examines the project record, subsidy assumptions, pipeline exposure and alternatives for Dutch industrial production. It includes the implications for ammonia, steel and refining that are beyond the scope of this CleanTechnica article.

#5741. What Happens in Vegas Should Inform the Rest of the Country

This research brief highlights lessons from Nevada’s workplace heat standard.

Coauthored with Barrak Alahmad and Yazan Alwadi, Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health

Most U.S. states lack formal workplace standards for dangerous heat. This long-standing gap puts millions of working people at risk every year, especially as climate change increases the intensity and duration of hot seasons across the country.

Many lawmakers and worker protection agencies are now trying to fill the gap, in part by learning from existing standards in California, Colorado, Maryland, Minnesota, Nevada, Oregon, and Washington State.

However, not all existing heat standards are created equal. Nevada’s workplace heat standard in particular lacks several evidence-based elements that are common to other states, including temperature thresholds that trigger specific safeguards. Instead, Nevada allows employers to decide at what point to provide heat protections to their workers—a choice that many industry representatives want replicated elsewhere.

This research brief uses heat complaints lodged by Nevada workers to make the case that there are, in fact, clear data-driven temperature thresholds that can be implemented across industries. These thresholds should be an essential part of any system of heat protections that includes water, rest, shade, acclimatization, and other safeguards on the job.

What Happens in Vegas Should Inform the Rest of the Country: Lessons Learned from Nevada’s Heat Complaints Database (PDF)

Article from NRDC. By Juanita Constible, Senior Advocate, Environmental Health.

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5740. Secure Solar Futures set to install 313-kW array on Virginia elementary school

Secure Solar Futures, a commercial solar contractor, will build a 313-kW solar power system at Preston Park Elementary School in Roanoke, Virginia. Construction of the system is expected to start later this year at no cost to the Roanoke Schools through a 25-year power purchase agreement with Secure Solar Futures.

A total of 531 bifacial solar panels manufactured by VSUN Solar in Houston, Texas, will be installed on the rooftop of the school, and the array is expected to offset 67% of the power used by the school annually. In its first year, the school’s solar system is projected to produce 421 MWh of clean energy, enough to power 44 average Virginia homes.

Under the PPA, the solar power equipment will be provided at no upfront cost to Roanoke Schools. For the 25-year term of the agreement, Pardee Renewable Energy and Secure Solar Futures will own, operate and maintain the solar power system and will sell the energy produced to the school division at a rate lower than the local electric utility.

Preston Park Elementary School is expected to save $581,000 electricity costs over the term of the PPA. After the conclusion of the term, Roanoke Schools can opt to take ownership of the solar power system at a nominal cost, producing its own energy for the remainder of the equipment’s lifespan.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5739. That Mystery SUV Spied In Moab Is An Audi-Bodied Test Mule For The New Scouts

A few days ago, Reddit user CapnDangerPony took to the r/whatisthiscar subreddit with a far-away photo of a very strange-looking test mule driving around in Moab. The poster only got a good photo of the side of the mule, and another from further away that gave a glimpse of its front end, neither of which revealed any details aside from a strange shape, lots of camouflage, and some sort of body-on-frame platform. Now our spies have gotten a better look at the test mule, and I know exactly what it is: This is an early prototype for the new Scouts.

Somehow, I don't think I saw a single comment online thinking that it was a Scout mule based on the Reddit photos, but that's definitely what it is. This time, it wasn't spied in Moab but at a secret Audi test facility in Germany, which is one of the few giveaways as to its identity — another obvious one being the tow hooks. I've already been surprised that Scout test mules haven't been regularly spied by now, since just this week Scout announced that it's starting to construct "validation builds" at its factory in newly blue South Carolina, and prototypes with closer-to-production bodies have apparently already been out there for months. This one is likely an older mule that's still out there racking up test miles, which is probably fine since since the Scouts have been delayed so much.

So, all of you distraught internet commenters can rest easy now. The mystery is solved. This weird test mule is for the Scout Terra and Traveler — my guess is the Traveler because of its shorter wheelbase — and despite how strange it looks, there's still some information we can glean from it.

There have been persistent rumors that Audi wants to build a competitor for the Mercedes-Benz G-Class using a modified version of the Scout's platform (and at the same factory), which seems to be at least partially why Volkswagen wanted to resurrect the brand at all, and could also be a reason for some of the trucks' delays and development problems. Audi has yet to green-light that project, so it's highly unlikely this mule is testing out a new Audi model, but the company is clearly helping out with the vehicle's engineering.

It is wearing an Audi body, though, specifically one from a Q6 E-Tron. It's basically just a placeholder, so Scout can test out the platform and powertrain without needing to put a real design on top yet. It's got huge fender flares tacked on, and you can see that the wheelbase is longer than the Q6's, with the wheels sitting in cut-outs made in the wheel arches. There's clearly a big battery pack under there, with rudimentary side steps added on in a similar fashion to the GMC Hummer EV, and the chunky six-lug wheels wear BFGoodrich all-terrain tires.

The rear tow hooks are just likes the ones on the Scout show trucks, as are the overall shapes of the bumpers, and the bits we can see of this mule's suspension setup appear to align with what Scout has shown us of its architecture. There's no tells as to whether this test mule is running the fully electric setup or is the range-extender hybrid model that will use a four-cylinder Volkswagen engine behind the rear axle; it's likely the latter, as the vast majority of Scout reservations have been for the EREV, and Scout will start production with them.

Scout first broke ground on its plant in Blythewood, South Carolina, two and a half years ago, with the project being part of a $2 billion investment that hopes to create more than 4,000 jobs. Now the company says it's starting early validation builds "to validate vehicle engineering, buildability and parts maturity while confirming that newly installed equipment is set up and functioning as expected." The company has started welding, assembly and painting operations, and in the background of a released photo we can see a couple bodies-in-white of the Traveler SUV.

750 robots help to put together 160 individual parts using 5,000 weld points, and don't worry, there's "AI-supported weld monitoring" so that everything is perfect. An "advanced metrology system" is also used to match the body against the engineering specs, checking thousands of measurements. Scout says the body shop can process up to 40 vehicles per hour, but right now the company is just testing things to make sure it all works properly before real manufacturing starts. Next it's sent through "an electrified automotive paint shop" before the truck goes to assembly, where the painted body is attached to the chassis and humans put all the components together.

The company says "validation vehicle builds will continue and mature into 2027 as Scout Motors prepares for its next phase of pre-production vehicle builds. As engineering, buildability and parts maturity advance, the manufacturing team will progress from equipment set up and functionality to proving manufacturing processes and ultimately scaling those processes toward production speed." There's no word as to updated production timelines beyond that, apart from Scout saying first deliveries are planned to start "in 2028." According to MotorTrend, the company has 210,000 pre-orders with that number expected to increase to 400,000, or about 12 to 18 months of confirmed sales, and executives deny any further delays.

#5738. Response to Standard PBC Treatment at 1 Year Linked to Lower Long-Term Risks

— "Response predicts outcomes in real-world care, and nonresponders carry a risk," researcher says

A biochemical response to ursodeoxycholic acid (UDCA) at 12 months was associated with lower 5-year risks of hepatic decompensation and mortality in patients with primary biliary cholangitis (PBC), a propensity-matched landmark cohort study suggested.

Among patients considered to be responders -- defined as an alkaline phosphatase (ALP) level ≤130 U/L and a bilirubin level ≤1.0 mg/dL -- the 5-year cumulative incidence of hepatic decompensation was 10.2% versus 17.8% among nonresponders (HR 0.60, 95% CI 0.51-0.70, P<0.001), reported EzzElDien A. Ibrahim, MD, MSc, of the University of Missouri in Columbia, at the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases annual meeting in Denver.

In addition, the cumulative incidence of 5-year all-cause mortality was 12.2% among responders versus 16.3% for nonresponders (HR 0.74, 95% CI 0.65-0.84, P<0.001).

Other clinical outcomes also trended in favor of or significantly favored responders:

"Response predicts outcomes in real-world care, and nonresponders carry a risk," Ibrahim said. "We can check ALP and bilirubin at 1 year on UDCA," and at that point, clinicians should consider "escalating nonresponders without delay."

UDCA is the first-line treatment for PBC, a rare and chronic autoimmune disease of the bile ducts, "and it ... works," Ibrahim noted.

An international cohort study that included 3,902 patients from the Global PBC Study Group database showed that after inverse probability treatment weighting, the 10-year cumulative liver transplant-free survival rate was 79.7% among UDCA-treated patients and 60.7% among untreated patients, with UDCA treatment resulting in a statistically significantly reduced risk of liver transplant or death (HR 0.46, 95% CI 0.40-0.52, P<0.001).

However, 20% to 40% of patients do not respond biochemically to UDCA at 12 months. In 2024, seladelpar (Livdelzi) and elafibranor (Iqirvo) were granted accelerated approval for patients with PBC who either did not respond to UDCA or were intolerant to the treatment.

While a number of criteria such as the PARIS criteria link 12-month response to UDCA to outcomes, Ibrahim said real-world validation in routine care for 5-year clinical endpoints -- such as decompensation and death -- is limited.

This explains "why this study matters," he noted.

For this study, Ibrahim and colleagues used the TriNetX U.S. Research Network that includes data on 147.5 million patients from 112 health systems.

They included matched cohorts of 2,911 patients with PBC. Mean age was 61.3, 87% were women, 77% were white, 34% had a cirrhosis code, 14% had portal hypertension, and mean albumin level was 3.86-3.91 g/dL.

Responders and nonresponders to UDCA were categorized based on propensity score matching of 29 covariates that included demographics, liver and mental health comorbidities, alanine aminotransferase levels, aspartate aminotransferase levels, platelet counts, albumin levels, and international normalized ratio.

Ibrahim acknowledged that the study had a number of limitations, including the fact that PBC was defined by codes and not by a positive anti-mitochondrial antibody test or histology. In addition, there was no access to UDCA dose or adherence data, and there were a limited number of 5-year events for the liver transplant and hepatocellular carcinoma analyses.

#5737. BYD Is Combining Solar Energy Storage, Five-Minute Flash Charging, And Robotic Arms For Its New Charging Stations

Despite not selling any cars in the U.S., China's BYD is quietly taking over the EV world. It has surpassed Tesla as the world's largest electric carmaker by global sales, and it's also been hard at work trying to dethrone Tesla as the provider of the world's most advanced EV charging network. Now it's announced the latest big step toward that goal. On Tuesday, BYD announced that it will combine clean solar power, its proprietary Flash Charging tech that uses local energy storage, and robotic arms into one EV charging system.

The core of this system is BYD's Flash Charging, which draws power from the grid like any other EV charging station. Unlike others, though, it stores the electricity in two on-site 185-kilowatt batteries. That stored energy can then be pumped into your EV way quicker than it would be if it were drawing from the grid directly, BYD says.

Each Flash Charger has two connectors, capable of up to 1,500 kW charging if only one is in use, or 1,000 kW if two cars are sharing. For context, Tesla's new V4 Superchargers top out at 500 kW, regular superchargers hit 250 kW, and the fastest DC chargers from Electrify America can do 350 kW. BYD's quoted charge times are 10% to 70% in five minutes, 10% to 97% in nine minutes, and in cold weather, 20% to 97% in 12 minutes given an ambient temperature of minus 30 degrees Fahrenheit.

BYD is now incorporating solar generation into the equation, drawing power from the sun to those same local 185-kW batteries, presumably via solar panels placed on top of the charging station's canopy. This means the system is less reliant on the grid, which is huge in remote, sun-soaked regions like, say, the desert in the United Arab Emirates where BYD launched all this.

Then there are the robotic arms. The idea is far from new or original — Tesla promised them a decade ago, and EV makers like Volkswagen, Hyundai, and China's Xiaomi have since built their own renditions. It's a simple premise: A robotic arm locates your car's charging port, opens the cover, and inserts the connector automatically.

BYD plans to install 6,000 Flash Charging stations worldwide by the end of next year. Don't expect all or even most of them to get solar power or the robot arms initially, but if BYD can actually normalize five-minute charging with solar and robot arms sometime in the next couple of years, we might be looking at the new charging network king.

#5736. 1970 Buick Wildcat For Sale With 31,908 Miles Shown Offers 455 V8 With One-Owner History

Some cars don't do small. Or economical. They're big-block-toting, street-sailing cruise ships with little regard for petite parking spaces. Tight garages need not apply. That's exactly where the Buick Wildcat lives. At almost 18 and a half feet long, a 1970 Wildcat fits the land yacht bill, though it doesn't fit into many other places. It's a testament to the automotive excess of the era, right down to its huge, 455-cubic-inch V8. Make no mention of how the average MPG in the '70s compares to today. Still, even in a time when efficiency is important to the average car buyer, collectors and enthusiasts want the big ol' boats. If that describes you, this surviving B-body muscle cruiser is up for grabs.

This 1970 Buick Wildcat is clean, too. Original paint? Check. Factory interior? Also yes. Heck, the seller says this big cat has belonged to the same owner since they bought it new over half a century ago. Of course, you might expect a 56-year-old car to have some serious mileage on its clock. Not this one. It's listed in Mount Clemens, Michigan, with just 31,908 original miles. If you've got the garage space, this old Buick needs a driver to rack up some mileage.

What's not to love? First off, it's called the "Wildcat." Certainly cool enough to land Buick on the list of automakers that have the coolest model names, according to our readers. This one, posted for sale by Michael's Auto Sales in Mount Clemens, Michigan, is something of a time capsule. The 1970 Buick Wildcat wears the original Sherwood Green Iridescent shade, along with a similarly verdant original interior. The original seats and interior treatments survived the years, right down to the door sills and green factory Buick carpeting. Even the climate control selector and radio are delightfully original. Speaking of climate control, this one has air conditioning, an option you could have selected from the factory back in 1970.

Then there's the lump under the hood. It's a 455-cubic-inch, naturally aspirated big block V8. That's eight cylinders and 7.4 liters of displacement. Displacement so cavernous, in fact, it puts the Buick's 455-cubic-inch mill among the biggest V8 engines ever put into production cars. That engine, a first for the Le Sabre-based 1970 Wildcat, had a factory horsepower rating of 370. Doesn't sound like a lot for such a massive V8, but it also produced a stump-pulling 510 pound-feet of torque.

Don't go thinking you're going to find a manual gearbox with which to row that behemoth 455-cubic-inch V8, though. This one sports a three-speed Turbo Hydra-Matic automatic transmission. Don't expect an immaculate, concours-condition car, either. For instance, there's a little damage on the hood breaking up the expanses of Sherwood Green Iridescent paintwork. If anything, this Wildcat is a solid, well-preserved driver. It does, however, come with original documentation, including a detailed ownership history.

As of this writing, the Wildcat is up for sale for $29,995. Not too shabby for a big-body American muscle cruiser, especially one in this condition. It is a bit pricier than the average Wildcat of this vintage, though. Hagerty puts the average 1970 Buick in excellent condition at about $12,833. And that's the high end of the value range. Drop down to an example in good condition, and that average value drops to $8,367 on the high side. Still, your average 56-year-old Wildcat isn't likely to have a mileage this low.

A 1970 Buick Wildcat convertible, on the other hand, is worth a bit more. A ragtop in excellent condition is closer to $32,700. A drop top in good condition drops things down to around $21,633 on the high side of the value range.

It is rare that a 1970 Buick Wildcat like this one would live its many years without updates and modifications? Definitely, but it's much wilder that this big ol' classic cat has such a low odometer reading. It's true; this one-owner Wildcat shows just 31,908 miles – every one of which the seller says are original. That's an average of less than 570 miles per year. Somebody, please drive this big green B-body; it's waited long enough.
