#5735. Trump Officials Will Fund New Research on Controversial Psychedelic Drug Ibogaine

— Veterans groups, psychedelic advocates say the drug has great promise for mental heath conditions

Federal officials on Thursday announced new funding to explore the medical potential of ibogaine, a controversial psychedelic drug championed by veterans, conservative lawmakers, and podcasters aligned with President Donald Trump.

Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. said his department is awarding up to $4.9 million to prepare for the first U.S. studies of ibogaine for post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), a hard-to-treat condition linked to higher suicide and substance abuse rates among veterans.

The money will not go directly to patient research, Kennedy said, but to lay the groundwork needed to manufacture, dose, and design studies of the drug.

Ibogaine is made from a shrub native to West Africa. It can cause intense hallucinations, nausea, vomiting, tremors, and sometimes deadly irregular heart rhythms. Ibogaine is currently illegal, in the same ultra-restrictive class as LSD, ecstasy, and most other psychedelic drugs.

Veterans organizations and psychedelic advocates contend that ibogaine has great promise for intractable conditions such as PTSD, severe depression, and opioid addiction. U.S. research on the drug has essentially been frozen for decades due to ibogaine's restricted status.

Kennedy said the government will receive data and treatment guidance from Beond Inc., a Texas-based company that has treated more than 3,000 patients with ibogaine in Mexico, where the drug is not illegal.

"That is going to allow us to dramatically accelerate the clinical trial process," Kennedy said. "We're going to have, I think, a record drug approval process."

Additional funding awards may explore ibogaine's use for addiction, he said.

Veterans Affairs Secretary Doug Collins, who joined Kennedy for the announcement, said the collaborative effort would focus first on safety and "best-in-class research."

"For veterans living with PTSD, we owe them both urgency and the best science we can produce," Collins said.

Kennedy and Collins have previously voiced support for the potential of psychedelics, citing the experiences of veterans who have traveled abroad for therapy with ibogaine and other mind-altering substances.

Thursday's announcement was previewed in April, when Trump signed an executive order directing federal agencies to speed up review and research of psychedelics that could help with mental health conditions.

Three applications for psychedelics are pending review at the FDA. They include the first potential medicines made from psilocybin, the active ingredient in magic mushrooms, and MDMA, also called ecstasy. Decisions on those drugs could come within weeks or months.

Because of its safety risks, ibogaine research has moved more slowly.

The little data currently available mostly come from patients who have traveled to Mexico or other countries where ibogaine is not heavily regulated. Ibogaine clinics typically charge $10,000 to $20,000 for one course of treatment, which often involves monitoring of a patient's heart readings while emergency medical workers are on standby.

Trump's order on psychedelics came after podcaster Joe Rogan said he texted Trump about ibogaine, which he repeatedly has discussed on his show. According to Rogan, the president quickly responded: "Sounds great. Do you want FDA approval? Let's do it."

#5733. At $2,600, Is This 1999 Ford Expedition XLT Worth The Trek?

In today's used car market, it's hard to find a vehicle that offers more bang (or space) for your buck than Ford's Expedition. Today's Nice Price or No Dice '99 XLT might be a prime example, but, as always, you'll get the final word on that.

Daimler-Benz deserves kudos for being one of the few stalwart automakers to still sell a traditional estate model here in the U.S. Mercedes has offered a wagon on the E-Class platform for nearly half a century, beginning with the W123 in 1977. Yesterday, we looked at that model's prodigy of nearly two decades later (and with only one major model change): a 1995 Mercedes-Benz E320 Estate.

That car came to us in stately silver, with the promise of proper maintenance and no mechanical issues. It also had all the wagon-y goodness one could want, with a spacious load area and a fold-up rear-facing third row to take us all back to our formative years. That was all enough to make the classic Benz a solid contender at its $10,250 asking price. We all circled our wagons and awarded the longroof a razor-thin 51% 'Nice Price' win.

Yesterday's Mercedes held a lot of appeal, with its classic proportions, legendary build quality, and status-symbol three-pointed star hood ornament. What if, however, you needed something similar, but with vastly more room inside, more pedestrian mechanicals to make maintenance easier, and a price tag that eschewed any sense of status-seeking? If those criteria fit your expectations and budget (more on that in a minute), then the 1999 Ford Expedition XLT 4X4 we have under adjudication today should fit the bill.

Ford introduced the Expedition for the 1997 model year, basing it on the then-new F-Series pickup chassis and styling it to look like the company's wildly popular, and more fun-sized Explorer. Nominally, the Expedition replaced the long-in-the-tooth two-door Bronco in Ford's sales-leading truck lineup. Sorry, O.J. It also finally gave Ford a direct competitor to Chevrolet's long-running Suburban three-row SUV, something the company had never offered before.

The mad mix of solid F-Series stoutness and Explorer-curious-buyer-friendly style proved a winning combination for the Expedition, and even more so for the Lincoln Navigator that it spawned, the model that arguably has kept the lights on at Ford's fancy-pants brand for more than three decades.

This Expedition comes in appliance white over an institutional but dutifully long-wearing gray mousefur-and-plastic interior. There's nothing particularly fancy or luxurious about this large SUV, which is laudable for anyone seeking basic but dang-big transportation. And dang-big it is.

If you were buying Fords at Costco, this would be the model the box stores would offer, and you'd probably have to buy two of them, connected by a plastic grab handle. The deets are: a 119-inch wheelbase, supporting over 200 inches of overall length. With over six and a half feet of overall width, the truck offers more than 63 inches of interior shoulder room for stretching out or for road trip games of slug bug.

This Expedition is claimed to have a V8, but the ad doesn't specify which V8 is under its hood. Standard equipment was Ford's 4.6-liter SOHC Triton V8 offering 215 horsepower and 290 pound-feet of torque. As an option, buyers could opt for a 5.4-liter displacement edition making 230 horses and raising torque output to 325 pound-feet.

Whether or not the engine makes a difference at this Expedition's price (more on that in a minute) will have to be seen. What we do know is that behind the engine is a standard four-speed automatic transmission and, as this is a 4X4 edition, a two-speed Borg-Warner transfer case feeding Ford's ControlTrac pushbutton 4WD.

According to the ad, that drivetrain has confidently pushed the truck to 143,279 miles, and it seems ready for even more, as the seller claims the truck is mechanically sound and has no warning lights nagging from the instrument cluster. The exterior is free of dents or significant paint failure, and it rolls on factory alloys fitted with tires with plenty of life left. The cabin looks just as serviceable as it is unexciting, with only a cracked, worn console armrest as an obvious sign of age and use. This truck has all the basics covered, too, with front and rear climate control, cruise, power windows and locks, and what looks like a towing package. A clean title and get-out-of-jail-free card from the smog tester are additional check marks in this Expedition's favor.

Okay, so that's all well and good, but what would something this size and with these capabilities rightfully cost? This Expedition—which appears to do it all—asks $2,600. See? I told you we would get to that value proposition. This isn't an outlier, either: everywhere you look, prices on old Expeditions are down in the bottom of the well with poor, unfortunate Timmy.

For all of us who have lamented that the entry point for the used car market has (like everything) risen substantially since the pandemic, here is a class of vehicles that the valuation tsunami passed by unaffected. It's like discovering a valley of big, dopey dinosaurs right in your own backyard. Two reasons these Fords are likely so cheap are that they get really bad fuel economy, averaging around 15 mpg no matter what, and that, regardless of displacement, the Triton V8s fitted to these models have a reputation for spitting out spark plugs like expectorated loogies. Both issues are expensive considerations in themselves.

Regardless, we now need you to choose whether this Expedition is worth even that $2,600 asking, or if it's best to pass on the big—but cheap—beast.

Sacramento, California, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5732. 2027 Acura Integra Type S Gets A Type R-Inspired Heritage Edition

The Acura Integra Type S is, unquestionably, one of my favorite cars on the market right now, if not my outright favorite car on the market, full stop. #5731. SOLRITE and sonnen collaborate on integrated VPP breaker

SOLRITE and sonnen plan to digitally integrate VPP smart breakers into what the companies are calling the SOLRITE sonnen VPP. The expanded VPP Node combines sonnenHub with ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart breaker technology to manage major household loads. The VPP smart breaker, controlled by ADAPT Energy, can also participate in on-grid VPP events, enabling the same electrical infrastructure to be used for both backup and grid participation.

“Our work with ADAPT Energy is a meaningful step forward in how we think about residential battery storage and VPP participation,” said Regan George, CEO and founder of SOLRITE Energy. “We have already demonstrated the tremendous value of connecting sonnen batteries together as a pooled grid asset. Now, we’re going inside the home and unlocking another layer of flexibility that has been sitting there all along. By combining the battery with additional load management made possible by the VPP smart breaker technology, we’re creating a more valuable node for the grid while also giving homeowners a clever backup solution.”

The system can shed large loads without requiring those circuits to be relocated. ADAPT Energy keeps the smart breakers synchronized with the sonnen battery during backup mode and automatically restores the breakers when grid power returns.

This integration is designed to manage whole-home electrical loads in the background, not requiring homeowners to reset circuits during an outage.

“This is about making whole-home backup work the way homeowners expect it to,” said Troy Morgan, president of PanTech Design, the developer of ADAPT Energy. “Instead of putting the pressure on homeowners to change the way their home is wired, and to manually manipulate loads during an outage, we’re putting intelligence into their energy system, which provides a seamless and automatic backup power experience for their home. Our technology empowers home loads to supply optimal backup power during an outage or provide valuable flexibility to the grid.”

When a VPP event happens, the sonnen battery can discharge or charge with direct load shedding via ADAPT. ADAPT Energy will strictly shed pre-approved household loads. A customer who has opted to include a pool pump, for example, can have that load temporarily turned off while the sonnen battery is discharging to the grid.

“With the addition of ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart-breaker technology to the SOLRITE–sonnen ecosystem, we are expanding the virtual power plant from a battery-centered resource into a fully orchestrated home-energy platform,” said Blake Richetta, chairman and CEO of sonnen. “By synchronizing energy that has been harnessed in our battery with intelligent, real-time control of household electrical loads, we can deliver more flexible, responsive capacity to the grid precisely when it is needed most. This integration materially strengthens the value of SOLRITE’s virtual power plant—supporting grid reliability, creating greater customer value, and enabling SOLRITE to continue delivering industry-leading solar-plus-storage VPA solutions to homeowners.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5729. What's Your Weirdest Non-USDM Car Spotting Moment?

If you're like me, car spotting is worth getting excited about, especially when you see something that wasn't originally sold in your country. There are few things more fun than seeing a really cool, really rare or, in some cases, really weird car that may have no business being in the U.S., snapping a few pictures, sending it to your other car-loving friends and saying something dumb like, "The automotive gods have looked kindly upon me today." Maybe that last part is just me, but you get my point, and it's what brings me to today's question.

I want to know what your weirdest non-U.S. domestic market car spotting moment is. What car that was never meant to be driven in the U.S. made you stop dead in your tracks and take a few photos when you saw one Stateside? The thing is, I'm not asking for the time you saw some sort of supercar over here that isn't technically sold in America. No, I'm looking for boring, strange and otherwise mundane cars that no one in their right mind would bring to America, but someone in their wrong mind did anyway.

The inspiration for this question is a vehicle I saw over four years ago and still think about almost every day. I couldn't tell you what I was doing in Bethesda, Maryland, at 10 a.m. on June 14, 2022, but it's a time and place that'll live in infamy, because that's where and when I saw an utterly unremarkable Škoda Roomster traveling along one of Maryland's many roads. Other than the fact its standard Virginia license plate was zip-tied onto the European license plate bracket, there was nothing about this Roomster that would give away that it was something weird to the uninitiated.

It's one of the few times I've ever pulled my phone out while driving, because I almost couldn't believe what I was seeing. It's not like Škodas are even sold in Mexico or Canada. Whoever brought this over brought it a very, very long way, and I thank them for their service. Again, I can't remember what I was doing, but I remember being in enough of a rush that I couldn't stop to ask the driver what their whole deal was — something I'd otherwise do — and it's a regret I'll live with forever.

Anyway, that's enough for me. How about you all drop on down below and share your weirdest non-USDM car spotting moments? I'll be handing out bonus points to folks who can provide photographic evidence of their findings, Big Year birding-style.

#5727. Prescriptions Down 25%, Overdoses Cut in Half: What Stopped a Gabapentinoid Surge?

— Study identifies three interventions that may have influenced pregabalin use

High-profile media coverage of the harms associated with the gabapentinoid pregabalin (Lyrica) and tighter safeguards coincided with fewer prescriptions and overdoses in Australia, a retrospective study showed.

Following a media campaign, tighter dispensing rules, and boxed warnings, pregabalin prescriptions and overdoses dropped significantly below projected trends -- falling by 25% and 47%, respectively -- reported Rose Cairns, PhD, of the University of Sydney, and co-authors in The BMJ.

This was a reversal from a steep upward trajectory that had persisted since 2013, the researchers noted. The shift corresponded to ongoing monthly reductions in prescriptions of 0.7% (95% CI 0.5-0.9) and in poison center overdose calls of 1.6% (95% 1.0-2.3).

Australia historically has had a big problem with pregabalin use and overdose, and many other countries face similar issues, Cairns noted.

"Importantly, the interventions that were associated with a decrease in harms included things like raising public awareness of the risks via the media and boxed warnings. These relatively 'soft' interventions can still have an impact," she told MedPage Today.

The findings are "really positive, but we think use of pregabalin is still far too high," Cairns continued. "There's a lot of off-label use for conditions with minimal evidence to back that up. And while high-risk use has decreased, we really need to continue to minimize co-use with opioids, which has been shown to increase the risk of death."

Gabapentinoids -- pregabalin and gabapentin -- were originally developed as antiseizure drugs. Subsequent indications have expanded to include neuropathic pain, postherpetic neuralgia, and fibromyalgia.

"Despite this relatively narrow range of approved indications, gabapentinoids are considered 'blockbuster' drugs," Cairns and colleagues wrote. "High-income countries have the highest rates of gabapentinoid consumption, while low- and middle-income countries have had the highest consumption growth in recent years. Most of this consumption is driven by off-label use for non-registered indications."

In the U.S., pregabalin is scheduled under the Controlled Substances Act. Gabapentin is not scheduled at the federal level but eight states have enacted legislation to classify gabapentin as a schedule V controlled substance.

In 2019, the FDA warned that respiratory depression or death may occur in patients taking pregabalin or gabapentin with opioid pain medicines or other drugs that depress the central nervous system. Despite this, co-prescribing of gabapentinoids and opioids persists.

In the U.K., one-third of substance use deaths involve a gabapentinoid, predominantly pregabalin, and deaths involving gabapentinoids have more than doubled from 2018 to 2022, Cairns and co-authors noted.

The study in Australia is "timely and rigorous, but these figures warrant caution," noted David Juurlink, MD, PhD, of the Sunnybrook Health Sciences Centre in Toronto, in an accompanying editorial. "The ecological design cannot establish causation, and poison center calls are an imperfect measure of poisoning incidence, influenced by reporting practices and patterns of healthcare use," he pointed out.

Pregabalin should be used more selectively, Juurlink argued. "Although it can provide meaningful relief in some patients with neuropathic pain or fibromyalgia, most do not achieve substantial pain relief," he wrote.

"For other painful conditions, the evidence is even less encouraging," Juurlink added. "Randomized trials and meta-analyses have found no convincing evidence of benefit for low back pain or sciatica, conditions for which the drug is nevertheless frequently prescribed. Pregabalin's commercial success, in other words, has far outstripped its demonstrated clinical utility."

To evaluate pregabalin use and misuse in Australia, Cairns and colleagues evaluated dispensing claims and poison center overdose data from 2016 to 2024, focusing on three key events: widespread media reporting about the harms of pregabalin in December 2018, tighter prescribing restrictions after April 2020, and the introduction of boxed warnings in February 2021.

The researchers randomly sampled 10% of Australians with drug subsidies and 50% of calls placed to the country's largest poison control center. During the study period, 227,864 people were dispensed 3,441,851 pregabalin prescriptions, and 4,871 pregabalin overdose calls were reported.

Use and overdoses peaked in 2018 before plateauing and declining. Dispensing dropped significantly at three time points: in December 2018 (corresponding to high-profile press coverage), in May 2020 (after tighter prescribing restrictions were introduced), and in December 2021 (after boxed warnings were in effect).

The findings are observational, the researchers acknowledged. The study analyzed subsidized prescriptions only and poisons center data may be subject to reporting bias, they added. The researchers also could not determine patients' access to pain treatments.

#5726. Managing Offshore Wind in the Face of Uncertainty

This report lays out key adaptive management principles and recommendations for regulators.

Despite current headwinds from the Trump administration, offshore wind energy is essential for transitioning away from harmful fossil fuels to a clean energy economy. Offshore wind could provide more than three times the electricity required to power the United States, giving the country an opportunity to fight climate change, reduce local and regional air and water pollution, and grow a new industry that supports thousands of high-quality careers.

While the need for clean energy is urgent, we can and must ensure that all offshore wind in the United States, including floating offshore wind systems, are developed responsibly and in a manner that avoids, minimizes, mitigates, and monitors for adverse impacts to biodiversity and coastal and marine ecosystems. “Adaptive management” is crucial for meeting the dual goals of facilitating a rapid transition to renewable energy while minimizing impacts to the ocean in the face of uncertainty—and continuing to do so over time.

This report explores how adaptive management can be implemented in offshore wind development, provides examples of its use in other energy sectors today, and offers clear recommendations for agencies and policymakers.

#5725. Elon Musk Won't Let A Blue Wave Stop His SpaceX-Tesla Merger

Happy Friday! It's November 13, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition we're looking at the continued efforts for a SpaceX-Tesla merger, and Mercedes dialing back its Germanity. We'll also look at EV sales in the U.S., and a BMW recall.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

Elon Musk wants to merge his biggest companies, giving Robinhood addicts a single ticker to watch to satisfy their obsession with Musk's weird antics. Tesla and SpaceX are still figuring out how to make a deal, despite the midterm blue wave which could meaningfully impede the merger. From Reuters:

Nov 12 (Reuters) – SpaceX and Tesla are progressing in their merger negotiations, and a ​deal could be announced as early as ‌December, Bloomberg News reported on Thursday, citing people familiar with the matter.

The proposed merger would unify Elon Musk's ​AI, robotics, EV and aerospace ambitions under ​a single corporate roof, though it faces steep ⁠regulatory hurdles over SpaceX's US defense contracts ​and Tesla's reliance on China.

We've known this deal was coming for a while, but it'll be interesting to watch how newly blue regulators approach it. Will they take the midterm elections as a mandate to stand against business consolidation, or will they rubber-stamp the merger of a U.S. government contractor in order to show business donors they're not all bad?

Mercedes-Benz, noted German automaker, has decided that being German is just too expensive. The company's latest round of negotiations with unions hasn't yielded any cost savings, so the company is now halting new spending on its German factories — and considering closing some altogether. From Automotive News:

FRANKFURT — Mercedes-Benz is freezing investment in its German plants and exploring site closures after failing to agree with worker representatives on labor cost cuts.

The automaker made the move after talks with its works council and union stalled, according to an interview with executive board members shared on the company's intranet.

Mercedes' production chief Michael Schiebe said in the interview that management had "jointly decided to halt all further investments in our German locations."

Mercedes' reasoning is that labor in Germany is more expensive than in Eastern Europe or the United States, which is the sort of reasoning an employer would use to evade such considerations like cost of living changes. I imagine the union members involved will have something to say about losing their jobs entirely.

The state of electrification in the United States is dire, as legacy automakers rush to cancel their electric models in the wake of Republicans canceling the EV tax credit last year. EV sales are dropping, relative to the broader U.S. car market, and no automaker can hold a candle to Tesla's electric sales dominance. One automaker, though, is actually climbing the rankings: Rivian. From Automotive News:

Auto industry newcomers Tesla and Rivian are taking over the U.S. electric vehicle market following last year's repeal of the federal tax credit, as traditional brands turn to gas models and hybrids, analysts said.

While EV registrations fell 44 percent in September compared with the record set a year earlier, Tesla registrations slipped a relatively modest 27 percent and Rivian posted a gain, the most recent data from Mobility Global showed. The data does not include hybrids.

Rivian's share of the EV market in September rose to 7.6 percent, a gain of 4.5 percentage points. Combined, the two brands captured nearly two-thirds of all EV registrations for the month.

I've liked the Rivians I've driven a lot, and I'm glad to see the company become the second-best-selling EV maker in the States. Hopefully that's enough to get Rivian on sounder financial footing — I want the company to last long enough that I can buy a cheap used R1S in ten years and still get spare parts.

Ah, the vaunted Fourth Gear Recall Spot returns. Not Ford this time, though — today, it's BMW's turn in the box. Even worse, the company is re-recalling previously "fixed" cars, which can now catch fire while turned off. That's generally not ideal. From Reuters:

#5724. Data Centers, AI, & the Growing Demand for Energy: Impacts, Risks, & Solutions

Learn how the rapid growth of data centers is affecting electricity bills, public health, communities, and the environment and what can be done about it.

Data centers, the physical infrastructure powering the rapid growth of artificial intelligence and our increasingly digital lives, are expanding at an unprecedented pace and scale around the world. This growth is creating enormous new demand for electricity and other resources, with consequences for communities and the environment. In the United States, where data center development is surging, unchecked growth could raise electricity bills for families and small businesses, strain power grids and water supplies, increase pollution, and slow progress toward a clean energy future.

Communities should not be left to bear the costs of this expansion without a meaningful voice in the decisions that affect them. People deserve transparency and a real say in whether and how data centers are built. The wealthy technology companies driving this growth should pay for the infrastructure their facilities require rather than shifting those costs onto the public, bring new clean energy to meet new electricity demand, and meet strong protections for public health and the environment.

Linked below are fact sheets, expert blogs, guides, and other resources that explore the impacts of data center development in the United States and around the world as well as solutions that can help ensure people and the planet remain at the center of the decisions ahead.

Asking ChatGPT a quick question might feel innocuous, but you might end up paying for it in the long run. The massive data centers that are popping up around the country to support the AI boom are using up enormous amounts of energy and water and creating noise and air pollution. NRDC’s Ben Schaefer, senior manager of strategic communications, and Jackson Morris, director of state power sector policy, dive into how these centers can impact nearby communities and your energy bill.

Read various reports, analyses, blogs, and backgrounds here.

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5723. Major Change to Prostate Cancer Screening Guidelines

— ACS now recommends PSA testing every 2 years for average-risk men ages 50 to 69

Shifting gears on prostate cancer screening, the American Cancer Society (ACS) now recommends screening with prostate-specific antigen (PSA) every 2 years for average-risk men ages 50 to 69.

Since the release of its previous guideline 16 years ago, the ACS had recommended that average-risk men consult with a physician before deciding for themselves whether to undergo a screening PSA test.

The new ACS recommendations also strengthened guidelines for men at high risk for prostate cancer, recommending annual screening beginning at age 45 for Black men and those with a strong family history of the disease. The organization previously recommended that those men discuss the pros and cons of screening with a physician at age 40 or 45.

The changes reflect the accumulation of stronger evidence that screening PSA tests reduce prostate cancer mortality and diagnosis of late-stage disease, according to the updated guidelines from Robert Smith, PhD, ACS senior vice president for early cancer detection science, and colleagues, appearing in CA: A Cancer Journal for Clinicians.

"We've just reached a point in our understanding of prostate cancer, especially that [screening] can reduce the risk of being diagnosed with metastatic disease and dying of prostate cancer," Smith told MedPage Today. "We have good protocols for avoiding harms."

Smith noted that the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) has not updated their prostate cancer screening guidelines since 2018. For average-risk men, the USPSTF recommends discussion-backed decisions on PSA testing for men ages 55 to 69.

"I cannot tell you what [their recommendations] would be if they updated them today, but they did not have the data in 2018 that we had in 2025 with the update of the European trial data showing the endurance of the mortality reduction," said Smith.

The update aligns with recent evidence supporting PSA screening for prostate cancer as "one of the most important steps men can take to protect their health," said Ash Tewari, MD, of the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai in New York City.

"We have strong evidence that PSA testing can reduce the risk of dying from prostate cancer, and advances in MRI and other diagnostic tools are helping us identify potentially dangerous cancers while avoiding unnecessary procedures," he said. "Knowing your risk, getting screened regularly, and having these conversations early can save lives."

With the update, the ACS takes a clear position on prostate cancer screening, said Mark Ashamalla, MD, of Episcopal Health Services in New York City.

"For years, the ACS largely focused on encouraging men to discuss the pros and cons of PSA screening with their doctors," said Ashamalla. "Now they're taking a much clearer position and actually recommending regular screening. For men at average risk, that means a PSA blood test every 2 years between ages 50 and 69, while Black men and those with a strong family history should begin annual screening at age 45. This is a significant shift and reflects growing evidence that regular screening can reduce the risk of advanced prostate cancer and save lives."

"The goal isn't to find and treat every prostate cancer," he added. "It's to catch the dangerous ones early enough to make a difference. I think that's the biggest takeaway from these new guidelines."

By way of the guidelines, the ACS also recommends:

The updated guidelines are supportive of the recent trend toward active surveillance as the primary option for men with low-risk prostate cancer.

"It's important to recognize that prostate cancer screening has evolved over the past 30 years," said Smith. "More and more people appreciate that very worrisome harms associated with therapy can be avoided, especially if a man is diagnosed with prostate cancer that is not aggressive. Active treatment should be avoided until such time as it appears that the disease has become aggressive. Some men may be diagnosed with a prostate cancer that's not very aggressive, and they will live with it in that state for the rest of their lives. [They can] avoid harms that are quite diminishing to quality of life."

"On the other hand, if a man's prostate cancer becomes more aggressive, and treatment is necessary, then the process of active surveillance will at least have postponed it for a number of years, allowing men to not have to live with those [treatment-related] side effects," said Smith.

#5722. Is Tesla The Apple Of The EV Industry?

Tesla is an interesting company at an interesting phase of the electric vehicle transition. Well, it has always been an interesting company in this transition. However, lately, as the industry has moved quickly into a new phase, I have been wondering where Tesla will sit amidst it in the coming years.

While there are now hundreds and hundreds of electric vehicle models on the market and EV share of new vehicle sales have risen to 32% globally (or 23% if we only count full electrics/BEVs), Tesla sells only two models but remains the second best selling BEV producer in the world. It remains solidly ahead of other automakers and only trails Chinese giant BYD (or also Geely if we throw in PHEVs).

In the US, where there’s much less EV competition (especially because of Chinese EVs basically being blocked from entering the market), Tesla’s share of EV sales is above 50%.

The Apple iPhone holds about 20–22% of the global smartphone market, and around 58% of the US smartphone market. While that’s a bit higher than Tesla’s shares, it’s quite similar. And considering that Apple just sells that one phone (but in a few different versions) and Tesla just sells the Model 3 and Model Y, it’s hard to not see a comparison.

As EV sales have been picking up in parts of the world, or at least reaching portions of the market in the USA, it seems to me that a lot of people choose Tesla because they’ve heard it’s the EV brand. Tesla has long been the face of the EV transition in most of the world, and even considered the only “real” option. I know of many buyers in the US who just went straight to Tesla without looking at other options. It’s a bit like how many people will only consider and look at Apple for phones. However, how much will that change in the coming years? Will Tesla’s dominance fade further (it’s already down from about 80% of the market in the US to a bit more than 50%) as more and more options come to market, or will it hold it’s special position as a first mover, a tech leader, and an EV-only carmaker?

One more interesting issue here is the matter of “Full Self Driving” — which only comes as “Supervised” at the moment, but Tesla is itching to make a fully hands-off, eyes-off option for Tesla owners. Could Tesla achieve a leading role for years again of having the only fully hands-off, eyes-off, self-driving consumer cars on the road? Or is it not going to beat other automakers to market with a self-driving feature that takes liability for any accidents away from the human operating the car and allows them to sleep, watch movies, work, etc. in the car? This has been hotly debated for about a decade, with Tesla nearly a decade late on Elon Musk’s targets for this, but the company is now operating robotaxis in a few cities and ramping up production of the Cybercab. Risky or not, is Tesla close to taking on liability for humans using FSD in their own cars? And if it does take that step, will that make demand for Tesla cars so high that Tesla sees its EV market share increase significantly again?

I know many people have strong opinions on these matters — on both sides of the debate. Some are certain Tesla is going to rise again and will release true Full Self Driving broadly soon, or at least long before other automakers. Some are certain Tesla’s vision-only approach is fatally flawed and Tesla will never take on full liability for Tesla owners using FSD. I don’t have enough insight or expertise to say one thing is absolutely certain and the other is not, but I do think the FSD matter is quite important to Tesla remaining “the Apple of the EV industry” — even potentially without taking on accident liability. Even with things staying as they are, I wonder if Tesla could remain the dominant EV player in the US and some other markets just because of its early reputation, branding, and 21st century marketing. We shall see.

Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5719. Europe’s Hydrogen Strategy Should Begin With the Industrial Customer

A European fertiliser manufacturer buys ammonia. Its immediate concern is getting the right product, at the right quality and price, with reliable deliveries. The manufacturer may produce ammonia using hydrogen made on its own site, buy hydrogen through a pipeline, or import ammonia made somewhere else. The last choice preserves much of the downstream industrial work but creates no demand for additional hydrogen production or pipelines inside Europe. That distinction belongs at the start of Europe’s hydrogen strategy.

The European Commission has reopened the strategy for consultation, with submissions due November 23. The Commission acknowledges that renewable and low-carbon hydrogen production and use have grown more slowly than expected. Europe’s public investment decisions now need a closer look at the industries expected to use hydrogen, the products they make, and the routes by which those products can be supplied competitively. The consultation is open until November 23, 2026.

I submitted a demand-led assessment to the Commission on November 11. My TFIE Strategy Briefing explains the industrial argument, and the open research report documents the model and its assumptions.

Europe’s existing hydrogen market is primarily a market for industrial chemicals. ACER estimated EU consumption of about 7.4 million tons in 2024, including 4.2 million tons for petroleum refining and 1.9 million tons for ammonia. Passenger cars, building heat and power generation account for very little of that established market. Refining faces structural decline as petroleum demand changes, while fertiliser and chemical producers can keep manufacturing products even when some upstream inputs come from elsewhere.

Steelmaking also has choices that cannot be captured by a single hydrogen-demand target. Producers can use recycled scrap, purchase reduced iron made where electricity is cheaper, or use hydrogen-based direct reduction where its economics work. The equipment, feedstock quality and production location determine the actual hydrogen requirement. Industrial heat offers direct electrical options for many duties. Transport electrification cuts the amount of liquid fuel that must be produced in the first place. These choices determine hydrogen demand long before anyone chooses a pipeline route.

Electricity costs expose why the industrial comparison matters. Electrolysis consuming 55 kWh/kg uses €3.30 of electricity per kilogram at €60/MWh, and €5.50 at €100/MWh. Those prices exclude the electrolyser, finance, maintenance, compression and delivery. An industrial customer pays for the finished product, not for the complexity of the process used to manufacture it.

The new assessment compares qualifying green ammonia imported into Europe at modelled 2050 delivered costs of €755–839/t with European ammonia made using on-site hydrogen at about €1,222/t. The €383–467/t gap puts a substantial price on choosing the domestic route in this model. Emissions certification, quality, reliability and supply concentration belong in the procurement appraisal, alongside the cost and strategic value of retaining European upstream production. They do not turn a forecast of ammonia demand into a forecast of domestic hydrogen demand.

Industrial policy has reasons to support European production beyond the lowest immediate price. Domestic facilities provide employment, technical capabilities and supply security. The right decision is to identify those benefits, calculate the extra cost and state who will pay it. Hydrogen production quotas bury that choice in a volume target and risk making European industrial products less competitive.

The infrastructure consequence follows. A pipeline needs industrial customers who can afford delivered hydrogen. A capacity reservation or a project announcement provides neither a binding purchase agreement nor a business case for long-term utilisation. Germany’s hydrogen-network reservations and the Netherlands’ lower demand projections already make that difference worth examining. Europe should assess its planned networks against the industrial customers likely to remain after process and sourcing choices change.

European network planners already run scenarios covering industrial activity, technology and hydrogen supply. My November submission proposes adding direct competition between ways of supplying equivalent industrial products and transport services, including the location of production. The resulting hydrogen requirements then become inputs to infrastructure appraisal instead of predetermined outputs of hydrogen policy.

The next step is practical. Keep a proposed pipeline’s design, cost, capacity and planned opening date unchanged. Assess that same investment first under a policy-aligned case that includes existing hydrogen mandates, and then under a separate economic-competition case in which industrial buyers can select other emissions-qualified production routes. Publish projected utilisation, binding customers, delivered costs, tariffs and public exposure. After seeing the downside in the original proposal, compare smaller pipes, staged construction, local hydrogen supply or imported industrial inputs. Redesigning the pipeline at the outset of every scenario hides the risk of the investment actually under consideration.

The accompanying research uses a reproducible Python model, an evidence workbook and an interactive dashboard. Readers can change assumptions about industrial imports, technology and production costs and see how the modelled hydrogen requirement responds. In the Strong pathway with central costs, selected European industrial markets and transport services require 1.887 million tons of physical hydrogen in 2050. Halving the assumed import capacity for industrial products raises that result to 3.206 million tons. These figures describe selected markets and exclude hydrogen embodied in imported finished products; they are not a forecast of total EU demand.

A competitive industrial economy can still need hydrogen for chemicals and some other applications. It needs production and pipelines sized for customers, with any extra capacity supported by a stated security or industrial rationale. The Commission’s renewed strategy should begin with Europe’s industrial products and services, then calculate the hydrogen system worth paying for.

Read the original TFIE Strategy Briefing, the open European hydrogen assessment, and explore the interactive dashboard, evidence workbook and reproducible Python model.

#5717. Florida Sinks SS United States In Gulf Of Mexico To Protect The Environment, And I'm Sorry, But That's Just Too Many Jokes

In a hilariously ironic turn of events, Okaloosa County, Florida, sank the SS United States to the bottom of the Gulf of Mexico on Veteran's Day, turning it into the world's largest artificial reef to provide shelter for local marine life in the wake of human-caused coral reef decimation in the area. The SS United States is longer than the Titanic, measuring 990 feet from bow to stern and 17 stories in height. It was once the fastest ocean liner in the world, known for carrying royalty and celebrities between the U.S. and Europe from its maiden voyage in 1952 to its retirement in 1969.

It actually still holds the record for the world's fastest trans-Atlantic ocean liner, accomplishing the trek in 3 days, 10 hours, and 40 minutes at an average speed of 35.59 knots, and it achieved top speeds of over 38.3 knots, or 44 mph, during trials. This serves as a reminder that even once-great nations — er, ships — can find themselves fallen so far from their former glory that they are better off being totally gutted and sank to the bottom of the ocean rather than wasting time and resources attempting to resuscitate them only for them to still be outdated and irrelevant in a modern world.

The SS United States was originally designed to be converted from luxury ocean liner to troop transport vessel in the event of war like the also-retired Queen Mary was, with the ability to carry 14,000 troops 10,000 miles without refueling, though the SS United States never actually faced wartime use. It was powered by four Westinghouse 1,000-psi steam turbines and eight boilers, which combined to produce a mind-boggling total power output of 240,000 horsepower.

Following the ship's retirement in 1969, the SS United States, "spent its years haunting old shipyards while failed plans to restore it came and went" according to Northeastern Global News. Okaloosa County bought the vessel in 2024 after it faced a court-ordered eviction from its prior docking point at Pier 82 along the Delaware River in South Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, where it stayed for nearly three decades.

Okaloosa County chose to sink the ship in its waters to attract tourism from divers, as its top funnels should be easily accessible at just 55 feet below the surface, while its keel will be at a depth of 180 feet. Prior to this, the largest artificial reef on the planet was the 910-foot-long USS Oriskany, which was a U.S. warship that was intentionally sunk in 2006. The SS United States Conservancy plans to commemorate the remarkable ship by constructing a museum and visitor's center in Florida.
